The Noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines (Āryāṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā)

This text is the Āryāṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā, known in Tibetan as the Phags pa shes rab kyi pha rol tu phyin pa brgyad stong pa, or in English The Noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, one of the most celebrated and historically significant scriptures of the Mahāyāna Prajñāpāramitā literature. Catalogued as Toh 12 in the Kangyur, it belongs to the Prajñāpāramitā section of sūtras attributed to the Buddha's discourses on the perfection of wisdom, emphasizing the nature of emptiness, non-attachment, and the bodhisattva path. The opening scene situates the teaching at Vulture Peak Mountain near Rājagṛha, where the Buddha addresses a vast assembly of realized arhats and bodhisattvas, establishing the setting for an extended and profound exposition on wisdom that unfolds across many chapters. As one of the foundational and earliest expressions of the Prajñāpāramitā tradition, this sūtra had immense influence on the development of Mahāyāna philosophy and practice throughout India and Tibet.

In the language of India: Āryāṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā. In the language of Tibet: The Noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The first section.

Homage to all buddhas, bodhisattvas, noble śrāvakas, and pratyekabuddhas.

Thus did I hear. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling at Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak Mountain, together with a great Saṅgha of twelve hundred and fifty monks. All of them were arhats: their defilements exhausted, without mental affliction, self-mastered, supremely and utterly liberated in mind, supremely and utterly liberated in wisdom, thoroughbreds, great elephants, who had done what was to be done, accomplished what was to be accomplished, laid down the burden, attained their own highest aim, entirely exhausted the bonds of existence, and who, through complete and perfect knowledge, had attained supreme and utter liberation of mind, having reached the far shore of the highest mastery over all minds. There was but one exception among these individuals, and that was the Venerable Ānanda.

Then the Blessed One spoke to the Elder, the Venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, beginning from the Perfection of Wisdom of the great bodhisattvas, speak with confidence in the very way that great bodhisattvas would come to enter into the Perfection of Wisdom."

Then the Venerable Śāriputra thought to himself: "Will the Venerable Subhūti expound the Perfection of Wisdom to the great bodhisattvas by generating the power of his own personal confidence in wisdom, and by virtue of the power of his own personal confidence in wisdom being blessed? Or will it be by the power of the Buddha?"

Then, by the power of the Buddha, the Venerable Subhūti perceived the thought of the Venerable Śāriputra's mind thinking in just such a way, and spoke to the Venerable Śāriputra as follows: "Venerable Śāriputra, whatever the hearers of the Blessed One speak, teach, explain, express, clarify, and make perfectly clear — all of that is to be understood as the power of the Tathāgata. Why is that? Because, when they train in the Dharma that the Tathāgata has taught, they directly realize and hold to the nature of reality (dharmatā). And whatever they, having directly realized and held to the nature of reality, then expound, teach, explain, express, clarify, and make perfectly clear in any and all ways — all of that is in accord with the nature of reality. Venerable Śāriputra, the fact that those sons of good family who thus expound the nature of reality are in accord with the nature of reality is itself concordant with the teaching of Dharma by the Tathāgata."

Then, by the power of the Buddha, the Venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One as follows: "Blessed One, you have said: 'Subhūti, beginning from the Perfection of Wisdom of the great bodhisattvas, speak with confidence in the very way that great bodhisattvas would come to enter into the Perfection of Wisdom.' Blessed One, as for what is called 'bodhisattva' — this very word 'bodhisattva': of which phenomenon is it the name? Blessed One, I do not see the phenomenon designated by the name 'bodhisattva' as truly existing anywhere at all."

"Blessed One, likewise the phenomenon called 'the Perfection of Wisdom' — this too I do not find anywhere as truly existing. Blessed One, neither the bodhisattva nor the phenomena of a bodhisattva do I seek out, apprehend, or find as truly existing; and the Perfection of Wisdom likewise I do not seek out, apprehend, or find as truly existing. If this is so, then which bodhisattva shall I instruct and guide in which Perfection of Wisdom? Blessed One, yet if, even when it is spoken of, taught, and explained in this way, a bodhisattva, a great being, does not lose heart, does not shrink back entirely, does not feel shame or embarrassment, does not become dismayed, does not let his mind turn away and waver, does not become broken and turn back, is not frightened, does not become altogether terrified, does not fall into a state of utter dread — then this itself is what I shall teach as the Perfection of Wisdom for the bodhisattva, the great being. This itself I shall recognize as the Perfection of Wisdom of that bodhisattva, that great being. This itself is the instruction in the Perfection of Wisdom. If one abides in just that way, then that itself is the instruction and guidance in it.

"Blessed One, furthermore, when a bodhisattva, a great being, is engaging in the Perfection of Wisdom and cultivating the Perfection of Wisdom, he should train in such a way that he does not make even the awakening mind (bodhicitta) itself into an object of conceit. Why? Because this mind is no-mind: the intrinsic nature of mind is luminosity (prabhāsvara)."

Then the Venerable Śāriputra said to the Venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, does that mind which is no-mind actually exist?"

When this was said, the Venerable Subhūti replied to the Venerable Śāriputra: "Venerable Śāriputra, can the very fact of being no-mind — that no-mind-ness itself — be apprehended as existing or not existing, or be cognized at all?"

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, it cannot."

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, if in that very no-mind-ness there is nothing that exists or does not exist, and nothing to be apprehended — then, Venerable Śāriputra, is the question you asked, 'Does that mind which is no-mind exist?' — is that question at all proper?"

When this was said, the Venerable Śāriputra said to the Venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, then what is this no-mind-ness itself?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, that no-mind is unchanging and free from conceptual thought."

Then the Venerable Śāriputra gave his approval to the Venerable Subhūti, saying: "Excellent! Venerable Subhūti, you have taught well — just as the Blessed One has declared you foremost among those who abide in freedom from affliction. Excellent, excellent! Therefore, one should also examine whether a bodhisattva, a great being, is irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. And one should also recognize that a bodhisattva, a great being, is inseparable from the Perfection of Wisdom. Even those who wish to train in the stage of the śrāvakas should listen to this very Perfection of Wisdom, take it up, retain it, read it, thoroughly master it, and proclaim it widely. They should train in this very Perfection of Wisdom and take it as their practice."

Those who wish to train in the stage of the pratyekabuddha should likewise listen to this very Perfection of Wisdom, take it up, retain it, read it, thoroughly master it, and proclaim it widely. They should train in this very Perfection of Wisdom and take it as their practice. Those who wish to train in the stage of the bodhisattva should likewise listen to this very Perfection of Wisdom, take it up, retain it, read it, thoroughly master it, and proclaim it widely. They should take this very Perfection of Wisdom as their practice, endowed with skillful means, in order to attain all the qualities of a bodhisattva. Why is that? Because it is from this very Perfection of Wisdom that all the qualities of a bodhisattva in which a bodhisattva, a great being, should train and take as practice are taught in full. Those who wish to train in unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening should likewise listen to this very Perfection of Wisdom, take it up, retain it, read it, thoroughly master it, and proclaim it widely. They should take this very Perfection of Wisdom as their practice, endowed with skillful means, in order to attain all the qualities of a buddha. Why is that? Because it is from this very Perfection of Wisdom that all the qualities of a buddha in which a bodhisattva, a great being, should train and take as practice are taught in full.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, I have not sought, not apprehended, and not rightly perceived this very name 'bodhisattva.' Nor have I sought, not apprehended, and not rightly perceived the Perfection of Wisdom. Blessed One, since I have not sought, not apprehended, and not rightly perceived this very name 'bodhisattva,' and have not sought, not apprehended, and not rightly perceived the Perfection of Wisdom either — to which bodhisattva, and in which Perfection of Wisdom, should I give instruction and guidance? Blessed One, when I have not sought any thing, not apprehended it, and not rightly perceived it, to gather and arrange under the mere label 'bodhisattva' — this, it seems to me, would be a presumption on my part. Blessed One, yet even that name neither abides nor does not abide; it is neither separated from abiding nor not separated from abiding. Why so? Because that name is not found to exist. In just that way, the name neither abides nor does not abide, and is neither separated from abiding nor not separated from abiding.

Blessed One, if, when the profound Perfection of Wisdom is being explained, taught, and expounded in this way, the mind of a bodhisattva, a great being, does not sink, does not shrink entirely, does not feel ashamed, does not become dejected, does not turn away in thought, does not collapse and turn back, is not timid, is not altogether frightened, and does not become thoroughly fearful — then one should know that such a bodhisattva, a great being, who is devoted with a superior aspiration, is inseparable from the Perfection of Wisdom. They abide on the stage of an irreversible bodhisattva — abiding well, in the manner of non-abiding.

Blessed One, furthermore, a bodhisattva, a great being, who practices the Perfection of Wisdom and cultivates the Perfection of Wisdom does not take up abiding in form. Likewise, they do not abide in feeling, nor in perception, nor in formations, nor in consciousness. Why is that?"

If one abides in form, one moves in the fabrication of form — and that is not practicing the Perfection of Wisdom. Likewise, if one abides in feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness, one moves in the fabrication of consciousness — and that is not practicing the Perfection of Wisdom. Why is that? Because one who moves in fabrication neither fully grasps the Perfection of Wisdom, nor practices yoga in the Perfection of Wisdom, nor brings the Perfection of Wisdom to complete fulfillment. And if the Perfection of Wisdom is not brought to complete fulfillment, then through grasping at what is not to be grasped, one will not come to emerge definitively into omniscience.

Why is that? Because in the Perfection of Wisdom there is no grasping at form. Likewise, in the Perfection of Wisdom there is no grasping at feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness. That which is the non-grasping of form — that is not form. Likewise, that which is feeling, that which is perception, those which are formations, and that which is the non-grasping of consciousness — that is not consciousness. And this Perfection of Wisdom itself is likewise without grasping.

It is in just this way that a bodhisattva, a great being, should practice this Perfection of Wisdom. This, moreover, is called the meditative absorption of the bodhisattva, the great being, known as "the non-grasping at all phenomena" — one that is vast, limitless in its certainty and scope, and not shared in common with all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

Omniscience itself is also without grasping and is not to be held as a defining mark. Were it to be held as a mark, Kuntu Gyupa Threngwacan would not have faith in it. Yet Kuntu Gyupa Threngwacan, having generated devoted interest in this wisdom of omniscience, follows it with faith, enters it with one-pointed wisdom, and upon entering, does not grasp at form. Likewise, one does not grasp at feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness.

That knowing awareness is not found to follow delight and pleasure. That knowing awareness is not found to follow interior form. That knowing awareness is not found to follow exterior form. That knowing awareness is not found to follow interior-and-exterior form. Nor is that knowing awareness found to follow anything other than form. Likewise, that knowing awareness does not follow the interior feeling, perception, and formations. That knowing awareness is not found to follow interior consciousness. That knowing awareness is not found to follow exterior consciousness. That knowing awareness is not found to follow interior-and-exterior consciousness. Nor is that knowing awareness found to follow anything other than consciousness. It is in this enumeration of expressions that Kuntu Gyupa Threngwacan generates devoted interest.

He follows this wisdom of omniscience with complete faith in every respect, and taking the nature of reality as his measure, he is said to be thus devoted. He does not grasp at any phenomenon whatsoever. He perceives no phenomenon that he would grasp or release. And he does not conceive of himself as having attained nirvāṇa.

Blessed One, this too should be understood as the Perfection of Wisdom of a bodhisattva, a great being: one who does not fully grasp at form, and likewise does not fully grasp at feeling, perception, and formations, and does not fully grasp at consciousness; and who, without abandoning midway the ten powers of the Tathāgata, the four fearlessnesses of the Tathāgata, and the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha, does not pass into nirvāṇa before they are fully perfected. Therefore, Blessed One, this too should be understood as the Perfection of Wisdom of a bodhisattva, a great being.

Blessed One, furthermore, a bodhisattva, a great being, who is engaged in the Perfection of Wisdom and who cultivates the Perfection of Wisdom should closely examine and carefully reflect in this way: "What is this Perfection of Wisdom? Whose is this Perfection of Wisdom? Is that which is without any phenomenon and without any referential object the Perfection of Wisdom?" If one examines closely and reflects carefully in this way, and yet does not grow despondent, does not lose heart, feels no embarrassment, does not become dismayed, does not let one's mind turn away, does not become broken and turn away, does not tremble, does not become frightened, does not fall into continual fear — then it should be understood that such a bodhisattva, a great being, has not parted from the Perfection of Wisdom.

Then the venerable Śāriputra said this to the venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, when form itself is devoid of the essential nature of form, and likewise when feeling itself and perception itself and formations themselves are devoid of their essential nature, and likewise when consciousness itself is also devoid of the essential nature of consciousness, when the Perfection of Wisdom itself is also devoid of the essential nature of the Perfection of Wisdom, and when omniscience itself is also devoid of the essential nature of omniscience — for what reason should it be understood that the bodhisattva, the great being, has not parted from the Perfection of Wisdom?"

When he had spoken thus, the venerable Subhūti replied to the venerable Śāriputra: "Venerable Śāriputra, just so, just so. Śāriputra, form itself is devoid of the essential nature of form. Likewise, feeling itself and perception itself and formations themselves are devoid of their essential nature, and likewise, venerable Śāriputra, consciousness itself is also devoid of the essential nature of consciousness. Venerable Śāriputra, the Perfection of Wisdom itself is also devoid of the essential nature of the Perfection of Wisdom. Venerable Śāriputra, omniscience itself is also devoid of the essential nature of omniscience. The Perfection of Wisdom is moreover devoid of the defining mark of the Perfection of Wisdom. The defining mark is moreover devoid of the essential nature of a defining mark. The basis of the defining mark is moreover devoid of the essential nature of the basis of a defining mark. Essential nature is moreover devoid of the defining mark of essential nature."

When he had spoken thus, the venerable Śāriputra said this to the venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, will every bodhisattva, every great being, who trains in this surely arrive at omniscience?"

The venerable Subhūti replied: "Venerable Śāriputra, just so, just so. Every bodhisattva, every great being, who trains in this will surely arrive at omniscience. And why is that?"

"Venerable Śāriputra, all phenomena are without definitive arising and are unborn. Venerable Śāriputra, the bodhisattva, the great being, who practices in that way will draw near to omniscience. And to whatever degree they draw near to omniscience, to that very degree, for the sake of bringing sentient beings to full maturation, they will also draw near to purity of body and mind, purity of the major marks, purity of the buddha-field, and proximity to the buddhas. Venerable Śāriputra, a bodhisattva, a great being, who practices the Perfection of Wisdom in that way is drawing near to omniscience."

Then the venerable Subhūti, turning to the bodhisattvas, the great beings, said this: "If one practices with regard to form, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the sign of form, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'form is a sign,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the arising of form, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the cessation of form, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the disintegration of form, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'form is empty,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'I am practicing,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'I am a bodhisattva,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one thinks 'I am a bodhisattva,' one is practicing with regard to an observed object itself.

"Likewise, if one practices with regard to feeling, perception, and formations, or if one practices with regard to consciousness, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the sign of consciousness, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'consciousness is a sign,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the arising of consciousness, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the cessation of consciousness, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with regard to the disintegration of consciousness, one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'consciousness is empty,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'I am practicing,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one practices with the thought 'I am a bodhisattva,' one is practicing with regard to a sign. If one thinks 'I am a bodhisattva,' one is practicing with regard to an observed object itself.

"Furthermore, if someone thinks 'whoever practices in that way is practicing the Perfection of Wisdom — that one is cultivating the Perfection of Wisdom' — if they think thus, they are practicing with regard to signs, and this bodhisattva should be understood as one who is unskillful in means."

Then the venerable Śāriputra said this to the venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, how then should a bodhisattva, a great being, practice in order to be practicing the Perfection of Wisdom?"

When he had spoken thus, the venerable Subhūti said this to the venerable Śāriputra: "Venerable Śāriputra, if a bodhisattva, a great being, does not practice with regard to form, does not practice with regard to the sign of form, does not practice with the thought 'form is a sign,' does not practice with regard to the arising of form, does not practice with regard to the cessation of form, does not practice with regard to the disintegration of form, does not practice with the thought 'form is empty,' does not practice with the thought 'I am practicing,' does not practice with the thought 'I am a bodhisattva,' does not practice with regard to the observed object of the thought 'I am a bodhisattva' — and likewise does not practice with regard to feeling, does not practice with regard to perception, does not practice with regard to formations, does not practice with regard to consciousness, does not practice with regard to the sign of consciousness, does not practice with the thought 'consciousness is a sign,' does not practice with regard to the arising of consciousness —

does not practice with regard to the cessation of consciousness, does not practice with regard to the destruction of consciousness, does not practice with the thought 'consciousness is empty,' does not practice with the thought 'I am practicing,' does not practice with the thought 'I am a bodhisattva,' and does not practice with regard to the observed object of the thought 'I am a bodhisattva.'

And if someone were to think, 'One who practices in that very way is practicing the Perfection of Wisdom, and is cultivating the Perfection of Wisdom' — one does not think even that. The bodhisattva, the great being, who is practicing right now in this way, is indeed practicing the Perfection of Wisdom. Yet even while practicing, one does not conceive 'I am practicing.' One does not conceive 'I am not practicing.' One does not conceive 'I am both practicing and not practicing.' One does not conceive 'I am neither practicing nor not practicing.' One does not conceive 'There is something to be practiced.' One does not conceive 'There is nothing to be practiced.' One does not conceive 'There is something to be practiced and also something not to be practiced.' One does not conceive 'There is neither something to be practiced nor something not to be practiced.' Why is that? Because all phenomena are not conceived and not grasped.

This is the meditative absorption of the bodhisattva, the great being, called 'the non-appropriation of all phenomena' — vast, open, definite, and not shared in common with any śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha. The bodhisattva, the great being, who abides in this meditative absorption will swiftly awaken to the unsurpassable, perfect, and complete awakening.

Thereupon, the venerable Subhūti, sustained by the power of the Buddha, spoke these words: 'Blessed One, the bodhisattva, the great being, who abides in this meditative absorption — has that one not been prophesied to unsurpassable, perfect, and complete awakening by the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas of the past? That one does not rightly perceive even that meditative absorption; that one does not conceive pride on account of the meditative absorption. I am in meditative equipoise; I am entering into meditative absorption; I am established in meditative absorption; I have entered into meditative absorption — no such thoughts arise in any way, from any direction, in any aspect whatsoever.'

When he had said this, the venerable Śāriputra spoke to the venerable Subhūti: 'Venerable Subhūti, can you point out that meditative absorption — the meditative absorption abiding in which a bodhisattva, a great being, is prophesied to unsurpassable, perfect, and complete awakening by the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas?'

Subhūti replied: 'Venerable Śāriputra, I cannot. Why is that? Because that son of good family does not know that meditative absorption, does not know it rightly.'

The venerable Śāriputra said: 'Venerable Subhūti, do you say that one does not know it, does not know it rightly?'

The venerable Subhūti said: 'Venerable Śāriputra, yes, I do say that one does not know it, does not know it rightly. Why is that? Because that meditative absorption is not to be found — therefore one does not know it, does not know it rightly. Indeed, even that meditative absorption itself, one does not know, does not know rightly.'

Then the Blessed One said to the venerable Subhūti: 'Excellent!' — and declared: 'Just as you have taught this, Subhūti, empowered by the Tathāgata's might and blessed by the Tathāgata's blessing — excellent, Subhūti, excellent! Subhūti, it is just so, it is exactly so.

The bodhisattva, the great being, must train in exactly this way. Why is that? Because when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, he trains in the Perfection of Wisdom.

Then the venerable Śāriputra said to the Blessed One: 'Blessed One, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, is he indeed training in the Perfection of Wisdom?'

When he had said this, the Blessed One declared to the venerable Śāriputra: 'Śāriputra, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, he is indeed training in the Perfection of Wisdom.'

When the Blessed One had said this, the venerable Śāriputra said to him: 'Blessed One, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, in what phenomenon is he training?'

When he had said this, the Blessed One declared to the venerable Śāriputra: 'Śāriputra, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, he is not training in any phenomenon whatsoever. Why is that? Because those phenomena do not exist in the way that ordinary childlike persons without learning cling to them. The venerable Śāriputra asked: 'Blessed One, then in what way do they exist?' The Blessed One declared: 'Śāriputra, they exist truly in the way that they do not exist, and because they are absent in that way, this is called ignorance. To those phenomena, ordinary childlike persons without learning become attached, and they conceptualize all phenomena as nonexistent. Having so conceptualized them, clinging to the two extremes, they do not know and do not see those phenomena. Therefore, even as those phenomena are absent, they conceptualize all phenomena. Having conceptualized them, they become attached to the two extremes. Abiding in that attachment with its basis in reference points, they conceptualize past phenomena, they conceptualize future phenomena, they conceptualize present phenomena. Having so conceptualized, they become fixated on name and form. They conceptualize all phenomena as nonexistent. And having conceptualized all those phenomena as nonexistent, they do not know and do not see the true path as it really is. Not knowing and not seeing the true path as it really is, they do not emerge with certainty from the three realms and do not enter into the true end of reality — and for that reason they are counted among those called childlike. They do not place their trust in the true Dharma. Śāriputra, great bodhisattvas, great beings, do not become attached to any phenomenon whatsoever.'

When the Blessed One had said this, the venerable Śāriputra said to him: 'Blessed One, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, is he training in omniscience (sarvākārajñatā)?'

The Blessed One declared: 'Śāriputra, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, he trains in omniscience. Śāriputra, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, he trains in all phenomena. Śāriputra, when a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in this way, he will come to train in omniscience, he will draw near to omniscience, and he will with certainty emerge into omniscience.'

Then the venerable Subhūti said to the Blessed One: 'Blessed One, if someone were to ask this: "Will this illusory person train in omniscience? Will he draw near to omniscience? Will he with certainty emerge into omniscience?" — if someone were to ask in those words, Blessed One, how should I explain this to them?'

The Blessed One then spoke these words to the venerable Subhūti: 'Therefore, Subhūti, I turn the question back to you. Explain it as best you can.'

'Very well, Blessed One,' said Subhūti, and he listened attentively to the Blessed One.

Then the Blessed One declared: 'Subhūti, what do you think — is the illusion one thing and its form another? Is the illusion one thing and its feeling another, its perception another, its formations another?'

And again: 'Subhūti, what do you think — is the illusion one thing and its consciousness another?'

Subhūti replied: 'That is not so, Blessed One. The illusion is not one thing and form another. Blessed One, form itself is illusion, and illusion itself is form. Likewise, Blessed One, the illusion is not one thing and feeling another, perception another, formations another. Blessed One, feeling, perception, and formations themselves are illusion, and illusion itself is feeling, perception, and formations. Blessed One, the illusion is not one thing and consciousness another. Blessed One, consciousness itself is illusion, and illusion itself is consciousness.'

The Blessed One then declared: 'Subhūti, what do you think — do these five appropriated aggregates (skandhas) carry the perception, the full recognition, the discernment, and the designation "bodhisattva"?'

When this was said, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One thus: 'It is so, Blessed One; it is just so, O Sugata. Blessed One, a bodhisattva, a great being, training in the Perfection of Wisdom, trains in unsurpassed, complete, perfect awakening just as an illusory person would. Why is that? Blessed One, those very five appropriated aggregates are to be held as the illusory person. Why is that? Because, Blessed One, as the Blessed One has declared, form is like an illusion — and form, whatever it may be, is precisely those five aggregates together with the six sense faculties. Blessed One, as the Blessed One has declared, feeling, perception, and formations are like an illusion; and as the Blessed One has declared, consciousness too is like an illusion — and consciousness, whatever it may be, is precisely those five aggregates together with the six sense faculties.

Blessed One, when newly entered bodhisattvas, great beings, hear this teaching, will they not be overcome with fright, utter terror, and dread?'

The Blessed One declared: 'Subhūti, if newly entered bodhisattvas, great beings, have come under the sway of an evil companion, then they will indeed be overcome with fright, utter terror, and dread. But, Subhūti, if newly entered bodhisattvas, great beings, have come under the guidance of a spiritual friend, they will not be frightened, not utterly terrified, and will not be overcome with dread.'

When this was said, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One thus: 'Blessed One, how shall we recognize who the spiritual friends of bodhisattvas, great beings, are?'

The Blessed One declared: 'They are those who teach and guide such bodhisattvas in the perfections; and they are those who point out to such bodhisattvas the deeds of Māra —'

and that the faults of Māra are to be recognized as such, that these are the faults of Māra, and that these are the deeds of Māra — one should recognize these as such, and having recognized these deeds of Māra, you should abandon them. Subhūti, these are to be known as the spiritual friends of bodhisattvas, great beings — those who have donned the great armor, who have rightly entered the Great Vehicle, and who rightly abide in the Great Vehicle.'

When this was said, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One thus: 'Blessed One, you have declared: "Subhūti, these are to be known as the spiritual friends of bodhisattvas, great beings — those who have donned the great armor, who have rightly entered the Great Vehicle, and who rightly abide in the Great Vehicle." Blessed One, when you also declared "bodhisattva, great being," what is the underlying meaning of the word "bodhisattva"?'

When this was asked, the Blessed One addressed the venerable Subhūti thus: 'Subhūti, the underlying meaning of "bodhisattva" is that there is no underlying meaning. Why is that? Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, trains in non-attachment to all phenomena. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, in being non-attached for the sake of fully comprehending all phenomena, fully and perfectly awakens to unsurpassed, complete, and perfect awakening. Subhūti, because one strives for awakening, one is called a bodhisattva, a great being.'

The venerable Subhūti asked: 'Blessed One, you have declared "bodhisattva, great being." Blessed One, for what reason is one called a bodhisattva, a great being?'

The Blessed One declared: 'It is because one aspires to be the foremost among the great multitude of sentient beings, the supreme of the great assemblies of sentient beings. For that reason, one is called a bodhisattva, a great being.'

Then the venerable Śāriputra addressed the Blessed One thus: 'Blessed One, I too venture to offer a reason why one is called a bodhisattva, a great being.'

The Blessed One declared: 'Śāriputra, if you know that the time is right, venture to speak.'

The venerable Śāriputra said: 'It is because the Dharma is taught for the purpose of abandoning such views as the view of a self, the view of a sentient being, the view of a living soul, the view of a person, the view of arising, the view of perishing, the view of annihilation, the view of permanence, and this great view pertaining to one's own body, and other such views. For that reason, one is called a bodhisattva, a great being.'

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One thus: 'Blessed One, I too venture to offer a reason why one is called a bodhisattva, a great being.'

The Blessed One declared: 'Subhūti, if you know that the time is right, venture to speak.'

The venerable Subhūti said: 'Blessed One, one is called a bodhisattva, a great being because that awakening mind — the mind of omniscience, the undefiled mind, the unequalled mind, the mind that is equal to the unequalled, the mind not shared in common with any śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha — toward that very mind one is not attached and does not cling to it. Why is that? It is because that very mind of omniscience is itself undefiled and unclung-to.'

That very mind of omniscience — the undefiled mind, unclung-to — is itself not attached to and not clung to, and it is for that reason that one is counted among those called a bodhisattva, a great being.

Then the venerable Śāriputra said this to the venerable Subhūti: 'Venerable Subhūti, for what reason is there no attachment to and no clinging to that mind?'

Subhūti replied: 'Venerable Śāriputra, because mind is without mind, there is no attachment to and no clinging to that mind.'

Śāriputra said: 'Venerable Subhūti, but then, does that mind — the mind that is without mind — actually exist?'

Subhūti replied: 'Venerable Śāriputra, could that which is without mind be apprehended as existing or non-existing?'

Śāriputra said: 'Venerable Subhūti, it could not.'

Subhūti replied: 'Venerable Śāriputra, if that which is without mind has in it neither existence nor non-existence, and is not apprehended at all, then how could one speak of a mind that is without mind as existing?'

Śāriputra said: 'Venerable Subhūti, you teach just as the Blessed One has declared: you are the foremost among those who abide without affliction. Excellent!'

Then the venerable Maitreya, son of Maitrā, addressed the Blessed One in these words: 'Blessed One, as for what is called a great being, a great being — that bodhisattva has donned the great armor, and that bodhisattva has entered the Great Vehicle rightly and dwells in the Great Vehicle rightly. It is for that reason that one is counted among those called a great being, a great being.'

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One in these words: 'Blessed One, when one speaks of "donning the great armor, donning the great armor," to what extent, Blessed One, has a bodhisattva, a great being, donned the great armor?'

The Blessed One declared: 'Subhūti, here a bodhisattva, a great being, thinks: "I shall lead immeasurable, innumerable sentient beings to complete nirvāṇa. I shall lead countless sentient beings to complete nirvāṇa." And yet those very sentient beings who are thus led to complete nirvāṇa — even those do not exist. Nevertheless, that bodhisattva leads all those vast numbers of sentient beings to complete nirvāṇa, while neither those who are led to complete nirvāṇa nor the one who leads them to complete nirvāṇa exist as anything whatsoever. Why is that? Subhūti, it is because the nature of reality of an illusion applies here, for this is the nature of reality of all phenomena. Subhūti, consider, for example, a skilled illusionist, or the apprentice of an illusionist, who at the great crossroads of a broad highway conjures up a great multitude of people, conjures them up and then makes that great multitude disappear. Subhūti, what do you think? Was there anyone who killed or died or was destroyed or was made to disappear?'

Subhūti replied: 'Blessed One, there was not.'

The Blessed One declared: 'Subhūti, in just the same way, a bodhisattva, a great being, leads immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings to complete nirvāṇa, yet there is no one whatsoever who is led to complete nirvāṇa and no sentient being who is led to complete nirvāṇa by anyone.'

'If, upon hearing a teaching such as this, a bodhisattva, a great being, is not terrified, not utterly frightened, does not fall into utter fear — by this alone, Subhūti, that bodhisattva, that great being, is to be understood as one who has donned the great armor.'

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: 'Blessed One, how wonderful! Blessed One, according to the meaning of what the Blessed One has declared, as I understand it, this bodhisattva, this great being, is to be understood as one who has donned armor that is without armor.'

The Blessed One declared: 'Subhūti, it is exactly so. This bodhisattva, this great being, is to be understood as one who has donned armor that is without armor. Why is that? Subhūti, omniscience itself is uncreated, untransformed, and unconstructed. And the sentient beings for whose sake this armor has been donned — those sentient beings too are uncreated, untransformed, and unconstructed.'

When this was declared, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: 'Blessed One, it is just so. Sugata, it is just so. Why is that? Blessed One, form is neither bound nor liberated. Likewise, feeling, perception, and formations — and, Blessed One, consciousness is neither bound nor liberated. Blessed One, the suchness of form too is neither bound nor liberated. Likewise, the suchness of feeling, the suchness of perception, the suchness of formations — and, Blessed One, the suchness of consciousness too is neither bound nor liberated.'

Then the venerable Gaṅgaputra Maitrāyaṇīputra said to the venerable Subhūti: 'Venerable Subhūti, do you say that form is neither bound nor liberated? Likewise, do you say that feeling, perception, and formations — and, venerable Subhūti, that consciousness is neither bound nor liberated? Do you say, venerable Subhūti, that the suchness of form too is neither bound nor liberated? Likewise, do you say that the suchness of feeling, the suchness of perception, the suchness of formations — and, venerable Subhūti, that the suchness of consciousness too is neither bound nor liberated?

Venerable Subhūti, then what is that form which you call neither bound nor liberated? What is that feeling, that perception, those formations — and, venerable Subhūti, what is that consciousness which you call neither bound nor liberated? Venerable Subhūti, what is that suchness of form which you call neither bound nor liberated? Likewise, what is that suchness of feeling, that suchness of perception, that suchness of formations? And, venerable Subhūti, what is that suchness of consciousness which you call neither bound nor liberated?'

When this was said, the venerable Subhūti replied to the venerable Gaṅgaputra Maitrāyaṇīputra with these words: 'Venerable Gaṅgaputra, the form of an illusory person — whatever that is — is neither bound nor liberated. Likewise, the feeling of an illusory person — whatever that is — the perception of an illusory person, the formations of an illusory person; and, venerable Gaṅgaputra, the consciousness of an illusory person — whatever that is — is neither bound nor liberated. Venerable Gaṅgaputra, the suchness of the form of an illusory person — whatever that is — is neither bound nor liberated.

Likewise, the suchness of the feeling of an illusory person, the suchness of the perception of an illusory person, the suchness of the formations of an illusory person — whatever those are — and, venerable Gaṅgaputra, the suchness of the consciousness of an illusory person — whatever that is — is neither bound nor liberated. And why is that? Because it is not real, it is neither bound nor liberated. Because it is solitary and apart, it is neither bound nor liberated. Because it is unborn, it is neither bound nor liberated. These, then, constitute the great armor of the bodhisattva, the great being — an armor without armor, of one who has rightly entered the Great Vehicle and rightly abides in the Great Vehicle.

When this was said, the venerable Gaṅgaputra Maitrāyaṇīputra was silent and said nothing.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: Blessed One, what then is this Great Vehicle — the Great Vehicle in which the bodhisattva, the great being, has donned the great armor, rightly entered, and rightly abides? How is one to understand that one has rightly entered the Great Vehicle? From where does one emerge through the Great Vehicle? By means of what has one rightly entered the Great Vehicle? Where does one come to abide through the Great Vehicle? And from what does one emerge through the Great Vehicle?

When this was asked, the Blessed One replied to the venerable Subhūti with these words: Subhūti, this called 'the Great Vehicle' — that is an expression whose meaning is immeasurable. Subhūti, the reason it is called immeasurable is that it is without measure. Subhūti, as for one who asks: how is one to understand that one has rightly entered the Great Vehicle, from where does one emerge through it, by means of what has one rightly entered it, where does one come to abide through it, and from what does one emerge through it — the answer is this: one rightly enters through the perfections, one emerges from the three realms, and one abides in omniscience by entering through non-apprehension. The bodhisattva, the great being, thus emerges — and yet there is no place from which one emerges, no means by which one has rightly entered, no place where one comes to abide; yet by way of non-abiding, one comes to abide in omniscience. And through the Great Vehicle one has not drawn anything out, will not emerge from anything, and there is no emergence — for the phenomenon that emerges and the phenomenon by means of which it emerges are both absent and unapprehended. This being so, when all phenomena are absent and unapprehended, what phenomenon could emerge from what phenomenon?

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: Blessed One, what is referred to as 'the Great Vehicle, the Great Vehicle' is that from which one emerges having overwhelmed the world together with its gods, humans, and asuras. Because it is equal to space, it is the Great Vehicle. Just as space affords room for sentient beings beyond measure, beyond count, beyond limit, so too, Blessed One, the Great Vehicle affords room for sentient beings beyond measure, beyond count, beyond limit. By this reasoning, Blessed One, this is the Great Vehicle of bodhisattvas, the great beings. In it no coming is seen, no going is seen, and no abiding in it exists.

Blessed One, in this Great Vehicle no former limit is perceived, no latter limit is perceived, and no middle is perceived either. Blessed One, this vehicle is equal throughout the three times. For that reason it is called the Great Vehicle, the Great Vehicle.

Then the Blessed One gave his approval to the Venerable Subhūti, saying: "Excellent! Excellent, Subhūti! That is so, that is exactly so. This, then, is the Great Vehicle of bodhisattvas, the great beings. Through training in it, bodhisattvas, the great beings, have attained omniscience, have attained it, and will attain it."

Then the Venerable Gañgadevīputra addressed the Blessed One in these words: "Blessed One, does the Elder Subhūti intend to teach the Great Vehicle through the blessing of the Perfection of Wisdom?"

Then the Venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One in these words: "Blessed One, would I not be presenting the Great Vehicle in a manner contrary to the Perfection of Wisdom?"

The Blessed One declared: "Not so, Subhūti. You are presenting the Great Vehicle in accordance with the Perfection of Wisdom."

When this was declared, the Venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One once more: "Blessed One, it is by the power of the buddhas. Blessed One, furthermore, a bodhisattva does not apprehend the former limit, a bodhisattva does not apprehend the latter limit, and a bodhisattva does not apprehend a middle. Why does the bodhisattva not apprehend them? Because form is without limit, a bodhisattva too is to be known as without limit. Likewise, because feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are without limit, a bodhisattva too is to be known as without limit. One does not apprehend 'form is the bodhisattva' — that too is absent, unperceived. Likewise, one does not apprehend 'feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are the bodhisattva' — that too is absent, unperceived.

"Blessed One, since in this way I do not perceive any phenomenon of a bodhisattva, from any direction, in any aspect, in any respect whatsoever — Blessed One, I do not correctly see the very dharma that bears the name bodhisattva. I do not correctly see, do not perceive, the Perfection of Wisdom. I do not correctly see, do not perceive, omniscience. Blessed One, since in every direction, in every aspect, in every respect, I have not correctly seen and not perceived any phenomenon, what dharma, by means of what dharma, to what dharma, would I be advising and instructing?

"Blessed One, the word 'buddha' is a mere name. Blessed One, the word 'bodhisattva' too is a mere name. Blessed One, the word 'Perfection of Wisdom' too is a mere name. That name also is not ultimately established. Blessed One, it is just as with the word 'self' — though one speaks of 'self,' Blessed One, the self is exceedingly unestablished. Likewise all phenomena are without essential nature: what, then, is form that is not to be grasped and is unestablished? What are feeling, perception, and formations? And what is consciousness that is not to be grasped and is unestablished?"

That which is the absence of essential nature in all those phenomena is itself not ultimately established. And that which is the non-ultimate-establishment of all phenomena is itself not a phenomenon. Therefore, since the Perfection of Wisdom is not ultimately established, and that which is not ultimately established is not ultimately established, what instruction and guidance is there to give? Blessed One, apart from what is not ultimately established, I perceive no phenomenon whatsoever — no qualities of a buddha, no qualities of a bodhisattva, and no engagement in awakening.

Blessed One, if, when one teaches, instructs, and thoroughly explains in this way, the mind of a bodhisattva, a great being, does not falter, does not shrink back, does not become ashamed, does not become discouraged, does not turn away in thought, does not become broken and turn aside, does not tremble, does not become frightened, does not become seized by terror — then know that this bodhisattva, this great being, is practicing the Perfection of Wisdom. This bodhisattva, this great being, is cultivating the Perfection of Wisdom. This bodhisattva, this great being, is reflecting closely upon the Perfection of Wisdom. This bodhisattva, this great being, is directing the mind resolutely toward the Perfection of Wisdom. So it should be understood.

Why is that? Blessed One, when a bodhisattva, a great being, reflects closely upon all these phenomena in the Perfection of Wisdom, at that time he does not apprehend form, does not grasp at form, does not truly follow after the arising of form, and does not truly follow after the ceasing of form. Likewise he does not apprehend feeling, does not apprehend perception, does not apprehend formations; he does not apprehend consciousness, does not grasp at consciousness, does not truly follow after the arising of consciousness, and does not truly follow after the ceasing of consciousness.

Why is that? Because that which is the absence of arising of form is not form, and that which is the absence of ceasing of form is not form. Therefore the absence of arising and what is called form are not two and are not to be made two. Likewise the absence of ceasing and what is called form are not two and are not to be made two. And so what is spoken of as form belongs to the category of non-duality.

In the same way, whatever is feeling, whatever is perception, whatever is formations — and likewise that which is the absence of arising of consciousness is not consciousness, and that which is the absence of ceasing of consciousness is not consciousness. Therefore the absence of arising and what is called consciousness are not two and are not to be made two. Likewise the absence of ceasing and what is called consciousness are not two and are not to be made two. And so what is spoken of as consciousness belongs to the category of non-duality.

Blessed One, when in the Perfection of Wisdom one reflects closely upon all phenomena in every respect, at that time one does not apprehend form, does not grasp at form, does not truly follow after the arising of form, and does not truly follow after the ceasing of form. Likewise one does not apprehend feeling, does not apprehend perception, does not apprehend formations.

One does not apprehend consciousness, does not grasp at consciousness, does not truly follow after the arising of consciousness, and does not truly follow after the ceasing of consciousness. Why is that? Because that which is the absence of arising of form is not form, and that which is the absence of ceasing of form is not form. Therefore the absence of arising and what is called form are not two and are not to be made two. Likewise the absence of ceasing and what is called form are not two and are not to be made two. And so what is spoken of as form belongs to the category of non-duality. In the same way, whatever is feeling, whatever is perception, whatever is formations — and likewise, that which is the absence of arising of consciousness is not consciousness, and that which is the absence of ceasing of consciousness is not consciousness. Therefore the absence of arising and what is called consciousness are not two and are not to be made two. Likewise the absence of ceasing and what is called consciousness are not two and are not to be made two. And so what is spoken of as consciousness belongs to the category of non-duality.

The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The Second Fascicle.

Then the Venerable Śāriputra said this to the Venerable Subhūti: "Therefore, as I understand the meaning of what the Venerable Subhūti has said, a bodhisattva too is without arising."

The Venerable Subhūti replied: "Venerable Śāriputra, if a bodhisattva is indeed without arising, then what enthusiasm could there be for a bodhisattva to engage in austerities, or to undergo all those sufferings on behalf of sentient beings?"

When this was said, the Venerable Subhūti then spoke to the Venerable Śāriputra as follows: "Venerable Śāriputra, I do not wish for a bodhisattva, a great being, to engage in austerities. Whoever practices with the perception of hardship will not even become a bodhisattva. Why is that? Venerable Śāriputra, one who has generated the perception of hardship will be unable to accomplish the aims of immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings. Rather, making it a perception of ease, one should generate toward all sentient beings the perception of them as mother, the perception of them as father, the perception of them as son, and the perception of them as daughter — and in the same way generating those perceptions toward men and women alike — and thus the bodhisattva, the great being, carries out the bodhisattva's practice. Therefore a bodhisattva, a great being, should generate toward all sentient beings the perception of mother, the perception of father, the perception of son, the perception of daughter, and even the perception of oneself, thinking: just as I am to be liberated from all suffering in every way and in every respect, so are all sentient beings to be liberated from all suffering in every way and in every respect. One should thus generate that perception toward all sentient beings, thinking: I will not abandon any of these sentient beings. I will liberate all these sentient beings from the immeasurable mass of suffering. Even if my body were cut into a hundred pieces and I were small and weak, I would not even give rise to a harmful thought toward them. Thus should a bodhisattva, a great being, arouse the mind."

If a sentient being abides in this way, such a one does not act with a perception of hardship, nor does one abide with a perception of hardship. Furthermore, venerable Śāriputra, a bodhisattva, a great being, should arouse the mind by thinking: just as I myself am, in every way and in every respect, utterly empty and without object of observation, so too are all phenomena, in every way and in every respect, utterly empty and without object of observation. One should arouse just such a perception toward all inner and outer phenomena. If the mind acts in this way, one does not act with a perception of hardship, nor does one abide with a perception of hardship.

When venerable Śāriputra said, "A bodhisattva too is without arising," Subhūti replied: "That is so, venerable Śāriputra, that is indeed so. A bodhisattva is without arising."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, is it only the bodhisattva that is without arising, or are the phenomena of the bodhisattva also without arising?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, the phenomena of the bodhisattva too are without arising."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, is it only the phenomena of the bodhisattva that are without arising, or is omniscience too without arising?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, omniscience too is without arising."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, is it only omniscience that is without arising, or are the phenomena of omniscience also without arising?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, the phenomena of omniscience too are without arising."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, is it only the phenomena of omniscience that are without arising, or are ordinary persons too without arising?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, ordinary persons too are without arising."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, is it only the ordinary person that is without arising, or are the phenomena of ordinary persons also without arising?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, the phenomena of ordinary persons too are without arising."

When this was said, venerable Śāriputra spoke to venerable Subhūti as follows: "Venerable Subhūti, if the bodhisattva is without arising, the phenomena of the bodhisattva are without arising, omniscience is without arising, the phenomena of omniscience are without arising, ordinary persons are without arising, and the phenomena of ordinary persons are without arising — then, venerable Subhūti, how does a bodhisattva, a great being, attain omniscience?"

When this was said, venerable Subhūti spoke to venerable Śāriputra as follows: "Venerable Śāriputra, I do not wish to attain what has the nature of non-arising, nor do I realize it. What is attained by means of what has the nature of non-arising does not, being non-arising, come to attain the non-arisen."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, does what has the nature of non-arising attain what is arisen, or does what has the nature of what is arisen attain what is non-arisen?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, can what has the nature of non-arising be arisen?"

"But what has the nature of non-arising is itself non-arisen."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, is what has the nature of what is arisen itself non-arisen, or is what has the nature of what is non-arisen itself non-arisen?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, I do not presume to speak of a 'nature of what is arisen' or a 'nature of what is non-arisen.'"

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, do you not presume even to speak of non-arising?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, it is precisely non-arising itself that I speak of. Venerable Śāriputra, it is precisely non-arising itself that is clearly apparent. Venerable Śāriputra, it is non-arising itself that I am confident to speak of; for, venerable Śāriputra, it is exactly as it is — clearly apparent as such."

When this was said, venerable Śāriputra spoke to venerable Subhūti as follows: "Venerable Subhūti is to be placed foremost among those who teach the Dharma. And why is that? Because the elder venerable Subhūti, in whatever matter he is thoroughly questioned, arrives at a definitive answer on that very matter, does not deviate from the nature of reality, and does not contradict that nature of reality."

When this was said, venerable Subhūti spoke to venerable Śāriputra as follows: "Venerable Śāriputra, this is the nature of reality of the Blessed One's śrāvakas who are without fixed abiding: in whatever matter they are thoroughly questioned, they arrive at a definitive answer on that very matter, do not contradict the nature of reality, and do not stray from the nature of reality. And why is that? Because all phenomena are without fixed abiding."

When this was said, venerable Śāriputra spoke to venerable Subhūti as follows: "Excellent, excellent, venerable Subhūti! What is this perfection of non-abiding in all phenomena belonging to the great bodhisattvas, the great beings?"

Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, it is precisely the Perfection of Wisdom that is the perfection of non-abiding in all phenomena for all vehicles, because all phenomena are without fixed abiding. Thus, when this profound Perfection of Wisdom is explained, taught, and demonstrated in this way, that great bodhisattva, the great being, whose mind does not shrink, does not become anxious, is not bewildered, and does not turn elsewhere — know that such a one abides in this abiding of the Perfection of Wisdom and is inseparable from this mental engagement."

Then venerable Śāriputra spoke to venerable Subhūti as follows: "Venerable Subhūti, how does the great bodhisattva, the great being, who abides in the abiding of the Perfection of Wisdom, remain inseparable from that mental engagement? Venerable Subhūti, if the great bodhisattva, the great being, is inseparable from that mental engagement, then in that case he would be separated from the abiding of the Perfection of Wisdom. And venerable Subhūti, if the great bodhisattva, the great being, is inseparable from abiding in the Perfection of Wisdom, then in that case he would be separated from that mental engagement."

"Venerable Subhūti, even if the great bodhisattva, the great being, were to be inseparable from that mental engagement and at the same time inseparable from the abiding of the Perfection of Wisdom, were that indeed the case, then all sentient beings too would be inseparable from the abiding of the Perfection of Wisdom. Why so? Because all sentient beings abide inseparably from mental engagement."

When this was said, venerable Subhūti spoke to venerable Śāriputra as follows: "Venerable Śāriputra, excellent, excellent! And yet you too will receive a counter-argument. For this very matter has been expressed in correct terms and is to be fully accepted as it stands. Why so? Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings have no essential nature, mental engagement too is to be understood as having no essential nature. Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings have no substantial existence, mental engagement too is to be understood as having no substantial existence. Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings are empty of inherent content, mental engagement too is to be understood as empty of inherent content. Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings are inconceivable, mental engagement too is to be understood as inconceivable. Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings have not attained manifest perfect awakening, mental engagement too is to be understood as not having attained manifest perfect awakening. Venerable Śāriputra, because sentient beings have attained manifest perfect awakening for the sake of what is not reality as it truly is, mental engagement too is to be understood as having attained manifest perfect awakening for the sake of what is not reality as it truly is. Venerable Śāriputra, the great bodhisattva, the great being, wishes to abide in this mental engagement of such a nature and in this very abiding."

This concludes the first chapter, entitled "The Practice of Omniscience," from the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines.

At that time, Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, gathered in that very assembly together with forty thousand divine sons of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. The four great divine kings who guard the world also assembled there, accompanied by twenty thousand divine sons of the realm of the Four Great Kings. Brahmā, lord of the world of endurance, likewise assembled there, accompanied by ten thousand divine sons of the Brahma realms. Five thousand divine sons of the Pure Abodes also gathered in that very assembly. Whatever radiance had arisen from the karmic maturation of the gods' own actions was entirely overwhelmed by the power of the Buddha, the majesty of the Buddha, and the blessing of the Buddha.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, spoke to venerable Subhūti, the elder, as follows: "Noble Subhūti, these many thousands of divine sons who have assembled in this retinue wish to hear from noble Subhūti the close exposition, the instruction, and the sequential teaching of the Perfection of Wisdom for great bodhisattvas, the great beings. How should that great bodhisattva, that great being, abide in the Perfection of Wisdom? How should he train? How should he practice yoga?"

Venerable Subhūti replied: "Kauśika, therefore the close exposition is to be given by the power of the Buddha, the majesty of the Buddha, and the blessing of the Buddha. Those divine sons who have not yet aroused the mind aimed at unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening should arouse it. As for those who have entered into the certainty of the real, they have no share in arousing the mind aimed at unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Why so?"

Because they have cut the stream of saṃsāra, they no longer turn again and again through cyclic existence, and so they have no share in arousing the mind aimed at unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Nevertheless, if they too were to arouse the mind aimed at unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, one should rejoice in that as well. As for me, I place no obstacle to their virtue; rather, one should direct one's attention above all to those qualities that are supremely excellent, transcending even the noble qualities.

Then the Blessed One declared to Venerable Subhūti: "Excellent, Subhūti, excellent! That you inspire enthusiasm in the great bodhisattvas, the great beings — in that too, Subhūti, you have done well."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, Venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, we shall indeed repay the kindness of the Blessed One; we shall not fail to repay it. Why so? Blessed One, just as you yourself, when you had formerly become a bodhisattva in the presence of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas of the past, practised the holy life for the sake of awakening on behalf of beings such as us, and the śrāvakas at that time instructed you and guided you in the perfections — and through that practice the Blessed One aroused the unsurpassed gnosis — in exactly that same way, we too shall attend upon the great bodhisattvas, the great beings, following them and encompassing them, following them completely and encompassing them completely. Why so? Blessed One, because by our following them, attending upon them, following them completely and encompassing them completely and rightly, they will swiftly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening."

Thereupon Venerable Subhūti said to Śakra (Indra), the lord of the gods: "Kauśika, therefore listen well and hold this in mind, for I shall explain to you how a great bodhisattva, a great being, should abide in the Perfection of Wisdom. Kauśika, a great bodhisattva, a great being, should abide in the Perfection of Wisdom by abiding in emptiness. Therefore, Kauśika, a great bodhisattva, a great being, should don the great armor. One should not abide in form. One should not abide in feeling, nor in perception, nor in formations, nor in consciousness. One should not abide in the eye, nor in form, nor in eye-consciousness, nor in eye-contact, nor in the feelings that arise from eye-contact. Likewise, one should not abide in the ear, the nose, the tongue, the body, or the mind; nor in sounds, smells, tastes, tactile objects, or mental phenomena; nor in ear-consciousness through to mind-consciousness; nor in mind-contact; nor in the feelings that arise from mind-contact. One should not abide in the earth element, nor in the water element, the fire element, the wind element, the space element, or the consciousness element. One should not abide in the close applications of mindfulness (skt. smṛtyupasthāna), nor in the thorough abandonments, nor in the bases of miraculous power, nor in the faculties, nor in the powers, nor in the limbs of awakening, nor in the path. One should not abide in the fruit of stream-entry.

One should not abide in the fruit of once-returning, the fruit of non-returning, or in arhathood itself. One should not abide in pratyekabuddhahood itself. One should not abide in buddhahood itself.

One should not abide thinking "this is form." One should not abide thinking "this is feeling," "this is perception," "these are formations," or "this is consciousness." One should not abide thinking "this is the eye" through to "this is feeling born of mind-contact." One should not abide thinking "this is the earth element" through to "this is the consciousness element." One should not abide thinking "this is the close application of mindfulness." One should not abide thinking "these are the thorough abandonments, the bases of miraculous power, the faculties, the powers, the limbs of awakening," or "this is a limb of the path." One should not abide thinking "this is the fruit of stream-entry." One should not abide thinking "this is the fruit of once-returning," "this is the fruit of non-returning," or "this is arhathood." One should not abide thinking "this is a pratyekabuddha." One should not abide thinking "this is a buddha."

One should not abide thinking "form is permanent" or "form is impermanent." Likewise, one should not abide thinking that feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are permanent or impermanent. One should not abide thinking "form is pleasurable" or "form is suffering." Likewise, one should not abide thinking that feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are pleasurable or suffering. One should not abide thinking "form is empty" or "form is not empty." Likewise, one should not abide thinking that feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are empty or not empty. One should not abide thinking "form has a self" or "form is selfless." Likewise, one should not abide thinking that feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness have a self or are selfless. One should not abide thinking "form is pleasant" or "form is unpleasant." Likewise, one should not abide thinking that feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are pleasant or unpleasant.

One should not abide by attending closely to form as empty. Likewise, one should not abide by attending closely to feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness as empty.

One should not abide thinking "the fruit of stream-entry is distinguished by the unconditioned." Likewise, one should not abide thinking that the fruit of once-returning, the fruit of non-returning, and arhathood are distinguished by the unconditioned. One should not abide thinking "pratyekabuddhahood is distinguished by the unconditioned." One should not abide thinking "buddhahood is distinguished by the unconditioned."

One should not abide thinking "a stream-enterer is a fitting recipient of generosity." One should not abide thinking "a stream-enterer, if obstructed for a time, will undergo seven existences." One should not abide thinking "a once-returner is a fitting recipient of generosity." One should not abide thinking "a once-returner, because of not having fully reached the end, will return to this world once more and there make an end of suffering." One should not abide thinking "a non-returner is a fitting recipient of generosity." One should not abide thinking "a non-returner will pass completely into nirvāṇa in that very realm, never returning to this world." One should not abide thinking "an arhat is a fitting recipient of generosity." One should not abide thinking "an arhat will, right here, pass completely into nirvāṇa in the element of nirvāṇa without remainder, without any residue of the aggregates." One should not abide thinking "a pratyekabuddha is a fitting recipient of generosity."

One should not abide thinking "a pratyekabuddha will pass completely into nirvāṇa, having transcended the stage of the śrāvaka yet without having attained the stage of a buddha." One should not abide thinking "a buddha is a fitting recipient of generosity." And one should not abide thinking in this way either: "A buddha, having transcended the stage of the ordinary person, the stage of the śrāvaka, and the stage of the pratyekabuddha, works for the welfare of immeasurable, countless sentient beings — leading immeasurable, countless sentient beings, hundreds of thousands of koṭis and niyutas of them, completely into nirvāṇa; establishing immeasurable, countless sentient beings definitively as śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and perfectly and fully awakened buddhas; abiding on the buddha-stage; performing the deeds of a buddha — and then, through the complete nirvāṇa of a buddha, passes completely into nirvāṇa in the element of nirvāṇa without remainder, without any residue of the aggregates."

Then the venerable Śāriputra thought to himself: "If one should not abide thinking in this way either — that a buddha, having transcended the stage of the ordinary person, the stage of the śrāvaka, and the stage of the pratyekabuddha, works for the welfare of immeasurable, countless sentient beings; leads immeasurable, countless sentient beings, hundreds of thousands of koṭis and niyutas of them, completely into nirvāṇa; establishes immeasurable, countless sentient beings definitively as śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and perfectly and fully awakened buddhas; abides on the buddha-stage; performs the deeds of a buddha; and then passes completely into nirvāṇa in the element of nirvāṇa without remainder, through the complete nirvāṇa of a buddha — then in what way should one abide in this? In what way should one train?"

Then, by the power of the Buddha, the venerable Subhūti knew with his mind the thought that had arisen in the mind of the venerable Śāriputra, and he said to the venerable Śāriputra the following: "Venerable Śāriputra, what do you think? In what does a Tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly and fully awakened buddha abide?"

The venerable Śāriputra replied: "Venerable Subhūti, a Tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly and fully awakened buddha does not abide in anything whatsoever. Why is that? Because the mind of a Tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly and fully awakened buddha is utterly without abiding: it does not abide in the conditioned element, nor does it abide in the unconditioned element, nor has it arisen from either."

Then the venerable Subhūti said to the venerable Śāriputra: "Venerable Śāriputra, it is in precisely this way that a bodhisattva, a great being, should abide and train: just as a Tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly and fully awakened buddha does not abide in anything whatsoever — neither abiding nor not-abiding, neither abiding in a contrary manner nor not-abiding in a contrary manner — one should abide in just that way. One should think: 'I will abide and train in whatever way the Tathāgata abides.' And one should think: 'If I abide as the Tathāgata abides, then I will dwell in the mode of knowing through non-abiding.' This is how one should train in this."

In this way a bodhisattva, a great being, should abide and train accordingly. A bodhisattva, a great being, who trains in this way is inseparable from this abiding of the Perfection of Wisdom and from this mental engagement.

Thereupon, some of the gods (devas) in that assembly thought to themselves:

"What the yakṣas speak — the sounds of yakṣas, the voices of yakṣas, the words of yakṣas, the speech of yakṣas, the declarations of yakṣas — all that can be understood. But what the Elder Subhūti expounds, proclaims, teaches, and fully explains cannot be understood at all."

Then the Elder Subhūti, through the power of the Buddha, discerned with his mind the precise nature of those sons of gods' mental thoughts, and said to them: "Sons of gods, as for the words 'cannot be understood, cannot be understood' — there is not even the slightest thing spoken here, and not even the slightest thing heard here."

Then those sons of gods again thought to themselves: "How wonderful! The noble Subhūti will make this clear. How wonderful! The noble Subhūti will make this clear. For the noble Subhūti enters into what is more distant than the distant, the noble Subhūti enters into what is more subtle than the subtle and more profound than the profound — and he teaches and expounds this."

Then again, through the power of the Buddha, the Elder Subhūti discerned with his mind those sons of gods' mental thoughts, and spoke to them thus: "Sons of gods, therefore, one who wishes to attain the fruit of stream-entry and who wishes to abide in the fruit of stream-entry cannot accomplish this without relying upon this patience. Likewise, one who wishes to attain the fruit of once-returning and who wishes to abide in the fruit of once-returning, one who wishes to attain the fruit of non-returning and who wishes to abide in the fruit of non-returning, one who wishes to attain arhatship and who wishes to abide in arhatship, one who wishes to attain solitary awakening and who wishes to abide in solitary awakening — none of these can be accomplished without relying upon this patience. One who wishes to attain unsurpassed, complete, and perfect awakening and who wishes to abide in unsurpassed, complete, and perfect awakening cannot accomplish this without relying upon this patience."

Then those sons of gods again thought: "What manner of hearing the Dharma should one seek from the noble Subhūti?"

Then, through the power of the Buddha, the Elder Subhūti discerned with his mind those sons of gods' mental thoughts, and said to them: "Sons of gods, one should seek to hear the Dharma from me as one seeks something like an illusion and an emanation. Why so? Because they neither hear nor directly realize."

Then those sons of gods said to the Elder Subhūti: "Noble Subhūti, do you mean that those sentient beings are like an illusion, while the illusion is not those sentient beings?"

When this was said, the Elder Subhūti spoke to those sons of gods thus: "Sons of gods, those sentient beings are like an illusion; sons of gods, those sentient beings are like a dream. Sons of gods, therefore, illusion and sentient beings are not two and cannot be made into two. Likewise, dream and sentient beings are not two and cannot be made into two. Sons of gods, all phenomena too are like an illusion and like a dream. A stream-enterer too is like an illusion and like a dream. The fruit of stream-entry too is like an illusion and like a dream. Likewise, the once-returner and the fruit of once-returning, the non-returner and the fruit of non-returning,

the arhat too is like an illusion and like a dream. Arhathood too is like an illusion and like a dream. The pratyekabuddha too is like an illusion and like a dream. Pratyekabuddhahood too is like an illusion and like a dream. A perfectly and fully awakened buddha too is like an illusion and like a dream. Perfect and full buddhahood too is like an illusion and like a dream.

Then those sons of gods said this to the Venerable Subhūti: "Noble Subhūti, are you saying that a perfectly and fully awakened buddha too is like an illusion and like a dream, and that perfect and full buddhahood too is like an illusion and like a dream?"

Subhūti replied: "Sons of gods, if I were to speak of nirvāṇa as like an illusion and like a dream, what need is there to speak of other phenomena? Those sons of gods said: "Noble Subhūti, are you saying that nirvāṇa too is like an illusion and like a dream?"

The Venerable Subhūti replied: "Sons of gods, even if there were some phenomenon more exalted than nirvāṇa, I would say of that too that it is like an illusion and like a dream. Sons of gods, therefore, illusion and nirvāṇa are not two and cannot be made into two. Likewise, dream and nirvāṇa are not two and cannot be made into two."

Then the Venerable Śāriputra, the Venerable Maudgalyāyana, the Venerable Mahākāśyapa, the Venerable Mahākatyāyana, the Venerable Mahākauṣṭhila, and other great śrāvakas, together with many hundreds of thousands of bodhisattvas, all said this to the Venerable Elder Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, who will come to properly hold this teaching of the Perfection of Wisdom as it has been set forth here?"
