Furthermore, a son of good family or daughter of good family who follows the bodhisattva vehicle should dedicate in the following way: whatever moral discipline there may be, whatever meditative absorption, whatever wisdom, whatever liberation, whatever the wisdom-vision of liberation — which does not belong to the desire realm, does not belong to the form realm, does not belong to the formless realm, and is neither past, nor future, nor present — one should dedicate in precisely that way.

Why? Because it does not belong to the three times or the three realms, and therefore the dedication likewise does not belong to them. Whatever dharma one dedicates to, that dharma to which the dedication is made also does not belong to them.

If one aspires in this way and dedicates in this way, the dedication of that bodhisattva, that great being who so dedicates, is inexhaustible — it is a dedication that does not belong to any of those, a dedication free of poison, a great dedication, a dedication within the dharmadhātu, complete and well complete.

But if one clings to and reifies that very dedication as a sign, then it becomes a mistaken dedication.

Now, regarding this dedication: the root of virtue that a bodhisattva, a great being, dedicates — dedicating it within the dharmadhātu just as the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, know it and just as they have approved it — thinking, "In this way I have well and truly dedicated it to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening," that is indeed a well-performed dedication. And thinking, "In this way I dedicate," that is a true and genuine dedication. Such a dedication is a dedication well made toward unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.

Then the Blessed One gave his approval to the venerable Subhūti, saying: "Excellent! Subhūti, you are performing the work of a teacher. Subhūti, it is excellent, truly excellent, that you teach the Dharma to bodhisattvas, to great beings. And why is that? Because this dedication is the dedication within the dharmadhātu of bodhisattvas, of great beings. The buddhas, the Blessed Ones, know and behold this very nature of reality — the root of virtue, of whatever kind, whatever category it belongs to, whatever its character, whatever its intrinsic nature, whatever its defining mark, whatever the nature of reality by which it exists — and they rejoice accordingly. Thinking, 'Just as they have approved it, so do I dedicate' — the mass of merit arising from such a thought, from such a dedication: even compared to the mass of merit that some son or daughter of good family might accumulate by establishing all the sentient beings — as many as there are in world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges river multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — by properly placing and maintaining them on the path of the ten virtuous actions, the mass of merit born from this dedication within the dharmadhātu of a bodhisattva, a great being, is supreme, is excellent, is foremost, is the best, is the most excellent, is the magnificent, is the highest, is unsurpassed, is without anything above it, is without equal, is equal to the unequal.

Subhūti, to set aside even the merit that accumulates from establishing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — on the path of the ten virtuous actions: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those beings to attain the four meditative concentrations (dhyānas) is surpassed by this mass of merit accompanying the dedication of a bodhisattva, a great being. That mass of merit is supreme, is excellent, is foremost, is the best, is the most excellent, is the magnificent, is the highest, is unsurpassed, is without anything above it, is without equal, is equal to the unequal.

Subhūti, to set aside even the merit of accumulating the merit of all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems — having perfected the four meditative concentrations: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings in world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm to attain the four immeasurables —

Subhūti, to set aside even the merit of accumulating the merit of all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — having attained the four formless absorptions (Skt. ārūpyasamāpatti): even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings in world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm to attain the four formless absorptions —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — to attain the five higher knowledges (Skt. abhijñā) —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — to become stream-enterers (Skt. srotāpanna) —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — to become once-returners (Skt. sakṛdāgāmin) —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — to become non-returners (Skt. anāgāmin) —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — to become arhats —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: even the mass of merit arising from causing all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — to become pratyekabuddhas —

Subhūti, to set aside even that: suppose all those sentient beings — as many as there are in those world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm — were to enter into unsurpassed perfect and complete awakening, and all of them were then to cause every sentient being in yet other world-systems, each as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm, to receive from each individual bodhisattva robes, alms-food,

bedding, medicine for the sick, requisites, and every comfort and convenience — serving them for a number of kalpas equal to the grains of sand in the Ganges — and that this giving were done with a perception of an object of reference; and if, considering it in that way, every one of those sentient beings were in turn served by each and every one of those bodhisattvas for a number of kalpas equal to the grains of sand in the Ganges with robes, alms-food, bedding, medicine for the sick, requisites, and every comfort and convenience — honoring them, revering them, esteeming them, making offerings to them, painting their images, and paying them homage — and if likewise each and every one of those bodhisattvas were to give in this manner, and were to give to all in this manner — Subhūti, what do you think? Would those bodhisattvas thereby generate a great quantity of merit?"

Subhūti replied: "A great quantity, Blessed One! A great quantity, Sugata! It is beyond measure, Blessed One! It is beyond measure, Sugata! Blessed One, that mass of merit is not easy even to count. Blessed One, that mass of merit is not easy to enumerate, to illustrate by example, to demonstrate by analogy, to take as a basis, or to take as a cause. Blessed One, if that mass of merit were to have material form, it would not fit within world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges multiplied by the great trichiliocosm."

When this was said, the Blessed One spoke these words to the elder, the venerable Subhūti: "Just so, Subhūti, just so. Subhūti, the reason why a person of the bodhisattva vehicle who dedicates roots of virtue to unsurpassed perfect and complete awakening — grasping them fully through the Perfection of Wisdom and through skillful means, and dedicating them through this dedication within the dharmadhātu — thereby generates great merit is this: Subhūti, the mass of merit born from dedication within the dharmadhātu does not come within even a hundredth part of the merit fabricated through the giving of those earlier bodhisattvas and great beings who possess a perception of an object of reference; it does not come within a thousandth part, a hundred-thousandth part, a ten-millionth part, a hundred-millionth part, a billionth part, a hundred-billionth part, a ten-trillionth part, nor a hundred-trillionth part. It does not come within any countable fraction, nor can it be reached by enumeration, by example, by illustration through analogy, by being taken as a basis, as a cause, or as a proximate cause. Why is that? Because the giving of those earlier bodhisattvas who possessed a perception of an object of reference — however much they gave, however abundantly — would still be countable as a quantity."

Then twenty thousand sons of the gods of the retinue of the Four Great Kings joined their palms together, bowed down, and addressed the Blessed One thus: "Blessed One, this dedication — through which all roots of virtue grasped by a bodhisattva or great being through the Perfection of Wisdom and through skillful means are dedicated to omniscience — this is a great dedication.

For this very reason, that dedication — the one that actively accumulates the mass of merit arising from the giving of those bodhisattvas who possessed a perception of an object of reference — is itself overwhelmed and surpassed by it, however great that mass may be.

Then one hundred thousand sons of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three scattered and strewed before the Blessed One a rain of divine flowers, divine incense, divine fragrances, divine garlands, divine unguents, divine powders, divine precious jewels, and divine garments. With divine parasols, divine victory banners, divine bells, divine pennants, and divine offerings of many kinds, they showed honor to the Blessed One, exalted him, paid him homage, made offerings, glorified him, and rendered him reverence. And having sounded the divine cymbals, they spoke these words: "O Blessed One, this dedication by which the bodhisattva, the great being, dedicates to the dharmadhātu is a great dedication. For it overwhelms and surpasses even that vast mass of merit — actively accumulating the merit arising from the giving of those bodhisattvas who possessed a perception of an object of reference — however great that mass may be. For this reason it is a great dedication, because it is embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and by skillful means."

Likewise, from other divine realms, gods came forth and paid the Blessed One the highest honor, the highest exaltation, the highest homage, the highest offering, the highest glorification, and the highest reverence. Having honored him, exalted him, paid him homage, made offerings, glorified him, and rendered him reverence, they raised their voices and sang in chorus.

And so too should it be understood with regard to the gods of the Heaven of Freedom from Strife (Yāma), the Heaven of Joy (Tuṣita), the Heaven of Delight in Creation (Nirmāṇarati), the Heaven of Making Use of Others' Creations (Paranirmitavaśavartin), the Heaven of the Brahmā Class, the retinue of Brahmā, the company of Brahmā, the Great Brahmā, the gods of Minor Light, Immeasurable Light, Radiant Light, Minor Virtue, Immeasurable Virtue, Abundant Virtue, the Cloudless, the Merit-Increasing, the Great Fruit, the beings without perception, the Not-Great, the Not-Distressed, the Auspicious Appearance, the Clearly Seeing, and the gods of the Highest Heaven — all of these as well joined their palms together, bowed down to the Blessed One, and spoke these words: "Blessed One, it is wondrous that this dedication of roots of virtue — dedicated by a bodhisattva, a great being, who has been embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and by skillful means — should overwhelm and surpass that mass of merit accumulated over so long a time, that great mass of merit so extensively and thoroughly accomplished, by a bodhisattva who possessed a perception of an object of reference. It is truly marvelous."

Then the Blessed One addressed the sons of the gods of the Pure Abodes and all those sons of the gods, saying: "Sons of the gods, set aside even the accumulation of merit through giving, done for the sake of enabling all sentient beings in world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges River — all of them having entered upon unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening — to attain unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Sons of the gods, by this same reckoning, in those other world-systems as numerous as the grains of sand in yet other Ganges Rivers, all the sentient beings dwelling there, having set forth an aspiration toward unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening and having aroused the mind of awakening,

in great trichiliocosms as numerous as the grains of sand in yet other and yet other Ganges Rivers, providing all sentient beings there with monastic robes, alms food, bedding, medicine for the sick, necessities, every comfort, and every pleasant sensation — with each and every bodhisattva sitting for a number of aeons equal to the grains of sand in the Ganges River and giving in that way — and likewise all of those giving with the perception of a referent object, giving in that way throughout — and then, by this same reckoning, having considered individually each and every one of those sentient beings and each and every one of those bodhisattvas, from each bodhisattva all the way to all those bodhisattvas, sitting for a number of aeons equal to the grains of sand in the Ganges River and attending upon all those sentient beings with monastic robes, alms food, bedding, medicine for the sick, necessities, every comfort, and every pleasant sensation — and even that giving done with the perception of a referent object — compared with a certain bodhisattva, a great being, endowed with the Perfection of Wisdom and perfectly held by skillful means, who rejoices with the supreme rejoicing — the rejoicing that is foremost, chief, excellent, supremely excellent, distinguished, highest, unsurpassed, unexcelled, higher even than the highest, incomparable, matchless and equal to the matchless, and supremely equal to the matchless, and inconceivable — in all the wholesome roots that have been, are being, and will be cultivated in connection with the heaps of moral discipline, the heaps of meditative absorption, the heaps of wisdom, the heaps of liberation, and the heaps of the wisdom of the vision of liberation of the blessed buddhas of the past, present, and future, together with the bodhisattvas, the pratyekabuddhas, and those of the śrāvaka vehicle, and also in connection with all the wholesome roots that other sentient beings have cultivated, are cultivating, and will cultivate — having gathered all of these together, totaled them, measured them, and left nothing out, and having rejoiced in the totality of them with that supreme rejoicing — who then dedicates that merit-producing object of rejoicing together with that dedication, saying, "May this serve to accomplish unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening," fully dedicating it to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening: the merit generated through the former accumulation of merit by those bodhisattvas who give with the perception of a referent object does not come close to even a hundredth part of this merit-producing object endowed with rejoicing and dedication. It does not come close to a thousandth part, nor to a hundred-thousandth part, nor to a ten-millionth part, nor to a billion part, nor to a trillion part, nor to a hundred-trillion part, nor to a thousand-trillion part, nor to a hundred-thousand-trillion part. It cannot be reached by counting, reckoning, example, illustration, basis, or cause. And why is that? It is because all those bodhisattvas give their giving with the perception of a referent object.

The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The Eighth Fascicle.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "The Blessed One has spoken of all the wholesome roots — past, present, and future — of all the blessed buddhas of the past, present, and future, together with the bodhisattvas, the pratyekabuddhas, and the entire Saṅgha of śrāvakas, and of all sentient beings — past, present, and future — having gathered all of these together, totaled them, measured them, and left nothing out.

rejoicing in all of them without exception with the supreme act of rejoicing — rejoicing with the incomprehensible act of rejoicing, which is supreme, excellent, foremost, unsurpassed, highest, supreme above the supreme, matchless, unequaled, equal and supremely equal to none" — when you spoke thus of rejoicing in this way, Blessed One, by what means does one's rejoicing become supreme?

When he had asked this, the Blessed One spoke these words to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, if persons who have set their course in the bodhisattva vehicle do not grasp, do not conceive with pride, do not apprehend, do not conceptualize, do not elaborately conceptualize, and do not rightly perceive phenomena of the past, present, and future — and yet they examine those phenomena in this way: 'All phenomena are established through conceptual fabrication. They are unborn, definitively unborn, without coming, without going. In this, no phenomenon has ever arisen, no phenomenon will arise, no phenomenon is arising. In this, no phenomenon has ever ceased, no phenomenon will cease, no phenomenon is ceasing' — having thus examined those phenomena closely, they rejoice in accordance with just how the nature of reality of those phenomena is. Having so rejoiced, they dedicate that rejoicing truly and completely to unsurpassable, perfect, complete awakening. Subhūti, by this means does the rejoicing of a great bodhisattva become supreme.

"Subhūti, in this dedication of wholesome roots, the merit-producing thing — the merit arising from the giving of those earlier bodhisattvas who perceived a referent object and who looked upon things as referentially existing — does not come even to a hundredth part of it, not to a thousandth part, not to a hundred-thousandth part, not to a ten-millionth part, not to a hundred-ten-millionth part, not to a thousand-ten-millionth part, not to a hundred-thousand-ten-millionth part, not to a hundred-thousand-hundred-thousandth part. It cannot bear comparison in number, in count, by example, by illustration, as a basis, or as a cause.

"Subhūti, furthermore, if a person set on the bodhisattva vehicle wishes to rejoice in the generosity of all the blessed buddhas of the past, future, and present, wishes to rejoice in their moral discipline, wishes to rejoice in their patience, wishes to rejoice in their diligence, wishes to rejoice in their meditative concentration, and wishes to rejoice in their wisdom — that person should rejoice in this manner: As liberation is, so is generosity. As liberation is, so is moral discipline. As liberation is, so is patience. As liberation is, so is diligence. As liberation is, so is meditative concentration. As liberation is, so is wisdom. As liberation is, so is liberation itself. As liberation is, so is the aggregate of the gnosis that sees liberation. As liberation is, so is the rejoicing. As liberation is, so is the merit-producing thing that comes with that rejoicing. As liberation is, so is the dedication. As liberation is, so are the blessed buddhas and the pratyekabuddhas. As liberation is, so are those śrāvakas of theirs who have passed into complete nirvāṇa. As liberation is, so are those phenomena of the past that have ceased and stopped. As liberation is, so are those phenomena of the future that have not yet come or arisen."

As liberation is, so are those phenomena of the present that have arisen and are presently abiding. As liberation is, so are the Blessed One buddhas of the past and their śrāvakas. As liberation is, so are the Blessed One buddhas of the future and their śrāvakas. As liberation is, so are the present Blessed One buddhas — those who now dwell, live, and remain in immeasurable and innumerable world-realms — and their śrāvakas.

As liberation is, so are all the Blessed One buddhas of the past, future, and present. In just this way, one rejoices with unsurpassed rejoicing in the nature of reality of these phenomena, which are neither bound nor liberated nor attached. Having so rejoiced, one dedicates the unsurpassed merit-producing thing that arises with that rejoicing — not by way of dedicating it to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, but rather dedicating it in accordance with the way things are, with the immovable and indestructible nature of reality of those very phenomena. This is what is called dedication.

Subhūti, for this reason alone this constitutes the supreme rejoicing of a bodhisattva, a great being.

Moreover, Subhūti, suppose that all the sentient beings present in world-realms as numerous as the grains of sand in as many Gaṅgā rivers as there are worlds in a great trichiliocosm were to enter rightly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Having entered rightly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, suppose further that all the sentient beings in those same world-realms — as numerous as Gaṅgā's grains of sand — were to generate the mind of awakening. And suppose that from each and every one of those bodhisattvas, one bodhisattva, sitting for a number of eons equal to the grains of sand in the Gaṅgā, were to give gifts — serving each and every bodhisattva with robes, alms-food, bedding, medicines for the sick, all requisites and all means of comfort, and all that brings contact with happiness, doing so with a perception that has an object of reference, and likewise all of them serving all the others in this same way, giving gifts with such a perception that has an object of reference. Subhūti, let that be.

Now, Subhūti, suppose that all the sentient beings — however many there are in world-realms as numerous as the grains of sand in as many Gaṅgā rivers as there are worlds in a great trichiliocosm — were all to enter rightly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Having entered rightly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, suppose that for a number of eons equal to the grains of sand in the Gaṅgā they were to properly take up and abide in the moral discipline of right conduct of body, right conduct of speech, and right conduct of mind — with a perception that has an object of reference. And suppose likewise that each and every one of those bodhisattvas were to sit for a number of eons equal to the grains of sand in the Gaṅgā and for that same number of eons were to properly take up and abide in the moral discipline of right conduct of body, right conduct of speech, and right conduct of mind — with a perception that has an object of reference. And in this same way, suppose all of those bodhisattvas were likewise to properly take up and abide in moral discipline with a perception that has an object of reference.

Now suppose a bodhisattva, a great being, who is embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and by skillful means, were to rejoice in the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis that sees liberation belonging to the Blessed One buddhas of the past, future, and present —

and the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis that sees liberation belonging to those pratyekabuddhas (solitary buddhas); and all the roots of virtue of all sentient beings of the past, future, and present — were to gather all of those together, collect them, measure them, and leave nothing out; and then, having left nothing out, were to rejoice in all of them with the supreme rejoicing — a rejoicing that is holy, primary, excellent, supremely excellent, distinguished, unsurpassed, without anything higher, without anything above it, above even what is above, incomparable, unequalled, supremely equalled by nothing, and inconceivable — and having so rejoiced, were to fully dedicate to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening the supreme merit-producing substance that is endowed with such rejoicing:

then, Subhūti, that earlier merit-producing substance that is endowed with rejoicing — the merit accumulated from the moral discipline of those bodhisattvas who abide in moral discipline with a perception that has an object of reference — does not come within even a hundredth part of this. It does not come within a thousandth part, a hundred-thousandth part, a ten-millionth part, a billion part, a ten-billion part, a hundred-billion part, a trillion part, a hundred-trillion part. It does not come within any numerical reckoning, does not come within any count, does not bear any analogy, cannot be illustrated by any example, has no basis for comparison, and admits of no causal measure.

And why is this? Because all of those bodhisattvas properly took up and abided in moral discipline with a perception that has an object of reference.

Now, Subhūti, suppose all the sentient beings in as many world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River were all to enter perfectly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening; and having so entered, were to abide for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River, properly taking up and abiding in moral discipline — right conduct of body, right conduct of speech, and right conduct of mind — with a perception that has an object of reference.

And now, Subhūti, suppose all the sentient beings in as many world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to enter perfectly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, and having entered, were to remain for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges; and suppose that all the sentient beings in as many other and yet other world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were each, for every single one of those bodhisattvas, to scratch, beat, and abuse them, and yet those bodhisattvas were to properly take up and abide in patience with pride — that is, with a perception that has an object of reference — and in this same way all of those bodhisattvas were likewise to properly take up and abide in patience. And in this same enumeration, each and every one of those bodhisattvas were to remain for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges, and all the sentient beings in as many other and yet other world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to scratch, beat, and abuse them for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges; and yet those bodhisattvas were to properly take up and abide in patience with pride — with a perception that has an object of reference — and likewise all of those were, every one of them, to be scratched, beaten, and abused by all, and yet were to properly take up and abide in patience with pride and a perception that has an object of reference.

A bodhisattva, a great being, who is fully endowed with the Perfection of Wisdom and with skillful means, were to gather together, sum up, measure out, and leave nothing unaccounted of all the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, of the past, the future, and the present; and likewise the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all those pratyekabuddhas; and likewise the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all those śrāvakas; and all the roots of virtue of all sentient beings of the past, the future, and the present — having gathered all of these together and summed them up without remainder, and having left nothing unaccounted — were then to rejoice in them all without remainder with the foremost act of rejoicing: rejoicing with that which is supreme, primary, excellent, supremely excellent, distinguished, unsurpassed, without superior, without anything above it, above even what is above, without equal, incomparable, equal to the equal, supremely equal, and inconceivable — and having so rejoiced, were to dedicate that meritorious basis accompanied by rejoicing to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening: then, Subhūti, the meritorious basis accompanied by rejoicing described here would not come even within a hundredth part of the meritorious basis that accrues from the patience of those earlier bodhisattvas who had an object of reference. It would not come within a thousandth part, nor a hundred-thousandth part, nor within a ten-millionth part, nor a billion-th part, nor a ten-billion-th part, nor a hundred-billion-th part, nor a hundred-trillion-th part. It cannot be counted, it cannot be reckoned, it cannot be compared, it cannot be illustrated by a simile, it cannot serve as a basis, it cannot serve as a cause — it does not bear comparison. And why is that? Because all those bodhisattvas had taken up and abided in patience through a perception that has an object of reference.

Now, Subhūti, suppose all the sentient beings in as many world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to enter properly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, and having entered properly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, all the sentient beings in as many other and yet other world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to scratch, beat, and abuse them — and yet they were to properly take up and abide in patience with pride and a perception that has an object of reference, for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges. Let that be set aside. Subhūti, suppose all the sentient beings there are in as many world-systems of the great three-thousand-fold universe as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to enter properly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, and having entered properly into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, were to dwell and conduct themselves there for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges; and for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges they were to dwell without flagging, without being overcome by sloth and drowsiness, and were to take up and abide in diligence with a perception that has an object of reference — and a bodhisattva, a great being, fully endowed with the Perfection of Wisdom and with skillful means, were to gather together all the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, of the past, the future, and the present,

and were also to gather together all the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all those pratyekabuddhas, and all the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all those śrāvakas, and all the roots of virtue accumulated in the past, the future, and the present by all sentient beings — gathering all of these together into one, summing them up, measuring them out, leaving none aside, and then with the supreme rejoicing that leaves none aside, were to rejoice — rejoicing with a rejoicing that is supreme, excellent, foremost, outstanding, extraordinary, highest, unsurpassed, unexcelled, beyond even what is supreme, incomparable, without equal, matchless, utterly without peer, and inconceivable — and having so rejoiced, were to dedicate that supreme meritorious act endowed with rejoicing to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening: then, Subhūti, this supreme meritorious act endowed with rejoicing — compared to the meritorious act of actively accumulating merit produced by the diligence of those earlier bodhisattvas possessed of a perception with an object of reference — does not approach even a hundredth part of it, nor a thousandth, nor a hundred-thousandth, nor a ten-millionth, nor a hundred-millionth, nor a thousand-millionth, nor a hundred-thousand-millionth, nor even a hundred-thousand-trillionth part of it. It cannot be reached by number, by calculation, by simile, by illustration, by ratio, or by cause. And why is that? It is because all those bodhisattvas took up and abided in diligence with a perception that has an object of reference.

Subhūti, consider further: suppose all the sentient beings in a world-system of a great trichiliocosm as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges were to enter genuinely into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, and having so entered, were to remain for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges — dwelling, walking, and abiding continuously — and for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to dwell without flagging, without being overcome by sloth and drowsiness, and were to take up and abide in diligence with a perception that has an object of reference. Now, Subhūti, suppose those very sentient beings — all of them, as many as there are in a world-system of a great trichiliocosm as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges — having all entered genuinely into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, and having so entered, for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges were to enter into meditative equipoise in the four meditative concentrations with a perception that has an object of reference. And suppose further that a bodhisattva, a great being, fully endowed with this Perfection of Wisdom and with skillful means, were to gather together all the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, of the past, the future, and the present, and the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all those pratyekabuddhas, and the aggregates of moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis of the vision of liberation belonging to all those śrāvakas, and all the roots of virtue accumulated in the past, the future, and the present by all sentient beings — gathering all of these together into one, summing them up, measuring them out, leaving none aside, and then with the supreme rejoicing that leaves none aside, were to rejoice —

— the supreme rejoicing that is incalculable, incomprehensible, rejoicing in what is holy and primary, excellent and supremely excellent, extraordinary and exalted, unsurpassed and utterly without anything above it, higher even than the highest, incomparable, unequal, equal, and supremely equal — and were then to dedicate to the unsurpassable, perfectly complete awakening that very merit-producing substance belonging to that rejoicing: then, Subhūti, that merit-producing substance belonging to such rejoicing would not come even to a hundredth part of this merit-producing substance that arises from the four meditative absorptions of bodhisattvas who have objects, nor to a thousandth, nor to a hundred-thousandth, nor to a ten-thousandth, nor to a hundred ten-thousandth, nor to a thousand ten-thousandth, nor to a hundred-thousand ten-thousandth, nor to a hundred-thousand-million ten-thousandth part. It cannot be reckoned, cannot be counted, cannot be illustrated by an example, cannot be demonstrated by comparison, cannot be made a basis for it, cannot be made a cause for it — it is beyond all measure. And why is that? It is because all those bodhisattvas enter into meditative absorptions while bearing the perception of an object. This is the sixth chapter, entitled "Rejoicing and Dedication," from the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines.

Then the venerable Śāriputra addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom fully accomplishes the gnosis of omniscience. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is omniscience itself."

The Blessed One declared: "It is so, Śāriputra. It is exactly as you say."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom illuminates. I pay homage to the Perfection of Wisdom, O Blessed One. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is worthy of homage. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is without defilement. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is undefiled by the whole world. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom makes things radiant. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom dispels all obscuration from the three realms. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom dispels all the darkness of affliction and false views. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom gives stability. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom makes supreme. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom brings to perfection all the qualities on the side of awakening. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom illumines those sentient beings who are blind. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom utterly removes all fear and harm, and makes things radiant. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom, by fully conferring the five eyes, shows the path to all sentient beings. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is the eye itself. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom frees from delusion, darkness, and all obscuration. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom dispels all obscuration."

Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom does not fabricate any phenomenon. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom leads onto the path those sentient beings who have gone astray. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is omniscience itself. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom does not give rise to any phenomenon, for the sake of abandoning all the afflictive and cognitive obscurations together with their habitual tendencies and their connections. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom does not suppress any phenomenon. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is unborn and unceased. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is the mother of all bodhisattvas, great beings, by virtue of the emptiness of intrinsic characteristics. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom acts with the ten powers for the sake of conferring all the precious qualities of a buddha. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is invincible for the sake of producing the four fearlessnesses. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is the protector of those sentient beings who have no protector. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is the antidote to cyclic existence, since nothing abides permanently. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom reveals the essential nature of all phenomena. Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is the complete fulfillment of the turning of the wheel of Dharma by the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, recited three times and set in motion in twelve aspects.

Blessed One, how should a bodhisattva, a great being, abide in the Perfection of Wisdom? Blessed One, how should one attend to the Perfection of Wisdom? Blessed One, how should one pay homage to the Perfection of Wisdom?

When this was said, the Blessed One spoke thus to the venerable Śāriputra: Śāriputra, one should abide in the Perfection of Wisdom just as the Teacher knows it. Śāriputra, one should attend to the Perfection of Wisdom just as the Teacher knows it. Śāriputra, one should pay homage to the Perfection of Wisdom just as the Teacher knows it.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods (Indra), thought to himself: How remarkable that the noble Śāriputra has asked this question! How remarkable — from what basis has the noble Śāriputra asked this?

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, said this to the venerable Śāriputra: Noble Śāriputra, why have you asked this? Noble Śāriputra, from what basis have you asked this?

When he had spoken thus, the venerable Śāriputra said this to Śakra, lord of the gods: Kauśika, a bodhisattva, a great being, who is fully sustained by the skillful means of the Perfection of Wisdom, rejoices in that and dedicates all the meritorious works gathered thereby entirely to omniscience. All the merit accumulated from generosity by those earlier bodhisattvas who had an object of focus — whatever merit arises from generosity, whatever arises from moral discipline, whatever arises from patience, whatever arises from diligence,

whatever merit arises from meditative concentration — all of that which is accumulated and made manifest it utterly surpasses and overwhelms. That is why I have asked from this basis. Kauśika, and yet all five perfections enter the path to omniscience only when the Perfection of Wisdom goes before them.

Kauśika, consider this analogy: a hundred blind people, or a thousand, or a hundred thousand — if there is no one to guide them fully, they have no chance of setting out on the road, and no chance of reaching a village, a town, or a city. In just the same way, Kauśika, when generosity, moral discipline, patience, diligence, and meditative concentration are separated from the Perfection of Wisdom, they do not attain even the name "perfection" and are like the blind — for without a guide they have no chance of entering the path to omniscience, so how could they ever enter or arrive at the great city of omniscience?

Kauśika, but when generosity, moral discipline, patience, diligence, and meditative concentration are fully taken up by the Perfection of Wisdom, they then attain the name and word "perfection," and at that point these five perfections have gained eyes in order to enter the path to omniscience and to arrive at omniscience in due course.

Then the venerable Śāriputra said this to the Blessed One: Blessed One, how should a bodhisattva, a great being, accomplish the Perfection of Wisdom?

When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One declared this to the venerable Śāriputra: Śāriputra, one should look upon form as something not to be accomplished. Śāriputra, in the same way, one should look upon feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness as things not to be accomplished. Śāriputra, not accomplishing the five aggregates — that, Śāriputra, is what is called accomplishing the Perfection of Wisdom. By not constructing and accumulating in that way, accomplishing the five aggregates is accomplishing the Perfection of Wisdom — and it is what is called accomplishment.

When he had declared this, the venerable Śāriputra said this to the Blessed One: Blessed One, when one accomplishes the Perfection of Wisdom in that way, what dharma does one project? The Blessed One declared: Śāriputra, when one accomplishes the Perfection of Wisdom in that way, one projects no dharma whatsoever. Śāriputra, when one projects no dharma whatsoever, that comes to be counted as what is called the Perfection of Wisdom.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, said this to the Blessed One: Blessed One, does this Perfection of Wisdom then also not project even omniscience? The Blessed One declared: Kauśika, when you say, "Does this Perfection of Wisdom not project even omniscience?" — it does not project in the way something is apprehended as an object. It does not project in the way a name is posited. It does not project in the way something is constructed and accumulated. Śakra said: Blessed One, then how does one project? The Blessed One declared: Kauśika, one projects in the very way that one does not project. Śakra said:

Śakra said: Blessed One, as long as this Perfection of Wisdom neither produces nor obstructs any phenomenon, and as long as one does not even remain in proximity to it for the sake of all phenomena being without arising and without ceasing — this Perfection of Wisdom is wonderful.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: Blessed One, if a bodhisattva, a great being, were to perceive it in just that way and were to make this Perfection of Wisdom something distant, were to accumulate this Perfection of Wisdom, were to fill himself with this Perfection of Wisdom — that would be not practicing this Perfection of Wisdom. Having said this, the Blessed One declared to the venerable Subhūti: So it is, Subhūti, so it is. Subhūti, there is this description of the way in which one would make this Perfection of Wisdom something distant, would accumulate this Perfection of Wisdom, would fill oneself with this Perfection of Wisdom — and thereby not practice this Perfection of Wisdom. Why is that? Subhūti, when this Perfection of Wisdom is made perfectly clear, form is not made perfectly clear; feeling is not, perception is not, formations are not, consciousness is not made perfectly clear. The fruit of stream-entry is not made perfectly clear; the fruit of once-returning is not made perfectly clear; the fruit of non-returning is not made perfectly clear; arhatship is not made perfectly clear; the state of a pratyekabuddha is not made perfectly clear; and buddhahood is not made perfectly clear.

The elder Subhūti said: Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is therefore a great perfection. The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, what do you think — by what account is this Perfection of Wisdom a great perfection? The elder Subhūti said: Blessed One, it does not make form great, it does not make it small, it does not gather form in, it does not scatter it. Likewise with feeling — no, and perception — no, and formations — no. Blessed One, it does not make consciousness great, it does not make it small, it does not gather consciousness in, it does not scatter it. Whatever the powers of the Tathāgatas may be — those tathāgata-powers, too, it does not render possessed of power, it does not render empty of power, it does not gather them in, it does not scatter them. Whatever omniscience may be, it does not make it great, it does not make it small, it does not gather it in, it does not scatter it. Why so? Blessed One, omniscience itself is ungathered and unscattered. Blessed One, even if a bodhisattva, a great being, were to seek in such a way yet not to be engaged in the Perfection of Wisdom, were to think, "I am endowed with the gnosis of omniscience; I shall teach the Dharma to sentient beings in just this way; I shall lead these sentient beings completely to nirvāṇa" — how much more so that very thought! Why? Because that thought, "I shall lead these sentient beings completely to nirvāṇa," is not in accord with the Perfection of Wisdom; the very act of taking sentient beings as an object of reference becomes a great object of reference for it. Why? Because the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as having the intrinsic nature of being devoid of the essential nature of sentient beings and devoid of arising.

Because sentient beings are devoid of essential nature, the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as devoid of essential nature. Because sentient beings are solitary and apart, the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as solitary and apart. Because sentient beings are inconceivable, the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as inconceivable. Because it is the nature of reality of sentient beings not to perish, the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as having the nature of reality of not perishing. Because sentient beings do not fully awaken to complete and perfect buddhahood, the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as not fully awakening to complete and perfect buddhahood. Because sentient beings do not fully awaken to complete and perfect buddhahood exactly as the ultimate meaning truly is, the Perfection of Wisdom is to be known as not fully awakening to complete and perfect buddhahood exactly as the ultimate meaning truly is. Because sentient beings perfectly accomplish power, the powers of the Tathāgata are to be known as perfectly accomplished. Blessed One, by this enumeration, this Perfection of Wisdom is indeed the Great Perfection.

Then the venerable Śāriputra spoke these words to the Blessed One: Blessed One, whichever bodhisattva, great being, has faith in this profound Perfection of Wisdom and does not waver, does not doubt, does not become confused — Blessed One, from where did that one pass away and be born here? Blessed One, that bodhisattva, great being, will follow after, will seek out, and will come to understand the meaning of this Perfection of Wisdom, its nature of reality, the manner of its meaning, and the manner of its nature of reality — know that such a one has practiced for a long time and has kept to the precepts. When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One declared these words to the venerable Śāriputra: Śāriputra, know that such a bodhisattva, great being, has passed away from other world-realms, having attended upon and thoroughly questioned the buddhas, the Blessed Ones there, and has been born here. Why is this? Śāriputra, whatever bodhisattva, great being, has attended upon and thoroughly questioned the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, has passed away from other world-realms, and has been born here — that one listens to the exposition, teaching, full teaching, and oral transmission of this profound Perfection of Wisdom; and having heard this Perfection of Wisdom, that one generates the perception that it is one's own teacher who is being presented, and arises the thought, "My teacher has appeared directly before me; I am seeing my teacher." That one lends an ear to the exposition, teaching, full teaching, and oral transmission of this Perfection of Wisdom, listens with reverence, and does not interrupt the discourse. Śāriputra, know that such a bodhisattva, great being, is of just such a nature — one who has practiced for a long time and kept to the precepts. Śāriputra, know in just this way that such a bodhisattva, great being, has attended upon many buddhas.

Then the venerable Subhūti spoke these words to the Blessed One: Blessed One, is it possible to hear, realize, contemplate, give rise to, or take up this Perfection of Wisdom?

Can it be pointed out — "The Perfection of Wisdom is this," or "The Perfection of Wisdom is here," or "The Perfection of Wisdom is in such-and-such a place" — or can it be indicated or heard by means of this form, this sign, or this mark?

The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, no. This Perfection of Wisdom cannot be indicated in terms of the aggregates, the elements, or the sense bases, nor can it be heard, realized, contemplated, given rise to, or taken up. Why is that? Subhūti, because all phenomena are empty, and all phenomena are utterly remote — it is for that reason that the Perfection of Wisdom cannot be indicated, heard, realized, contemplated, given rise to, or taken up. Know further that the Perfection of Wisdom does not exist apart from the aggregates, elements, and sense bases. Why is that? Because the aggregates, elements, and sense bases themselves are empty, remote, and at peace. In that way, the Perfection of Wisdom on the one hand and the aggregates, elements, and sense bases on the other are not two and cannot be made into two. Because of emptiness, remoteness, and peace, it has no referential object; and that very absence of any referential object with respect to all phenomena is what is called the Perfection of Wisdom. When perception, full cognition, conceptual designation, and conventional expression do not arise — that is what is to be called the Perfection of Wisdom.

The elder Subhūti then asked: Blessed One, how long a time must a bodhisattva, a great being, who practices this profound Perfection of Wisdom be understood to have practiced and to have kept to the precepts?

The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, because there are gradations in the faculties of bodhisattvas, great beings, this must be analyzed and set forth in detail. Subhūti, there is also a way of reckoning by which bodhisattvas who have seen many hundreds of buddhas, many thousands of buddhas, and many hundreds of thousands of buddhas, and who have led the holy life in their presence, may nonetheless come to lack faith and devotion toward this Perfection of Wisdom. Why is that? In the past, with regard to those blessed buddhas, when this profound Perfection of Wisdom was being expounded, taught, and fully taught, they did not show respect; and because they showed no respect, they did not listen well; because they did not listen well, they did not pay homage; because they paid no homage, they did not inquire closely; because they did not inquire closely, they did not develop genuine faith; because they did not develop genuine faith, they strayed away from those assemblies. In that way, they acted, accumulated, fully accumulated, and closely accumulated karma concordant with being deprived of the Dharma in that domain. Even now, when this profound Perfection of Wisdom is being expounded, taught, and fully taught, they stray away without respect, without faith, and without devotion, and so they do not apply body and mind to gathering the accumulations. Because they do not gather the accumulations, they do not know, do not see, do not penetrate, and do not realize this Perfection of Wisdom. Thus they come to lack faith in the Perfection of Wisdom; lacking faith, they do not listen; not listening, they do not know; not knowing, they do not see; not seeing, they do not penetrate. And not having penetrated, they continually perform, fully engage in, and closely engage in karma concordant with being deprived of the Dharma.

By having performed, accumulated, fully accumulated, and closely accumulated karma concordant with being deprived of the Dharma, they will then engage in forming karma concordant with the derangement of wisdom. Having fully formed, accumulated, fully accumulated, and closely accumulated karma concordant with the derangement of wisdom, when this Perfection of Wisdom is expounded, taught, and closely taught, they will reject it, denigrate it, and speak ill of it — and having denigrated it, they will go astray.

O Subhūti, moreover, by rejecting, denigrating, and speaking ill of this Perfection of Wisdom, they will reject, denigrate, and speak ill of the omniscience of all the blessed buddhas of the past, the future, and the present. These beings, having broken and burned away their own stream of being, being persons of little intelligence, little wisdom, little merit, and few wholesome roots, possessed of mere faith, mere joy, mere clarity, mere aspiration — beginners, those whose nature lacks good fortune — they will also turn back, drive away, and reverse that mere faith, mere joy, and mere aspiration even of others, saying, "One should not train in this," and uttering the words, "This is not the speech of a buddha." In this way, having broken and severed their own stream of being and also broken and severed the streams of others, they will denigrate the Perfection of Wisdom. When the Perfection of Wisdom is denigrated, omniscience itself is denigrated. When omniscience is denigrated, all the blessed buddhas of the past, the future, and the present are denigrated. In this way they will go astray from the blessed buddhas, be utterly abandoned by the Dharma, and become utterly excluded from the Saṅgha. Thus, from every direction, in every manner, and in every respect they will become utterly excluded from the Three Jewels. And because they cut off benefit and happiness for sentient beings, they will accumulate karma concordant with ripening in the great hells.

By forming, establishing, rousing, and fully engaging in such karma — karma concordant with being deprived of the Dharma and concordant with the derangement of wisdom — by that very nature they will fall into the great hells for many hundreds of years, many thousands of years, many hundreds of thousands of years, many tens of millions of years, many tens of billions of years, many hundreds of billions of years, and many hundreds of trillions of years. They will pass from one great hell to another great hell. While they are thus transferring for such a long time from great hell to great hell, there will arise an aeon of destruction by fire. When an aeon of destruction by fire occurs, they will be cast into whatever great hells exist in other world-systems, and they will fall into those great hells. Having been cast there and fallen into those great hells, there too they will transfer from great hell to great hell. And while there too they are transferring from great hell to great hell, there too that same aeon of destruction by fire will arise once more.

When an aeon of destruction by fire arose, they passed away from there and were immediately cast into whatever great hells existed in other world-systems, and they were reborn in those great hells. There too, in exactly the same way, they transferred from great hell to great hell. While they were transferring there as well, for a very long time, from great hell to great hell, there too an aeon of destruction by fire came to occur. When that aeon of destruction by fire arose, they once again passed away from there, and by that very same karma — unexhausted and with its remainder intact — they were cast back and thrown into this world-system, and upon being so thrown, they were reborn once again in the great hells. There, once more in the great hells, they experienced those great sufferings of hell-beings — and they continued to experience them for as long as an aeon of destruction by fire would arise. In this way they experienced suffering befitting karma that had brought forth such great suffering. Why was this so? Because they had uttered such deeply harmful words.

Then the venerable Śāriputra addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, those who have committed and accumulated the five actions of immediate retribution — the image or likeness or even a faint semblance of such misdeeds of mind and misdeeds of speech does not exist even remotely."

The Blessed One declared: "It is so, Śāriputra, it is indeed so. Śāriputra, those who have committed and accumulated the five actions of immediate retribution have performed and heaped up and thoroughly accumulated and proximately accumulated misdeeds through mind and misdeeds through speech — and not even an image or likeness or faint semblance of this exists.

As for those who turn back and speak disparagingly and utter unpleasant things against those who teach and expound and explain this profound Perfection of Wisdom, saying 'One should not train in this,' and who say 'This was not taught by the Tathāgata,' and who also cause other sentient beings to be separated from it — such persons ruin their own stream and destroy the streams of others; they poison their own stream and poison the streams of others; they ruin themselves and ruin others. They themselves neither know nor comprehend this profound Perfection of Wisdom, and they lead others to hold the view that one should not train in it.

Śāriputra, I do not even grant permission to look upon persons of such a nature — what need is there to mention associating with them, or keeping their company, or receiving gains and honors from them? Why is that? Śāriputra, persons of such a nature should be understood as those who revile the Dharma. Śāriputra, persons of such a nature should be understood as those who have become like rotted leaves. Śāriputra, persons of such a nature should be understood as being dark in nature and as generating darkness. Śāriputra, all those who think to listen to such persons and to place faith in them will be ruined through what is not the proper way. Śāriputra, those who revile the Perfection of Wisdom should be understood, Śāriputra, as persons who revile the Dharma."

Śāriputra then asked: "Blessed One, please show the physical measure of a being born in such a state and dwelling in the great hells."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, set aside for now the measure of the body of a person born in that great hell. Why? Because upon hearing the measure of the body of a person of such a nature, one would vomit warm blood from one's mouth and die, or come close to death, or be touched and burned by unbearable suffering and sickness, or be pierced by the thorn of grief, or fall into a great abyss, or become withered and parched. Since it would be inappropriate for any of these harms to befall one, let it not come to be heard — that measure of such a body, so full of great terror, in which all those faults exist." The Blessed One did not open the occasion to tell the venerable Śāriputra, "The measure of that body is such and such."

A second and a third time the venerable Śāriputra addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, please explain to me how, once the measure of that person's body is made apparent to people of future generations, they will understand that the actions of speech and mind that have been performed, accumulated, heaped up, and fully amassed will produce a body in the great hells that is so vast and immense."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, this very fact — that one who has acted wrongly through speech and mind, who has fully formed and accumulated non-virtuous karma, heaped it up and amassed it fully, will experience suffering of such extent for so long a time — when made apparent to people of future generations, will cause those sons and daughters of good family who have the fortune of merit to feel dismay, and thereafter to become impoverished in Dharma. Turning back from those corresponding actions, they will do only one thing: accumulate merit. Thinking 'I too must not encounter suffering like that,' they will not, even for the sake of their life, abandon the true Dharma."

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, a son or daughter of good family should be extremely restrained in the actions of body, speech, and mind. Why? Because, Blessed One, it is through speaking such wrongful words as these that this great mass of demerit has arisen. Blessed One, through what action did this great mass of demerit arise?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, it is through speaking wrongful words of that nature that this great mass of demerit arose. Subhūti, those deluded persons who would think to revile this profound Perfection of Wisdom, who would think to deprecate it, who would think to reject it — they will go forth into renunciation within this very well-proclaimed Dharma and Vinaya. If this Perfection of Wisdom is discarded, the awakening of the Blessed Buddhas will also be discarded. If the awakening of the buddhas is discarded, the omniscience of the Blessed Buddhas of the past, the future, and the present will also be discarded. If omniscience is discarded, the true Dharma will be discarded. If the true Dharma is discarded, the Saṅgha of the Tathāgata's śrāvakas will also be discarded."

When the Saṅgha of the Tathāgata's śrāvakas has been discarded in this way, one becomes utterly estranged from the Three Jewels in every respect, from every side, and in every dimension — and thereby one fully takes up and embraces an immeasurably vast and incalculably great unwholesome action.

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, what is the cause, what is the condition, that a son or daughter of good family would think to deprecate this Perfection of Wisdom?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that son or daughter of good family is either blessed by Māra, or, through verbal actions conducive to the corruption of wisdom, they come to lack faith in and fail to delight in these profound teachings. Subhūti, it is through being endowed with these two evil qualities that such a son or daughter of good family comes to deprecate this Perfection of Wisdom.

"Furthermore, Subhūti, whoever would think to deprecate this profound Perfection of Wisdom when it is being explained, taught, or expounded — that person will be endowed with these four qualities: that son or daughter of good family will either have fallen into the hands of an evil friend, or will be lacking in diligence, or will be attached to the aggregates, or will be one who praises themselves and disparages others, seeking out occasions for fault."

This concludes the seventh chapter of the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, called "The Chapter on Hell."

The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The Ninth Fascicle.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is difficult to have faith in for those lacking in diligence, those devoid of roots of virtue, and those who have fallen into the hands of evil friends."

The Blessed One declared: "So it is, Subhūti, so it is. Subhūti, the Perfection of Wisdom is difficult to have faith in for those lacking in diligence, those with meager roots of virtue, those of weak intelligence, those who do not seek its meaning, those of little learning, those of feeble wisdom, those firmly held by evil friends, those who do not listen well, those of a nature not to inquire, and those who make no effort in virtuous qualities.

Subhūti then asked: "Blessed One — how wonderful! — how very profound then is the Perfection of Wisdom, that it is so difficult to have faith in?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, form is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because form has no essential nature, form is neither bound nor liberated. Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because consciousness has no essential nature, consciousness is neither bound nor liberated.

"Subhūti, the past limit of form is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because the past limit of form has no essential nature. Subhūti, the future limit of form is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because the future limit of form has no essential nature. Subhūti, the present arising of form is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because the present arising of form has no essential nature.

"Subhūti, likewise the past limit of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because the past limit of consciousness has no essential nature."

Subhūti, the future limit of consciousness is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because the future limit of consciousness has no essential nature. Subhūti, the present arising of consciousness is neither bound nor liberated. Why is that? Subhūti, because the present arising of consciousness has no essential nature.

Then Subhūti addressed the Blessed One and said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is difficult to have faith in and difficult to have supreme faith in for those who make no effort, who have not accumulated roots of virtue, who have fallen into the hands of evil companions, who have come under the power of Māra, who are lazy, whose diligence is weak, whose mindfulness has declined, and whose wisdom is confused."

The Blessed One declared: "So it is, Subhūti, so it is. Subhūti, the Perfection of Wisdom is difficult to have faith in for those who make no effort, who have not accumulated roots of virtue, who have fallen into the hands of evil companions, who have come under the power of Māra, who are lazy, whose diligence is weak, whose mindfulness has declined, and whose wisdom is confused. Subhūti, the Perfection of Wisdom is difficult to have supreme faith in. Why is that? Subhūti, whatever is the purity of form, that is the purity of the fruit; and whatever is the purity of the fruit, that is the purity of form. Subhūti, thus the purity of form and the purity of the fruit are non-dual, cannot be made into two, are not distinct, and are not divided. Subhūti, thus by the purity of the fruit, form is pure; and by the purity of form, the fruit is pure. Subhūti, likewise whatever is the purity of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, that is the purity of the fruit; and whatever is the purity of the fruit, that is the purity of consciousness. Subhūti, thus the purity of consciousness and the purity of the fruit are non-dual, cannot be made into two, are not distinct, and are not divided. Subhūti, thus by the purity of the fruit, consciousness is pure; and by the purity of consciousness, the fruit is pure.

"Subhūti, furthermore, whatever is the purity of form, that is the purity of omniscience; and whatever is the purity of omniscience, that is the purity of form. Subhūti, thus the purity of form and the purity of omniscience are non-dual, cannot be made into two, are not distinct, and are not divided. Subhūti, thus by the purity of omniscience, form is pure; and by the purity of form, omniscience is pure. Subhūti, likewise whatever is the purity of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, that is the purity of omniscience; and whatever is the purity of omniscience, that is the purity of consciousness. Subhūti, thus the purity of consciousness and the purity of omniscience are non-dual, cannot be made into two, are not distinct, and are not divided. Subhūti, thus by the purity of omniscience, consciousness is pure; and by the purity of consciousness, omniscience is pure."

Then the venerable Śāriputra addressed the Blessed One and said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom illuminates."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said:

"Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom illuminates."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is without junction or connection."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is without any affliction whatsoever."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is without attainment and without realization."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is without full accomplishment."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom has no arising whatsoever in the desire realm, the form realm, or the formless realm."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom neither seeks nor truly seeks."

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, that is because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, what then does the Perfection of Wisdom neither seek nor truly seek?"

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, the Perfection of Wisdom neither knows nor truly knows form. And why is that? Śāriputra, because of purity. Śāriputra, in the same way, the Perfection of Wisdom neither knows nor truly knows feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness. And why is that? Because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom neither harms nor benefits omniscience."

The Blessed One declared: "Because of purity."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom neither fully appropriates nor fully relinquishes any phenomenon whatsoever."

The Blessed One declared: "Because of purity."

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One and said: "Blessed One, by the purity of self, form is pure."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, by the purity of self, feeling, perception, and formations are pure. Blessed One, by the purity of self, consciousness is pure."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, by the purity of self, result is pure."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, by the purity of self, omniscience is pure."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, by the purity of self, there is neither attainment nor realization."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said:

"Blessed One, by the limitlessness of self, form is limitless."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, by the limitlessness of self, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are limitless."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, whatever realization a bodhisattva, a great being, has in that way — that is this Perfection of Wisdom."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

The venerable Subhūti said: "Blessed One, moreover, this Perfection of Wisdom does not abide on the near shore, does not abide on the far shore, and does not abide in the vast expanse between the two."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of utter purity."

The venerable Subhūti said: "Blessed One, if one were to perceive it as being so, that bodhisattva, that great being, would abandon this Perfection of Wisdom. One would make this Perfection of Wisdom into something distant."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, excellent, excellent! It is just so, Subhūti, it is just so. And why? Because, Subhūti, one would become attached to the name, and one would become attached to the sign."

When this was declared, the venerable Subhūti spoke to the Blessed One as follows: "Blessed One, in the manner in which the Blessed One has explained these very attachments, how excellently has this Perfection of Wisdom been spoken and how excellently shown — that it reaches the utterly final end is wondrous."

Then the venerable Śāriputra said to the venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, what are those attachments?"

Subhūti replied: "Venerable Śāriputra, the thought that 'form is empty' — that is an attachment. Likewise, venerable Śāriputra, the thought that 'feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are empty' — that is an attachment. To know past phenomena correctly as past phenomena — that is an attachment. To know future phenomena correctly as future phenomena — that is an attachment. To know present phenomena correctly as present phenomena — that is an attachment. To know correctly that a person who has set out on the bodhisattva vehicle and who has first aroused the mind produces such and such an accumulation of merit — that is an attachment."

Then Śakra, lord of the gods (Indra), said to the venerable Subhūti: "Noble Subhūti, through what categories does one become attached?"

Subhūti replied: "Kauśika, if one correctly knows that this is the first arising of the awakening mind, and dedicates that awakening mind to unsurpassable, perfectly complete awakening — a son or daughter of good family who has genuinely entered the Great Vehicle cannot dedicate the intrinsic nature of mind. Therefore, Kauśika, one who correctly teaches others, correctly establishes them, correctly encourages them, and correctly inspires them in delight, through the correct understanding of unsurpassable, perfectly complete awakening itself — such a one should correctly teach, correctly establish, correctly encourage, and correctly inspire in delight.

In this way, such a son or daughter of good family does no harm to themselves, and through the correct establishment that has been granted by the buddhas they correctly establish others, and all these extremes of attachment are thoroughly abandoned.

Then the Blessed One spoke words of praise to the venerable Subhūti, saying: "Excellent! Subhūti, it is excellent, excellent, that you thus make bodhisattvas, great beings, understand and take to heart these extremes of attachment. Therefore, Subhūti, yet other subtle forms of attachment are to be explained. Listen well and hold it in mind, for I shall explain it to you."

"Excellent!" replied the Blessed One — and the venerable Subhūti listened attentively to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One spoke as follows:

"Subhūti, in this regard, when faithful sons or daughters of good family direct their minds toward the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete buddha, as a characteristic mark, then, Subhūti, for as long as that characteristic mark persists, there is attachment. Why is that? Because from a characteristic mark, attachment arises. Therefore, one rejoices in whatever undefiled qualities belong to the blessed buddhas of the past, the future, and the present. Having thus rejoiced, and then dedicating the roots of virtue that come with that rejoicing to unsurpassable, perfectly complete awakening — Subhūti, the nature of reality of all phenomena is neither past, nor future, nor present. That which is neither past, nor future, nor present is definitively liberated from the three times. That which is definitively liberated from the three times cannot be dedicated, cannot be made into a characteristic mark, cannot be made into an object of focus, nor is it something seen, heard, cognized, or known."

Subhūti asked: "Blessed One, the intrinsic nature of phenomena is profound."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is because of its solitariness."

Subhūti asked: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound in its intrinsic nature."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, because its intrinsic nature is utterly pure, and because its intrinsic nature is solitary, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound in its intrinsic nature."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, because the Perfection of Wisdom is solitary in its intrinsic nature, I pay homage to the Perfection of Wisdom, Blessed One."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, all phenomena too are precisely solitary in intrinsic nature. Subhūti, that which is the solitariness of the intrinsic nature of all phenomena — that is the Perfection of Wisdom. Why is that? Subhūti, it is because all phenomena have been perfectly and completely awakened to by the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete buddha, without having been made."

Subhūti said: "Blessed One, if that is so, then the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete buddha, has not perfectly and completely awakened to all phenomena."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, it is so — there are no phenomena whatsoever that exist by way of intrinsic nature.
