Subhūti, consider this further analogy: a person seeking precious jewels sees a great ocean. Without examining, considering, or entering into it for those jewels, he instead thinks to search for the source of precious jewels in a puddle left by a cow's hoofprint, and imagines that a cow's hoofprint might equal the great ocean. Subhūti, what do you think — is that person to be recognized as one of wise character? Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, he is not." The Blessed One declared: "In just the same way, Subhūti, a person belonging to the bodhisattva vehicle who, even having found this Perfection of Wisdom, fails to realize and know it and discards it, and who, desiring to abide with little effort in sūtras that explicitly teach the ground of the śrāvakas and those that explicitly teach the ground of the pratyekabuddhas, intends to seek them out — such a one should be recognized as being of such a disposition."

Wherever the bodhisattva vehicle is not proclaimed —

"I alone will tame myself, I alone will pacify myself, and I alone will thus pass into nirvāṇa" — thinking in this way, abiding inwardly, one should attain the fruit of stream-entry, the fruit of once-returning, the fruit of non-returning, and the fruit of arhatship; one should attain solitary awakening; and in this very life, having fully liberated the mind from defilements without clinging, one should pass into nirvāṇa. Whoever speaks thus is called one who belongs to the ground of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Therefore a bodhisattva, a great being, should not generate the intention in that way.

Why is that? Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, has properly entered the Great Vehicle and has donned the great armor. Such beings never generate their intention toward what is of little aspiration. Why is that? These noble persons are guides of the world and servants of the world's welfare. Therefore they should always train well, without ceasing, in the six perfections (pāramitās).

Moreover, Subhūti, those persons belonging to the bodhisattva vehicle whose roots of virtue are not yet matured, who are of inferior intelligence and weak superior intention, who neither know nor have penetrated the sūtras endowed with the six perfections — having abandoned and discarded this Perfection of Wisdom, they intend to seek out those sūtras that explicitly teach the grounds of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Subhūti, this conduct of persons belonging to the bodhisattva vehicle who are of such a character should be recognized as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, consider this analogy. A carpenter or a carpenter's apprentice who wishes to build and construct a house to the measure of a royal palace goes in search of the dimensions and proportions of the palace of the sun and moon. In searching for that, he comes upon the palace of the sun and moon and thinks to take its measurements. What do you think, Subhūti? Would one who wishes to build and construct a house to the measure of a royal palace do well to take the measurements of the palace of the sun and moon? Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "In just the same way, Subhūti, certain persons belonging to the bodhisattva vehicle who will appear in future times — hearing this Perfection of Wisdom, finding this Perfection of Wisdom, then abandoning this Perfection of Wisdom and casting it aside — will think to seek out omniscience from sūtras that belong to the ground of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, from sūtras that explicitly teach: 'I alone should tame myself; I alone should pacify myself; I alone should pass into nirvāṇa' — sūtras that, in this manner, teach the taming, pacifying, and attaining of nirvāṇa for oneself alone. If they think to seek out such sūtras and to train accordingly, then, Subhūti, what do you think? Should those bodhisattvas also be recognized as being of wise character?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, this too should be recognized as the work of Māra in their regard."

Subhūti, consider this further analogy. A certain person who wishes to see a wheel-turning king (cakravartin) comes to see such a king. Having seen him, he fixes in mind the marks of that wheel-turning king's complexion, form, majesty, and miraculous powers — 'this is what they are like.' Then, when he sees a regional king, he takes note of that regional king's complexion, form, majesty, miraculous powers, and marks,

and yet, being unable to distinguish between the two, he says: "That wheel-turning king is exactly like this — the same in complexion, form, majesty, miraculous powers, and marks." Subhūti, what do you think? Should that person, who imagines a wheel-turning king to be equal to a regional king, be recognized as being of wise character? Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, in the same way, those persons of the bodhisattva vehicle who will appear in the future — who hear this Perfection of Wisdom, and who, having obtained this Perfection of Wisdom, then neglect the Perfection of Wisdom, abandon the Perfection of Wisdom, set this Perfection of Wisdom aside at a distance, and instead seek omniscience from sūtras belonging to the level of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas — Subhūti, this too should be recognized as the work of Māra in their regard.

"Subhūti, furthermore, I do not say that great bodhisattvas should seek omniscience from these sūtras belonging to śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Subhūti, nevertheless, without training in the skillful means of great bodhisattvas that the Tathāgata has taught within the Perfection of Wisdom, no great bodhisattva will come to definitely attain unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. And why is that? In other sūtras, the complete fulfillment of a bodhisattva is difficult. Therefore, Subhūti, because the Tathāgata sees clearly these qualities of the Perfection of Wisdom, he teaches this Perfection of Wisdom to great bodhisattvas in many ways — presenting it rightly, setting it forth rightly, extolling it rightly, causing them to rejoice in it rightly, leading them into it rightly, and establishing them in it rightly. When this is done, great bodhisattvas will become irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.

"Subhūti, what do you think? Those who have obtained and accomplished the irreversible vehicle, the Great Vehicle — if they were to abandon it and instead seek the lesser vehicle, would those bodhisattvas be of wise character?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, consider this analogy. A certain hungry person finds a food of a hundred flavors — a food that is refined, beneficial, refined, pleasant, and that dispels hunger and thirst for the whole duration of life. He abandons it, and instead seeks and finds a sixty-day-old gruel. Having found this sixty-day-old gruel, he abandons the food of a hundred flavors and intends to eat that sixty-day-old gruel. Subhūti, what do you think? Is that person of wise character?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, in just the same way, some bodhisattvas who will appear in the future — who hear this Perfection of Wisdom and, having obtained this Perfection of Wisdom, then neglect the Perfection of Wisdom, abandon the Perfection of Wisdom, set this Perfection of Wisdom aside at a distance, and instead seek sūtras belonging to the vehicle of śrāvakas and the vehicle of pratyekabuddhas —

those sūtras which explicitly reveal the ground of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas — and who then think to seek omniscience and skillful means from them: Subhūti, what do you think? Should those bodhisattvas also be understood to be of wise character?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, this too should be understood as the work of Māra for those bodhisattvas.

Subhūti, here is an analogy. Suppose a certain person finds a priceless, precious jewel and then thinks to make it equivalent to a precious jewel of small value and little worth. Subhūti, what do you think? Should that person be understood to be of wise character?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, in just the same way, some persons of the bodhisattva vehicle who will appear in the future — who hear and obtain this profound, luminous, precious Perfection of Wisdom — and then think to make it equivalent to the vehicle of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, and think to seek omniscience and skillful means from the ground of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas: Subhūti, what do you think? Should those bodhisattvas also be understood to be of wise character?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, indeed not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, this too should be understood as the work of Māra for those bodhisattvas.

Subhūti, furthermore, when this profound Perfection of Wisdom is being explained, taught, expounded, held, retained, read, recited, chanted aloud, or being copied down in letters even by a young child, there will arise many an impulse that will cause someone's mind to be distracted. Subhūti, this too should be understood as the work of Māra for those bodhisattvas.

When this had been declared, the venerable Subhūti then said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, can the Perfection of Wisdom be written down in letters?" The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, it cannot. Subhūti, whoever writes the Perfection of Wisdom letter by letter, and then thinks either 'I have written the Perfection of Wisdom' or 'There is no Perfection of Wisdom,' or becomes attached to the letters as the Perfection of Wisdom, or thinks 'Without the letters there is no Perfection of Wisdom' — Subhūti, this too should be understood as the work of Māra for those bodhisattvas.

Subhūti, furthermore, when one is copying the Perfection of Wisdom in letters, mental engagements with objects will arise: mental engagements with villages, towns, hamlets, regions, surrounding districts, and royal palaces will arise; mental engagements with pleasure gardens will arise; mental engagements with teachers will arise; mental engagements with preceptors will arise; mental engagements with thieves will arise; mental engagements with tigers will arise; mental engagements with Viśikhā will arise; mental engagements with Śibikā will arise; mental engagements with prostitutes will arise; mental engagements with pleasure, with suffering, and with fear will arise; mental engagements with women, with men, and with those of indeterminate sex will arise; mental engagements with those who are beloved and those who are not will arise; mental engagements with one's father and mother will arise.

Mental engagements with brothers and sisters will arise; mental engagements with kinsmen and friends will arise; mental engagements with companions and with relatives on the paternal and maternal sides will arise; mental engagements with one's wife, sons, and daughters will arise; mental engagements with one's home, food, and drink will arise; mental engagements with clothing will arise; mental engagements with bedding will arise; mental engagements with the necessities of livelihood will arise; mental engagements with immediate tasks will arise; mental engagements with attachment, with aversion, and with delusion will arise; mental engagements with auspicious times, with good times, and with bad times will arise; mental engagements with songs, with musical instruments, and with dance will arise; mental engagements with poetry, with song lyrics, and with stories will arise; mental engagements with treatises will arise; mental engagements with conventional conversation will arise; mental engagements with laughter will arise; mental engagements with playfulness and coquetry will arise; mental engagements with sorrow will arise; mental engagements with affliction will arise; mental engagements with self will arise. Subhūti, all these mental engagements and others besides will be brought about by Māra the Evil One, so as to create obstacles for those who expound this Perfection of Wisdom, who teach it, who explain it in detail, who hold it, who preserve it, who read it, who recite it aloud, who chant it by heart, and who, even as a child, copy it in writing — he will create obstacles for them and cause their minds to be distracted. Therefore a bodhisattva, a great being, must recognize all these works of Māra, and having recognized them, must abandon them.

Furthermore, Subhūti, mental engagements with kings will arise; mental engagements with young men will arise; mental engagements with elephants will arise; mental engagements with horses will arise; mental engagements with chariots will arise; mental engagements with watching wheel-spinning jugglery with the hands will arise. Subhūti, these too are to be recognized by those bodhisattvas, those great beings, as works of Māra.

Furthermore, Subhūti, mental engagements with fire will arise; mental engagements with sensual desire will arise; mental engagements with an abundance of wealth and grain will arise. Subhūti, these too are to be recognized by the bodhisattva, the great being, as works of Māra.

Furthermore, Subhūti, for those bodhisattvas, those great beings, who expound this Perfection of Wisdom, who teach it, who explain it in detail, who recite it aloud, who chant it by heart, and who copy it in writing even as a child — Māra the Evil One will come in the guise of a monk and will offer them sūtra collections of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, those that are profound and deeply profound, saying: "Study this. Write this down. Receive the transmission of this. Recite this by heart. From this you will attain omniscience." Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, who is skilled in means will not generate delight in those sūtras. Why is that? Subhūti, although emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness are indeed taught in those sūtra collections,

yet the skillful means of bodhisattvas, great beings, were not taught in them. And those bodhisattvas who do not clearly know the particular gnosis of skillful means will form the intention to abandon this profound Perfection of Wisdom. Having abandoned this profound Perfection of Wisdom, they will form the intention to seek skillful means from those sūtra collections belonging to the vehicle of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when those who wish to hear the Dharma are endowed with aspiration and wish to take up the Perfection of Wisdom, and yet the Dharma-speaker becomes indolent and is unwilling to teach the Dharma — Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too, because of the discord in the assembly, as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when the Dharma-speaker is free of indolence and wishes to impart the Perfection of Wisdom, and yet those who wish to hear the Dharma become indolent and are burdened with many activities — Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too, because of the discord in the assembly, as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when those who wish to hear the Dharma are endowed with aspiration, and wish to take up, to retain, to read, to master, to recite aloud, and even to copy in writing the Perfection of Wisdom, and they are endowed with understanding, with intelligence, and with mindfulness — and yet the Dharma-speaker travels away to another region, and what he explains is either not comprehended when first stated, or is elaborated upon in such a way that it is still not comprehended, or he simply does not know it clearly — Subhūti, this too will cause discord for one who expounds, teaches, fully teaches, transmits by oral tradition, recites by heart, instructs in, and as a child copies in writing this Perfection of Wisdom. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when the Dharma-speaker is free of indolence and has clear understanding of the meaning and wishes to impart this Perfection of Wisdom and to have it read — and yet those who wish to hear the Dharma travel away to another region, and what they are told is either not comprehended when first stated, or is elaborated upon in such a way that it is still not comprehended, or they simply do not understand it clearly — Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too, because of the discord in the assembly, as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when the Dharma-speaker is intent on material gain, intent on receiving offerings and veneration, and intent on robes — and yet those who wish to hear the Dharma have few desires, are content, are deeply secluded, or have no wish to offer wealth — Subhūti, this too will cause discord for one who trains in and copies this Perfection of Wisdom. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when those who wish to hear the Dharma are endowed with faith, wish to hear this Perfection of Wisdom, wish to absorb its meaning, and wish to give up their wealth — and yet the Dharma-speaker either lacks faith, or has no wish to receive wealth, or has little desire, or has no wish to speak — Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too, because of the lack of harmony in the assembly, as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when those who wish to hear the Dharma are endowed with faith and wish to hear and to absorb the meaning — and yet obstacles to the Dharma arise for the Dharma-speaker such that those sūtra collections are unavailable or cannot be obtained — Subhūti, from this situation too, the Dharma-speaker who cannot find listeners will declare those listeners to be adversarial.

Subhūti, furthermore, one who has heard the Perfection of Wisdom and who wishes to retain it, uphold it, read it, or write it down may find themselves without the support of an assembly. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when the Dharma-speaker wishes to teach and the Dharma-listener does not wish to listen — one who would retain, uphold, read, or write down the Perfection of Wisdom after hearing it may find themselves without the support of an assembly. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when the Dharma-listener becomes overwhelmed by sleep and heaviness of body, and being thus afflicted by drowsiness and bodily sluggishness has no wish to listen, while the Dharma-speaker does wish to teach — Subhūti, this too is a lack of harmony in the assembly, and a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize it as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when the Dharma-speaker becomes overwhelmed by sleep and heaviness of body, and being thus afflicted by drowsiness and bodily sluggishness has no wish to teach, while the Dharma-listener does wish to listen — Subhūti, this too constitutes a lack of harmony for one who would write, read, and seek to master the text, and a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize it as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when someone comes to a place where the Perfection of Wisdom is being written, taught, or studied, and there speaks of the miseries of hell-beings, speaks of the miseries of the animal realm, speaks of the miseries of the realm of hungry ghosts, and speaks of the miseries of the bodies of asuras — saying: "Hell-beings suffer in such-and-such ways; the animal realm involves suffering of such-and-such kinds; the realm of hungry ghosts involves suffering of such-and-such kinds; the realm of asuras involves suffering of such-and-such kinds; all conditioned formations are suffering in such-and-such ways — therefore, right here and now, let suffering be utterly uprooted" — Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too, through the lack of harmony in the assembly, as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, furthermore, when someone comes to a place where the Perfection of Wisdom is being written, taught, or studied, and there speaks in praise of the gods — saying: "The gods enjoy such-and-such happiness; the higher realms are happy in such-and-such ways; in the desire realm, sense pleasures are to be enjoyed in such-and-such ways; in the form realm, one enters into the dhyānas in such-and-such meditative equipoise; in the formless absorptions, one rests in such-and-such equanimity; and yet even these, when examined with wisdom, show that all births are suffering — for the Blessed One himself has said: 'Monks, I do not teach the praises of even the slightest achievement in the realms of existence. All conditioned phenomena are impermanent; everything that has gone into existence is suffering; everything in the three realms is empty; all phenomena are without self. Therefore, knowing all those things to be unstable, impermanent, suffering, and subject to change, the wise should attain, right here and now, the fruit of stream-entry, the fruit of once-returning, the fruit of non-returning, and should fully attain arhatship in this very life — let us not encounter again those many sufferings of misfortune and decline'" — and if some bodhisattvas become disheartened upon hearing this, Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize this too, through the lack of harmony in the assembly, as the work of Māra.

Furthermore, Subhūti, whatever monk is a Dharma-teacher will come to delight in solitude alone, while those who are listeners of the Dharma will defer to the assembly. And that Dharma-teacher will say: "To those who attend upon me I will give this Perfection of Wisdom; to those who do not attend upon me I will not give it." Those sons of good family and daughters of good family, though they seek, desire, and with reverence for the Dharma attend upon that Dharma-teacher, will find no opportunity opened to them. And that Dharma-teacher, desiring some slight material gain yet unwilling to give even that, will set out for whatever place is stricken by famine, where there is no prosperity or well-being, and where there is danger to life. Those listeners of the Dharma, upon hearing from others that famine has broken out in that region, that there is no prosperity or well-being there, and that there is also danger to life in that direction, will recognize this. And that Dharma-teacher will say to those sons of good family: "Sons of good family, as for this region here — if a fear of famine should arise, if you were to go there, you might later come to regret it out of fear of famine." In this way the Dharma-teacher, through subtle means, causes them to turn away. They too become disheartened and understand: "These are the signs of one who drives away, not the signs of one who desires to give. This one does not wish to give." Recognizing this, they will cease to attend upon him.

Subhūti, this too — the questioning, studying, teaching, explaining, reciting, and chanting of this Perfection of Wisdom — will come to be attended by disharmony in the assembly. Subhūti, this too a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize as the work of Māra.

Furthermore, Subhūti, that Dharma-teacher will make his way to a place where there is fear of living creatures, where there is fear of beasts of prey, where there is fear from non-humans — going and dwelling there, in a wilderness of beasts of prey, a wilderness of venomous snakes, a wilderness of bandits, a wilderness of water, a wilderness of famine. And he will say to those listeners of the Dharma: "Sons of good family, know this: I am going to a region where there is fear of living creatures, fear of beasts of prey, fear of carnivores, a wilderness of venomous snakes, a wilderness of bandits, a wilderness of water, a wilderness of famine. Sons of good family, know this — are you capable of enduring these sufferings?" And through this subtle means he causes them to turn away. Then they too become disheartened. Being disheartened, they will not attend upon him and will withdraw.

Subhūti, in the reciting, chanting, and writing down of the Perfection of Wisdom, these obstacles will arise. Subhūti, this too a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognize as the work of Māra, since it results in disharmony in the assembly.

Furthermore, Subhūti, a monk who is a Dharma-teacher will devote himself to the homes of friends and the homes of those who give alms. By thus devoting himself to the homes of friends and to homes where alms are given, he constantly thinks of going nearby to look in upon the homes of friends and on homes where alms are given. And because of the many activities that come from always looking thus, he will neglect those listeners of the Dharma, thinking: "Just now I have a little errand to attend to."

"I have a little errand to attend to." Subhūti, this too will result in disharmony with respect to the writing out and mastering of the Perfection of Wisdom. Subhūti, this too the great bodhisattvas should recognize as the work of Māra, since it results in disharmony, and having recognized it, they should abandon it.

Subhūti, in this way the wicked Māra strives by every means and with great effort to ensure that no one whatsoever takes up, retains, reads, masters, recites aloud, teaches, explains at length, chants, recites by heart, copies out in writing, or has others copy out this Perfection of Wisdom — striving in all those various ways to bring that about.

Subhūti, therefore all the obstacles that arise causing disharmony — every single one of them — should be recognized by the great bodhisattvas as the work of Māra, and having recognized them, they should abandon them.

When this was said, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, for what reason does the wicked Māra strive by every means and with such great effort to ensure that no one whatsoever takes up, retains, reads, masters, recites aloud, teaches, explains at length, chants, recites by heart, copies out in writing, or has others copy out this Perfection of Wisdom?"

When this was said, the Blessed One replied to the venerable Subhūti as follows: "Subhūti, the omniscience of the buddhas, the Blessed Ones, is born from the Perfection of Wisdom. The teaching of the Tathāgata arises from omniscience. From the teaching of the Tathāgata arises the abandonment of the afflictions of immeasurable, incalculable, and inconceivable sentient beings. When afflictions are abandoned, the wicked Māra finds no opportunity. Finding none, he is tormented and made miserable by suffering, and is pierced to the core by the arrow of grief.

For this reason, he harbors intense displeasure toward those who copy out the Perfection of Wisdom, master it, or study it, and he strives with great effort and exertion by every means to ensure that no one whatsoever copies out, masters, or studies this Perfection of Wisdom, working in all those ways toward that end.

Subhūti, furthermore, the wicked Māra comes in the guise of a śramaṇa to cause divisions. He causes those sons of good family who are newly entered upon the vehicle, who are of lesser intelligence, weaker intelligence, and inferior intelligence, who are immature, and who have not yet received a prophecy of unsurpassed and perfect awakening — those bodhisattvas newly entered upon the vehicle — to be separated from it by saying: 'Venerable friend, what you are listening to is not the Perfection of Wisdom. Now, what is included in and belongs to my sūtras — that is the Perfection of Wisdom.' In this way, Subhūti, the wicked Māra causes them to give rise to doubt and leads them to rejection.

And so, Subhūti, the wicked Māra, having come in the guise of a śramaṇa and made this division, causes those bodhisattvas newly entered upon the vehicle — of lesser, weaker, and inferior intelligence, who are immature and have not yet received a prophecy of unsurpassed and perfect awakening — to fall into doubt."

Having fallen into doubt, they will neither take up, retain, recite, master, intone aloud, teach, explain in detail, transmit as scripture, chant, write down, nor, while young, apply themselves to writing this Perfection of Wisdom. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognise this too as the work of Māra.

Furthermore, Subhūti, the wicked Māra, having transformed himself into a monk and come in the appearance of a buddha, says to whatever bodhisattva is engaged in these profound teachings: "This one is realising the ultimate limit — he will become a śrāvaka, not a bodhisattva. For example, a bodhisattva is just like this." In this way he employs the work of Māra. Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, should recognise this too as the work of Māra.

Subhūti, when someone is writing down this Perfection of Wisdom, mastering it, or studying it, many other and varied works of the wicked Māra will arise. A bodhisattva, a great being, should recognise them, and having recognised them, utterly abandon them and not rely upon them. Instead, one should apply diligent effort and be ever possessed of mindfulness and vigilance.

When these words had been spoken, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, it is indeed so; Sugata, it is indeed so. Great and precious jewels — those have many enemies. And why? Blessed One, it is because they are rare and valuable, and because they are wealth. It is on account of that that they have many enemies. In the same way, Blessed One, many obstacles will arise with respect to this Perfection of Wisdom as well. Blessed One, those who become lazy on account of those who create obstacles — those bodhisattvas who intend not to take up, retain, recite, master, intone aloud, teach, explain in detail, transmit as scripture, chant, write down, or, while young, to apply themselves to writing this Perfection of Wisdom — those bodhisattvas, Blessed One, are ones blessed by Māra. Blessed One, they are ones newly entered upon the vehicle. They are of lesser intelligence, of inferior intelligence, of weak intelligence. Blessed One, their understanding is inverted, and their minds, I know, do not enter into the vast and expansive teachings."

When this was said, the Blessed One spoke these words to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, it is indeed so, it is indeed so. Those bodhisattvas who intend not to take up, retain, recite, master, intone aloud, teach, explain in detail, transmit as scripture, chant, write down, or, while young, to apply themselves to writing this Perfection of Wisdom — Subhūti, know those bodhisattvas to be ones blessed by Māra. Subhūti, those bodhisattvas are newly entered upon the vehicle. They are ones of lesser intelligence. They are ones of inferior intelligence. They are ones of weak intelligence."

"Subhūti, those are ones of inverted intelligence. They will not direct their minds toward the vast, immeasurable Dharma teachings. Subhūti, in just this way, the works of Māra will arise, and many of Māra's faults and obstacles will appear. Yet even so, Subhūti, those who intend to take up, retain, recite, master, intone aloud, teach, explain in detail, transmit as scripture, chant, write down, or, while young, to apply themselves to writing this Perfection of Wisdom — Subhūti, know that they do all this through the power of the buddhas, the blessing of the buddhas, and the embrace of the buddhas. Why is that? Because the evil Māra strives with great effort to create obstacles to this, and the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened buddha likewise strives with great effort to embrace and protect it."

This concludes the eleventh chapter of the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, the chapter on the works of Māra.

The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, the twelfth fascicle.

Then once again the Blessed One spoke these words to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, consider this example. There is a certain woman who has many children — five, or ten, or twenty, or thirty, or forty, or fifty, or a hundred, or a thousand, or a hundred thousand. When she becomes the mother of all of them, those children strive and make every effort so that no harm may come to their mother's life, that their mother may live long, that their mother's body may not become unwell, that their mother may remain at ease for a long time, that no misfortune may befall their mother, that no feeling of suffering may arise in their mother, that she may lack nothing she needs to know, and that nothing unpleasant may arise in her body. Why? Because they think: 'She bore us, she endured hardship for us, she gave us our lives, she is the one who shows us the world.' So those children attend their mother with every form of care and comfort, wishing that no sensation of suffering or painful contact whatsoever should arise in her. And if it should occur that wind, bile, phlegm, a combination of these, an insect, a flesh-worm, a venomous snake, a human being, a non-human being, a flowing sickness, a wasting disease, a sudden fright, or something unwanted should arise and afflict the eyes, ears, nose, tongue, body, or mind of their mother — then those children think of their mother in just that way, and with every form of care and comfort they tend to her, cherish her, nurture her, thinking: 'This one who bore us and brought us joy, this one who gave us our lives and shows us the world.' They tend her, cherish her, nurture her, and make her their own. Subhūti, in just the same way, the Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas, look upon this Perfection of Wisdom with deep regard, and all those who write it down, take it up, retain it, recite it, master it, intone it aloud, teach it, explain it in detail, transmit it as scripture, or chant it — all of these they bring about through the power, the blessing, and the intent of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas."

In other world-systems, however many Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas there are who dwell and live and abide at this very time out of compassion — compassion for the benefit of the many, for the happiness of the many, out of loving concern for the world, for the welfare, benefit, and happiness of the great multitude, out of compassionate care for gods, humans, and all sentient beings — every one of them, too, looks with deep regard upon this very Perfection of Wisdom, and acts to ensure that this Perfection of Wisdom will endure for a long time, that the very name of this Perfection of Wisdom will not be destroyed, and that māras of evil disposition or gods belonging to the hosts of Māra will not be able to create obstacles for those who teach this Perfection of Wisdom, write it down in letters, or study it. In just this way, Subhūti, the Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas cherish this Perfection of Wisdom, make it their own, and guard it. Why? Because this is the mother of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas — she who gives birth to them, she who reveals omniscience, she who reveals it throughout all the world.

Subhūti, the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas are born from this. Subhūti, the Perfection of Wisdom is that which generates and reveals the gnosis of omniscience in the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas — and thus reveals it throughout this entire world.

Subhūti, the omniscience of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas is born from this.

Subhūti, all those Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas who in times past manifestly and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening — every one of them did so in dependence upon this very Perfection of Wisdom.

Subhūti, all those Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas who in later, future times will manifestly and perfectly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening — every one of them will do so in dependence upon this very Perfection of Wisdom.

Subhūti, at the present time, in immeasurable and innumerable world-systems in the ten directions, all those Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas who dwell and live and abide out of compassion — compassion for the benefit of the many, for the happiness of the many, out of loving concern for the world, for the welfare, benefit, and happiness of the great multitude, out of compassionate care for gods, humans, and all sentient beings — and who have manifestly and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening: every one of them has done so in dependence upon this very Perfection of Wisdom.

Subhūti, I myself, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened buddha, have also awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening in dependence upon this very Perfection of Wisdom.

Subhūti, it is thus: this Perfection of Wisdom gives birth to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas. And being so, it reveals the world to them all.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, you have declared that the Perfection of Wisdom reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas. Blessed One, how does the Perfection of Wisdom reveal the world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas? What is it that the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas have proclaimed as 'the world'?"

When this was said, the Blessed One spoke thus to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, the Tathāgata has proclaimed the five aggregates as 'the world.' And what are the five? They are as follows: form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Subhūti, these five aggregates are what the Tathāgata has proclaimed as 'the world.'"

Subhūti then asked: "Blessed One, how has the Perfection of Wisdom shown the five aggregates to the Tathāgata? Blessed One, what has the Perfection of Wisdom shown?"

The Blessed One then spoke thus to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, because they neither perish nor utterly perish, the Perfection of Wisdom has shown the five aggregates as 'the world' to the Tathāgatas. And why are they taught as 'neither perishing nor utterly perishing'? Subhūti, it is because the five aggregates, being without essential nature, have the essential nature of emptiness. And emptiness, Subhūti, is neither perishing nor utterly perishing. Thus, Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas.

Subhūti, because it is signless, wishless, non-conditioning, unborn, without substantial existence, and because the dharmadhātu neither perishes nor utterly perishes — thus, Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this very Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that sentient beings are immeasurable and innumerable. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that sentient beings are immeasurable and innumerable? It is, Subhūti, because sentient beings are without essential nature. Because they are without essential nature, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that sentient beings are immeasurable and innumerable. Thus, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that sentient beings, immeasurable and innumerable as they are, are exactly what they are.

Furthermore, Subhūti, because sentient beings are without substantial existence, the Tathāgata has also perfectly and thoroughly known the mental activities of those immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings. Thus, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, the immeasurable and innumerable mental activities of those immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings."

Subhūti, it is in just this way that the Perfection of Wisdom reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas. Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the collected minds of immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings are exactly what they are — collected minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the collected minds of immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings are exactly collected minds? He has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that those collected minds, together with their collectedness, are exhausted, and that even that exhaustion is itself without exhaustion. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the collected minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly collected minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the distracted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly distracted minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the distracted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly distracted minds? Subhūti, those minds that are distracted from the nature of reality are without defining characteristics; those minds are inexhaustible and utterly inexhaustible — and those very minds, undistracted, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the distracted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly distracted minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the immeasurable and inexhaustible minds of immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings are exactly immeasurable and inexhaustible minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the immeasurable and inexhaustible minds of immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings are exactly immeasurable and inexhaustible minds? Subhūti, by that very fact that mind is immeasurable and inexhaustible like space, the one who has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as it is, that mind is immeasurable and inexhaustible — that mind itself has been blessed by the Tathāgata; it is without cessation, without arising, without abiding, without support, immeasurable, and inexhaustible. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the immeasurable and inexhaustible minds of immeasurable and innumerable sentient beings are exactly immeasurable and inexhaustible minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the thoroughly afflicted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly thoroughly afflicted minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the thoroughly afflicted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly thoroughly afflicted minds?

Subhūti, those minds have not actually become thoroughly afflicted by affliction, and there is nothing to which a label can be affixed. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the thoroughly afflicted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly thoroughly afflicted minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the unafflicted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly unafflicted minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the unafflicted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly unafflicted minds? Subhūti, those minds are luminous by nature. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the unafflicted minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly unafflicted minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the abiding minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly abiding minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the abiding minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly abiding minds? Subhūti, those minds abide without foundation. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the abiding minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly abiding minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the thoroughly grasping minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly thoroughly grasping minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the thoroughly grasping minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly thoroughly grasping minds? Subhūti, those minds are not grasped and are not something to be thoroughly grasped. It is in just this way, Subhūti, that in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the thoroughly grasping minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly thoroughly grasping minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the defiled minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly defiled minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the defiled minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly defiled minds? Subhūti, those minds have no essential nature and are like what does not exist.

In this way, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the defiled minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly defiled minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the undefiled minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly undefiled minds.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the undefiled minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly undefiled minds? Subhūti, those minds are spontaneously accomplished by way of inclining toward the absence of inherent existence. In this way, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the undefiled minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly undefiled minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with attachment of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with attachment.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with attachment of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with attachment? Subhūti, whatever is the mind's attachment is not the mind's true suchness, and whatever is the mind's true suchness is not a mind with attachment. In this way, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with attachment of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with attachment.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from attachment of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from attachment.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from attachment of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from attachment? Subhūti, whatever is the freedom of the mind is not itself a mind with attachment, and whatever is the true suchness of a mind free from attachment is not a mind with attachment. In this way, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from attachment of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from attachment.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with aversion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with aversion.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with aversion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with aversion?

Subhūti, whatever mind is accompanied by aversion is not the true reality of mind, just as it is. Whatever is the true reality of mind, just as it is, that is not a mind accompanied by aversion. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with aversion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with aversion.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from aversion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from aversion.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from aversion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from aversion?

Subhūti, whatever is freedom in mind is not a mind accompanied by aversion, and whatever is the true reality of a mind free from aversion, just as it is, is not a mind accompanied by aversion. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from aversion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from aversion.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with delusion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with delusion.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with delusion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with delusion?

Subhūti, whatever mind is accompanied by delusion is not the true reality of mind, just as it is. Whatever is the true reality of mind, just as it is, that is not a mind accompanied by delusion. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with delusion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with delusion.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from delusion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from delusion.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from delusion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from delusion?

Subhūti, whatever is freedom in mind is not a mind accompanied by delusion, and whatever is the true reality of a mind free from delusion, just as it is, is not a mind accompanied by delusion. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds free from delusion of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds free from delusion.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that are not vast of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that are not vast.

"Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that are not vast of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that are not vast? Subhūti, those minds belong to the category of minds without arousal — without arousal in the sense of being unaccompanied by arousal." Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that are not vast of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that are not vast.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the vast minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly vast minds.

Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the vast minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly vast minds? Subhūti, those minds neither diminish nor increase, and because those minds are without separation, they are without distinction. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the vast minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly vast minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that have not become great of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that have not become great.

Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that have not become great of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that have not become great? Subhūti, those minds belong to the category of minds without coming and without going. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that have not become great of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that have not become great.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that have become great of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that have become great.

Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that have become great of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that have become great? Subhūti, those minds are equal through their very equanimity — equal through their essential nature. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that have become great of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that have become great.

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the immeasurable minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly immeasurable minds.

"Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the immeasurable minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly immeasurable minds?" "Subhūti, those minds are immeasurable because they are without support. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the immeasurable minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly immeasurable minds."

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that can be indicated of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that can be indicated.

"Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that can be indicated of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that can be indicated?" "Subhūti, those minds and the essential nature of mind are equally indicated. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that can be indicated of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that can be indicated."

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that cannot be indicated of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that cannot be indicated.

"Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that cannot be indicated of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that cannot be indicated?" "Subhūti, because that mind is without characteristic mark and devoid of any referential object, it does not appear — it has not become manifest to the three eyes or to all eyes. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds that cannot be indicated of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds that cannot be indicated."

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with obstruction.

"Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with obstruction?" "Subhūti, those minds impute what does not exist — they are subject to the apprehension of emptiness as an object. Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with obstruction."

Furthermore, Subhūti, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds without obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds without obstruction.

"Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds without obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds without obstruction?" Thus, Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds without obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds without obstruction.

"Subhūti, those minds have come to arise without there ever having occurred a becoming of non-duality. Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds without obstruction of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds without obstruction.

"Subhūti, furthermore, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with something superior of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with something superior. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with something superior of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with something superior? Subhūti, with regard to whatever is the true suchness of a mind with something superior, there is in it no conceit. Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with something superior of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with something superior.

"Subhūti, furthermore, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with nothing superior of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with nothing superior. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with nothing superior of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with nothing superior? Subhūti, those minds are free from conceptual elaboration because not even the most subtle atom of mind is apprehended. Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds with nothing superior of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds with nothing superior.

"Subhūti, furthermore, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds not in meditative equipoise of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds not in meditative equipoise. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds not in meditative equipoise of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds not in meditative equipoise? Subhūti, those minds enter into non-evenness by means of the even and the non-even, and it is in just this way that they are minds not in meditative equipoise. Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds not in meditative equipoise of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds not in meditative equipoise.

"Subhūti, furthermore, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds in meditative equipoise of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds in meditative equipoise. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds in meditative equipoise of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds in meditative equipoise? Subhūti, those minds enter into evenness by means of the even and the even, and those minds in meditative equipoise in this way are comparable to space. Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds in meditative equipoise of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly minds in meditative equipoise."

"Furthermore, Subhūti, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the unfreed minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly unfreed minds. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the unfreed minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly unfreed minds? Subhūti, those minds are by their very nature freed — their nature is one of having no intrinsic existence. It is in this way, Subhūti, that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the unfreed minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly unfreed minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the freed minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly freed minds. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the freed minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly freed minds? Subhūti, because mind is non-existent, the Tathāgata does not apprehend mind as past, does not apprehend it as future, and does not apprehend it as presently arisen. It is in this way, Subhūti, that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the freed minds of immeasurable sentient beings are exactly freed minds.

Furthermore, Subhūti, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds of immeasurable sentient beings that are invisible are exactly invisible minds. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds of immeasurable sentient beings that are invisible are exactly invisible minds? Subhūti, because mind is non-existent, it is in that way non-existent; because it has not arisen, it cannot be cognized; and because it is not fully established, it cannot be apprehended even by the eye of wisdom or the divine eye — since it does not appear to anyone at all, what need is there to speak of seeing it with the fleshly eye? It is in this way, Subhūti, that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, that the minds of immeasurable sentient beings that are invisible are exactly invisible minds.

Subhūti, it is in this way that the Perfection of Wisdom reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and completely awakened buddhas.

Furthermore, Subhūti, the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, the open and closed states of immeasurable and innumerable other sentient beings and other persons — knowing their open states as open and their closed states as closed. Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata, in dependence upon this Perfection of Wisdom, has perfectly and thoroughly known, just as they are, the open and closed states of immeasurable and innumerable other sentient beings and other persons? Subhūti, it is because every birth among them is perfectly and thoroughly known, just as it is, as abiding in form and arising in form."

Subhūti, in just the same way, every birth among them is perfectly and thoroughly known, just as it is, as abiding in feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness and arising in them.

Subhūti, how does the Tathāgata come to know perfectly those open and closed states as abiding in form, and in just the same way, how does he come to know perfectly those open and closed states as abiding in feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness? The view that "after death, a thus-gone one arises" — this is grounded in form. The view that "after death, a thus-gone one does not arise" — this is grounded in form. The view that "after death, a thus-gone one both arises and does not arise" — this is grounded in form. The view that "after death, a thus-gone one neither arises nor does not arise" — this is grounded in form. And likewise with feeling, perception, and formations: the view that "after death, a thus-gone one arises" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "after death, a thus-gone one does not arise" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "after death, a thus-gone one both arises and does not arise" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "after death, a thus-gone one neither arises nor does not arise" — this is grounded in consciousness.

The view that "the self and the world are permanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the self and the world are impermanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the self and the world are both permanent and impermanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the self and the world are neither permanent nor impermanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. And likewise with feeling, perception, and formations: the view that "the self and the world are permanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the self and the world are impermanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the self and the world are both permanent and impermanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the self and the world are neither permanent nor impermanent — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness.

The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, the thirteenth fascicle.

The view that "the self and the world are finite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the self and the world are infinite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the self and the world are both finite and infinite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the self and the world are neither finite nor infinite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. And likewise with feeling, perception, and formations: the view that "the self and the world are finite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness.

The view that "the self and the world are infinite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the self and the world are both finite and infinite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the self and the world are neither finite nor infinite — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the vital principle and the body are one and the same — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. The view that "the vital principle is one thing and the body is another — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in form. And likewise with feeling, perception, and formations: the view that "the vital principle and the body are one and the same — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness. The view that "the vital principle is one thing and the body is another — this alone is true, all else is delusion" — this is grounded in consciousness.

Subhūti, it is in this way that the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and fully awakened buddha, relying on this Perfection of Wisdom, came to know distinctly how the open and the closed among immeasurable, countless other sentient beings and other persons are grounded in form. And in the same way he came to know distinctly how those who are open and those who are closed are grounded in feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Furthermore, Subhūti, relying on this Perfection of Wisdom, the Tathāgata fully knows, just as it truly is, how the open and the closed among immeasurable, countless other sentient beings and other persons stand.

How, Subhūti, does the Tathāgata, relying on this Perfection of Wisdom, fully know, just as it truly is, how the open and the closed among immeasurable, countless other sentient beings and other persons stand? Subhūti, in this regard the Tathāgata knows form. How does the Tathāgata know form? Subhūti, the Tathāgata knows form just as suchness is — that is how the Tathāgata knows form. And likewise with feeling, perception, and formations. Subhūti, in this regard the Tathāgata knows consciousness. How does the Tathāgata know consciousness? Subhūti, the Tathāgata knows consciousness just as suchness is — that is how the Tathāgata knows consciousness.

Thus, Subhūti, it is by this means that the Tathāgata fully knows, in accordance with right view, how the open and the closed among immeasurable, countless other sentient beings and other persons stand. And so, Subhūti, with regard to suchness, the Tathāgata designates the suchness of the Tathāgata, the suchness of the aggregates, and the suchness of the open and the closed as one undivided suchness.

Whatever is the suchness of the aggregates — that itself is the suchness of the world. Why is this? Subhūti, the Tathāgata has declared in name that the five aggregates are the world. Therefore, Subhūti, whatever is the suchness of the aggregates — that is the suchness of the world. Whatever is the suchness of the world — that is the suchness of all phenomena. Whatever is the suchness of all phenomena — that is the suchness of the fruit of stream-entry.

Whatever is the suchness of the fruit of stream-entry — that itself is the suchness of the fruit of once-returning. Whatever is the suchness of the fruit of once-returning — that itself is the suchness of the fruit of non-returning. Whatever is the suchness of the fruit of non-returning — that itself is the suchness of the fruit of arhatship. Whatever is the suchness of the fruit of arhatship — that itself is the suchness of the pratyekabuddha. Whatever is the suchness of the pratyekabuddha — that itself is the suchness of the Tathāgata. And so, Subhūti, the suchness of the Tathāgata, the suchness of the aggregates, the suchness of all phenomena, and the suchness of all noble śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas — this is one undivided suchness, free from the many that are the open and the closed: because it is not one, because it is not other, because it is inexhaustible, because it is changeless, because it is non-dual, and because it cannot be made into two.

Subhūti, it is in reliance upon this Perfection of Wisdom that the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened to this suchness. Subhūti, therefore this Perfection of Wisdom reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas. Subhūti, therefore the Tathāgata reveals this world to the world with perfect accuracy, and in just this way the world in its entirety is revealed. Subhūti, therefore this Perfection of Wisdom is the mother, the progenitor, and the one who gives birth to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas.

Subhūti, having fully and perfectly awakened to suchness, the Tathāgata knows the suchness of the world. He knows suchness as it is, without distortion. He knows suchness as it is, without otherness. Subhūti, because the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened to suchness, he is called Tathāgata.

The elder Subhūti then said: Blessed One, suchness is profound. It is from that suchness, Blessed One, that the awakening of the blessed buddhas is fully disclosed and fully revealed. Blessed One, who else would place trust in this? Those who would are irreversible bodhisattvas, great beings; or arhats whose discriminative understanding is fully perfected; or those persons whose view is complete and excellent. Blessed One, these supremely profound matters were spoken by the Tathāgata after he had fully and perfectly awakened.

When this had been said, the Blessed One spoke thus to the venerable Subhūti: So it is, Subhūti, so it is. The Tathāgata has taught these supremely profound matters after fully and perfectly awakening to them. Subhūti, this suchness to which the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened is inexhaustible, and having fully and perfectly awakened, he has taught the inexhaustible as inexhaustible.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, along with twenty thousand sons of the gods from among the gods of the desire realm who dwell in that realm, and sons of the gods of the Brahmā realms, came and approached the Blessed One, and having bowed their heads at the feet of the Blessed One, they stood to one side. Standing to one side, those sons of the gods of the desire realm and the form realm spoke to the Blessed One with these words: Blessed One, when such profound Dharma is fully taught, Blessed One, what characteristic is to be assigned to it?

The Blessed One declared: Sons of the gods, the characteristic assigned to it is this: it is empty.

Sons of the gods, the characteristics assigned to it are these: it is signless; it is wishless. Sons of the gods, it is unborn; it is unceasing; it is without defilement; it is without purification; it is without substantial existence; it is nirvāṇa; it is the dharmadhātu; it is suchness. Such are the characteristics assigned to it. And why? Sons of the gods, these characteristics are non-abiding characteristics; these characteristics are like space. Sons of the gods, these characteristics were not established by the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas. These characteristics are not to be reckoned as form, nor are they to be reckoned as feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness.
