These characteristics do not rest upon form, nor do they rest upon feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness. These characteristics were not established by gods, humans, or non-humans. These characteristics cannot be moved by the world together with its gods, humans, and asuras. And why? Because the world together with its gods, humans, and asuras is itself entirely exhausted within these very characteristics. These characteristics were not placed there by anyone's hand. Sons of the gods, if someone were to say, "Who established this space?" — sons of the gods, would that be speaking the truth? When he had spoken thus, those sons of the gods of the desire realm and the form realm said to the Blessed One: Blessed One, space was not established by anyone. And why? Blessed One, space is unconditioned, and therefore space was not established by anyone. Then the Blessed One declared to those sons of the gods of the desire realm and the form realm: Sons of the gods, so it is, so it is. Whether Tathāgatas arise or whether Tathāgatas do not arise, these characteristics abide just as they are. And why? Because it is just as these abide that the Tathāgata, having fully and perfectly awakened, teaches them. Therefore, sons of the gods, the Tathāgata is called "Tathāgata."

Then the venerable Subhūti said this to the Blessed One: Blessed One, the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened to these profound characteristics — namely, that the Perfection of Wisdom is the unattached wisdom of the Tathāgatas. Because it is the unattached wisdom, the Perfection of Wisdom is the domain of the Tathāgatas. When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One declared to the venerable Subhūti: Subhūti, so it is, so it is. Subhūti, in just this way the Perfection of Wisdom correctly reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas. Subhūti, just as the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas rely upon and abide in this Dharma of the Perfection of Wisdom, and because they do not abide in those very dharmas in the way they truly are, they abide permanently — and in just the same way the Tathāgata fully and perfectly awakens, and therefore they rely upon and abide in the Dharma itself alone. They honor the Dharma. They hold it as supreme. They venerate it. They revere it. They take it as their emblem. They pay homage to it. Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom is the nature of reality of all dharmas.

Therefore, the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas honor the Perfection of Wisdom, hold it as supreme, revere it, venerate it, take it as their emblem, and pay homage to it. And why is that? Subhūti, it is from this Perfection of Wisdom that omniscience is fully unfolded for the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas. The Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas are those who know what has been done and feel what has been done.

Subhūti, when someone says, "The Tathāgata knows what has been done and feels what has been done," that person speaks with right speech. When one says, "He knows what has been done and feels what has been done," that is speech spoken with right speech.

Now, Subhūti, how is it that the Tathāgata knows what has been done and feels what has been done? Subhūti, by those very vehicles by which the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and fully awakened buddha has traveled and accomplished — by those very vehicles and accomplishments by which he has fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening — it is by that very knowing and feeling of what has been done that the Tathāgata upholds and maintains them. Subhūti, this is to be understood as the Tathāgata's knowing what has been done and feeling what has been done.

Furthermore, Subhūti, the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened to all phenomena as uncreated — as simply uncreated. He has fully and perfectly awakened to them as unchanging — as simply unchanging. He has fully and perfectly awakened to them as not explicitly conditioned — as simply not explicitly conditioned. Subhūti, this too is to be understood as the Tathāgata's knowing what has been done and feeling what has been done.

Subhūti, by relying upon the Perfection of Wisdom, the gnosis of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas enters into all phenomena. Subhūti, by this synonym as well, the Perfection of Wisdom is what correctly reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas.

When this had been declared, the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, when there is no knower and no seer of all phenomena, how does the Perfection of Wisdom correctly reveal this world to the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly and fully awakened buddhas?"

When this had been asked, the Blessed One spoke thus to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, that you intend to ask and inquire of the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and fully awakened buddha about such a matter — excellent! Excellent! Subhūti, that you say, 'All phenomena have no knower and no seer' — Subhūti, that is so, that is so indeed. All phenomena have no knower and no seer.

And how is it, Subhūti, that all phenomena have no knower and no seer? Subhūti, all phenomena are empty. Subhūti, all phenomena are without abiding. Therefore, Subhūti, all phenomena have no knower and no seer. Therefore, Subhūti, it is by relying upon the Perfection of Wisdom that the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened to all those phenomena."

"Subhūti, so it is that the Perfection of Wisdom rightly reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and fully awakened buddhas. It rightly reveals because form is not seen, and because feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are not seen. Subhūti, thus it is that the Perfection of Wisdom rightly reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and fully awakened buddhas."

Subhūti asked: "Blessed One, how is it that form is not seen? How is it that feeling, perception, and formations are not seen? Blessed One, how is it that consciousness is not seen?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, if consciousness that takes form as its referent object does not arise, then form is not seen. Likewise, if consciousness that takes feeling, perception, and formations as its referent object does not arise — and, Subhūti, if mind that takes consciousness itself as its referent object does not arise — then consciousness is not seen. Subhūti, that which is the non-seeing of form, that which is the non-seeing of feeling, perception, and formations, and, Subhūti, that which is the non-seeing of consciousness — in that way the Tathāgata sees the world. Subhūti, thus it is that the Perfection of Wisdom rightly reveals this world to the Tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly and fully awakened buddhas.

Subhūti, and how does the Perfection of Wisdom rightly reveal the world? In this way: the world is empty. In this way it declares the world. In this way it makes the world known. In this way it causes the world to be seen. In this way the world is inconceivable, in this way the world is peaceful, in this way the world is solitary, in this way the world is utterly pure — so does it declare the world. So does it make it known. So does it cause the world to be seen."

This is the twelfth chapter, called "Rightly Revealing the World," from the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, this Perfection of Wisdom is profound. Blessed One, how wondrous — this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by great activity. Blessed One, how wondrous — this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is inconceivable, by activity that has no equal, by activity that is immeasurable, by activity that is innumerable, by activity that is unequalled-yet-equal."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that is so, that is so indeed. Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by great activity. Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is inconceivable, by activity that has no equal, by activity that is immeasurable, by activity that is innumerable, by activity that is unequalled-yet-equal.

Subhūti, how is it that this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by inconceivable activity? Subhūti, Tathāgatahood itself, buddhahood itself, self-arisen enlightenment itself, and omniscience itself are inconceivable. Subhūti, thus this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by inconceivable activity and cannot be thought by the mind. Why is that? It is because mind, thinking, and the phenomena arisen from mind do not enter into it here.

Subhūti, how is it that this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that has no equal?"

"Subhūti, Tathāgatahood itself, buddhahood itself, self-arisen enlightenment itself, and omniscience itself cannot be thought or equalled. Subhūti, thus this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that has no equal. Subhūti, how is it that this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is immeasurable? Subhūti, Tathāgatahood itself, buddhahood itself, self-arisen enlightenment itself, and omniscience itself are immeasurable. Subhūti, thus this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is immeasurable. Subhūti, how is it that this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is innumerable? Subhūti, Tathāgatahood itself, buddhahood itself, self-arisen enlightenment itself, and omniscience itself are innumerable. Subhūti, thus this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is innumerable. Subhūti, how is it that this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is unequalled-yet-equal? Subhūti, among Tathāgatahood itself, buddhahood itself, self-arisen enlightenment itself, and omniscience itself, there is nothing equal to them — what need is there to speak of anything superior? Subhūti, thus this Perfection of Wisdom is attended by activity that is unequalled-yet-equal."

The elder Subhūti then asked: "Blessed One, is it so that Tathāgatahood itself is inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal? And likewise that buddhahood itself, self-arisen enlightenment itself, and omniscience itself are inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, and unequalled-yet-equal? Or is form also inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal? And likewise feeling, perception, and formations? And is consciousness also inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal? And are all phenomena also inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal?"

When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One declared to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, it is exactly so, it is exactly so. Subhūti, form too is inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal. Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal. Subhūti, likewise all phenomena are inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, and unequalled-yet-equal. Why is that? Subhūti, in the nature of reality of form there is no mind, no thinking, and no phenomena arisen from mind — and so there is no conceiving of it as such. Subhūti, likewise in the nature of reality of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness there is no mind, no thinking, and no phenomena arisen from mind — and so there is no conceiving of it as such. Subhūti, in the nature of reality of all phenomena there is no mind, no thinking, and no phenomena arisen from mind — and so there is no conceiving of it as such. Subhūti, thus form too is inconceivable and without equal. Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are inconceivable and without equal. Subhūti, likewise all phenomena are inconceivable and without equal. Subhūti, form too is immeasurable."

Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are also immeasurable. Subhūti, all phenomena too are immeasurable. Why is that? Subhūti, the measure of form is not known. Subhūti, likewise the measure of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness is not known, and the measure of all phenomena too is not known. Subhūti, why is the measure of form not fully known? And likewise feeling, perception, and formations — Subhūti, why is the measure of consciousness not fully known? Subhūti, why is the measure of all phenomena not fully known? Because, Subhūti, form is without measure. Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are without measure, and Subhūti, all phenomena too are without measure. Subhūti, why is form without measure? Subhūti, why are feeling, perception, and formations likewise without measure? Subhūti, why is consciousness without measure? Subhūti, why are all phenomena without measure? Because all phenomena are without measure. Subhūti, form too is innumerable. Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are innumerable. Subhūti, all phenomena too are innumerable — because they transcend counting itself. Subhūti, form too is without equal and yet equal. Subhūti, likewise feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are without equal and yet equal. Subhūti, likewise all phenomena are without equal and yet equal, because, Subhūti, all phenomena are equal to space.

Subhūti, what do you think — is there any equating, numbering, measuring, likening, minding, thinking, or phenomenon arisen from mind in space? Subhūti replied: Blessed One, there is not. The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, in exactly that way, by this enumeration of categories, all these phenomena too are inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, without equal and yet equal. Subhūti, these are the Tathāgata's phenomena. Subhūti, by this same enumeration of categories, all phenomena are inconceivable, without equal, immeasurable, innumerable, without equal and yet equal. Subhūti, these Tathāgata's phenomena are inconceivable because they are free from mind. They are without equal because they fully transcend likening. Subhūti, this expression "inconceivable and without equal" is the supreme word of Dharma for those in whom consciousness has arisen. Likewise the expressions "immeasurable," "innumerable," "without equal and yet equal" are the same.

Subhūti, the expressions "immeasurable," "innumerable," "without equal and yet equal" mean being free from equality, from number, and from measure. The Tathāgata's phenomena are immeasurable, innumerable, without equal and yet equal. These phenomena, by virtue of being equal to space, innumerable and immeasurable, are without equal and yet equal, innumerable and immeasurable. Ah, how wondrous — these phenomena, being equal to space, do not enter into being equal or without equal. Therefore, Subhūti, these phenomena are spoken of as without equal. Because space is inconceivable, these phenomena are inconceivable. Because space is without equal, these phenomena are without equal. Because space is immeasurable, these phenomena are immeasurable. Because space is innumerable, these phenomena are innumerable. Because space is without equal and yet equal, these phenomena are without equal and yet equal.

Even as the Blessed One taught these qualities — the inconceivable, the without equal, the immeasurable, the innumerable, the without equal and yet equal — five hundred monks attained liberation of mind from the defilements without remainder. Two thousand nuns likewise attained liberation of mind from the defilements without remainder. Six thousand laymen (upāsakas) gained the Dharma-eye regarding phenomena — pure, free from dust and stain. Three thousand laywomen (upāsikās) also gained the Dharma-eye regarding phenomena — pure, free from dust and stain. Twenty bodhisattvas attained the forbearance of non-arising (anutpattikadharmakṣānti). These same bodhisattvas the Blessed One then prophesied would attain unsurpassable, perfectly complete awakening in this very good aeon. As for those laymen and laywomen who had gained the Dharma-eye regarding phenomena — pure, free from dust and stain — them too the Blessed One prophesied, declaring that they would come to attain liberation of mind from the defilements without remainder.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound. Blessed One, how wondrous — this Perfection of Wisdom abides in great activity."

When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One said to the venerable Subhūti: "Just so, Subhūti, just so. Subhūti, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound. Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom abides in great activity. Why is that? Because it is genuinely endowed with omniscience. It is genuinely endowed with the ground of the pratyekabuddhas. It is genuinely endowed with the ground of the śrāvakas.

"Subhūti, consider this analogy. When a king of the warrior-noble caste (kṣatriya) has been consecrated as sovereign, whatever actions he undertakes with regard to his people — whether the power and effort he brings to those actions — all affairs of the city and all affairs of the kingdom come to be conducted through his ministers, and in this way the king's own effort is small, the burden set aside. In the same way, Subhūti, all the qualities of a buddha, all the qualities of a pratyekabuddha, and all the qualities of a śrāvaka — all of these are genuinely endowed with the Perfection of Wisdom, so that it is the Perfection of Wisdom itself that performs the activity among them. By this way of understanding, Subhūti, this Perfection of Wisdom abides in great activity.

"It abides as such in order that form not be clung to and not be attached to; and likewise in order that feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness not be clung to and not be attached to; in order that the fruit of stream-entry not be clung to and not be attached to; and likewise in order that the fruit of once-returning, the fruit of non-returning, and arhatship not be clung to and not be attached to; in order that individual awakening not be clung to and not be attached to; and in order that omniscience not be clung to and not be attached to — for these reasons the Perfection of Wisdom abides."

Subhūti asked: "Blessed One, how does the Perfection of Wisdom abide so that one does not cling to omniscience and does not become attached to it?"

The Blessed One replied: "Subhūti, what do you think? Do you truly see arhatship as something to be clung to or attached to?"

Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, that is not so. Blessed One, I do not truly perceive any such thing as a phenomenon of arhatship that one could cling to or become attached to."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, it is exactly so, it is exactly so. I too do not truly perceive the Tathāgata himself. Subhūti, because I do not truly perceive the Tathāgata himself, I do not cling to him and I do not become attached to him. Subhūti, it is for this reason that there is likewise no clinging and no attachment with regard to omniscience."

Subhūti then said: "Blessed One, when bodhisattvas, great beings, who have newly entered the vehicle and whose roots of virtue are few hear this teaching — that there is no clinging and no attachment even with regard to omniscience — might they not be seized with alarm, with utter terror, with dread?"

"Blessed One, however, those bodhisattvas, great beings, who are well-provisioned with excellent causes, who have served the Victors of the past, and who have long cultivated roots of virtue — when they hear this profound Perfection of Wisdom, they will come to have faith in it."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, it is exactly so, it is exactly so."

Then the sons of the gods dwelling in the desire realm and the sons of the gods dwelling in the form realm addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound, difficult to behold, and difficult to realize. Blessed One, those bodhisattvas who will come to have faith upon hearing this profound Perfection of Wisdom — they are ones who have served the Victors of the past and have long cultivated roots of virtue."

"Blessed One, if all the sentient beings throughout a great trichiliocosm were to practise on the level of following through faith for an aeon or even more than an aeon, and if someone were to endure, to delight in, to seek, to reflect upon, to examine, and to contemplate this profound Perfection of Wisdom for even a single day — Blessed One, this latter one would be superior to all those others."

When these words had been spoken, the Blessed One replied to the sons of the gods dwelling in the desire realm and the sons of the gods dwelling in the form realm: "Sons of the gods, if any son or daughter of good family were to hear this profound Perfection of Wisdom, they should hope for swift passage to nirvāṇa — far swifter than those who practise on the level of following through faith for an aeon or more than an aeon."

Then the sons of the gods dwelling in the desire realm and the sons of the gods dwelling in the form realm said to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, this Perfection of Wisdom is indeed a great perfection." So saying, they bowed their heads at the feet of the Blessed One, circumambulated the Blessed One three times, took their leave of the Blessed One's presence, and departed. Having gone not very far at all, they became invisible — the sons of the gods dwelling in the desire realm returning to abide in the desire realm, and the sons of the gods dwelling in the form realm returning to abide in the Brahmā world.

This concludes the thirteenth chapter of the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, entitled "The Inconceivable."

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, when a bodhisattva, a great being, hears this profound Perfection of Wisdom being taught, and upon hearing it at that very moment has faith and does not turn away, is never dejected, is not bewildered, is not confused, harbors no doubt, and entertains no suspicion — and thus rejoices in the Perfection of Wisdom — Blessed One, from where does such a one pass away, and where will he be reborn?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, who upon hearing this profound Perfection of Wisdom being taught, at that very moment has faith and does not turn away, is never dejected, is not bewildered, is not confused, harbors no doubt, and entertains no suspicion — who rejoices in what is equal to seeing it — who will uphold and cultivate this profound Perfection of Wisdom, who will not abandon the mental engagement endowed with the Perfection of Wisdom and will not let his mind turn elsewhere, who will arouse aspiration for the sake of grasping it, retaining it, reading it, mastering it, and proclaiming it aloud — who will follow it, proceed in accordance with it, and not give up the teaching of the Dharma — Subhūti, such a one, just like a calf that is young and does not abandon the cow, so too, Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, who having heard this profound Perfection of Wisdom, for as long as this Perfection of Wisdom has not yet come to rest in his body or been contained in a volume, will not give up the teaching of the Dharma — that bodhisattva, that great being, Subhūti, has passed away from among human beings and has been reborn as a human being."

Subhūti asked: "Blessed One, might there also be bodhisattvas, great beings, endowed with these qualities who have passed away from another buddha-field and been born here?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, bodhisattvas, great beings, who have attended upon other buddhas, the Blessed Ones, and have questioned them, inquired of them, and thoroughly examined them — those who have passed away from those other buddha-fields and been born here should also be understood as endowed with precisely these same qualities."

"Furthermore, Subhūti, a bodhisattva, a great being, who has passed away from among the gods of Tuṣita and been born here should likewise be understood as endowed with precisely those same qualities — namely, one who has attended upon the bodhisattva great being Maitreya (Loving-kindness, the future Buddha), and with reference to this Perfection of Wisdom has questioned him, inquired of him, and thoroughly examined him; such a one too should be understood as endowed with these very qualities."

"Subhūti, furthermore, a bodhisattva who has heard this profound Perfection of Wisdom and yet in prior times has never questioned, inquired, or thoroughly examined it — if such a one is born among human beings, when this profound Perfection of Wisdom is being taught he will become perplexed, become confused, and become faint-hearted. Subhūti, that bodhisattva should be understood as one who by nature has never asked questions in prior times. And why is that? It is because such a one, when this profound Perfection of Wisdom is being taught, becomes perplexed, becomes confused, and becomes faint-hearted."

"Subhūti, furthermore, there is a bodhisattva who in prior times heard this very profound Perfection of Wisdom for only the span of a single day, or two days, or three, or four, or five days —"

— served and honored the teacher, made thorough inquiry, questioned fully, and asked in full — yet that person's faith, for a time, will become lost and will be snatched away by questioning. And why is that? Subhūti, it is because that person who, in former times, did not make thorough inquiry into this profound Perfection of Wisdom, did not question it properly, did not ask about it properly, and did not follow it — such a one will sometimes be moved by aspiration to follow along when this profound Perfection of Wisdom is being taught, and sometimes will feel no aspiration at all. For that reason, they will become distracted and faint-hearted; their mind will become agitated and will fray apart like cotton passing through. That bodhisattva should be understood as one who entered the vehicle not long ago and has newly come to the vehicle. That bodhisattva will altogether abandon that faith, that clarity, and that aspiration — in the sense that they will neither retain this profound Perfection of Wisdom, nor engage with it, nor rely upon it — and so one should expect that they will settle upon one ground: either the ground of a śrāvaka or the ground of a pratyekabuddha, whichever of those two it may be.

Subhūti, consider this analogy: when a ship has gone out onto a great ocean and then breaks apart, those aboard who do not seize hold of a plank of wood or a flat board, and who do not cling to a corpse — know, Subhūti, that they will meet their end in the water without reaching the far shore. Subhūti, and those who, when a ship has gone out onto a great ocean and broken apart, do seize hold of a plank of wood or a flat board or a corpse — know, Subhūti, that they will not meet their end in the water, but will reach the far shore safely, without being submerged or soaked, and will come to rest on dry ground.

Subhūti, in the same way, a bodhisattva who possesses only faith, only joy, only clarity, only aspiration — if they do not rely upon the Perfection of Wisdom, know, Subhūti, that they will fall apart midway along the path and, without attaining omniscience, will come to rest in the state of a śrāvaka or a pratyekabuddha.

Subhūti, furthermore, those bodhisattvas, great beings, who wish to awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening — if they possess faith, forbearance, earnest longing, aspiration, diligence, heedfulness, devotion, elevated intention, relinquishment, respectfulness, joy, contentment, clarity, exultation, and unwavering effort, and if they rely upon the Perfection of Wisdom — then that very faith of theirs, that forbearance, that earnest longing, that aspiration, that diligence, that heedfulness, that devotion, that elevated intention, that relinquishment, that respectfulness, that joy, that contentment, that clarity, that exultation, and that unwavering effort will, upon attaining the Perfection of Wisdom, come to rest in omniscience.

Subhūti, consider this analogy: if a woman or a man were to place an unfired clay pot in water, know, Subhūti, that that pot will not last long — it will break and crumble very quickly. And why is that? It is because the pot is unfired, and so it will in the end simply become earth again.

In just the same way, Subhūti, there may be some bodhisattva, some great being, who has faith in the prospect of fully and perfectly awakening to unsurpassable, complete, and perfect awakening, and who has patience, longing, aspiration, diligence, heedfulness, devotion, elevated intention, relinquishment, respectfulness, joy, exultation, clarity, contentment, and unwavering effort — and yet, if that bodhisattva has not been fully sustained by the Perfection of Wisdom and skillful means, then, Subhūti, know that that bodhisattva will collapse and be destroyed midway along the path. And what, Subhūti, is this collapse and destruction midway along the bodhisattva's path? It is the ground of the śrāvaka or the ground of the pratyekabuddha.

Now, Subhūti, consider this analogy: suppose a woman or a man were to take a well-fired clay pot, draw water from a river, a pond, a lake, a great lake, a well, or some other source of water, and carry that water away — then, Subhūti, know that the pot will arrive home safely. And why is that? It is because the pot has been well fired. In just the same way, Subhūti, whatever bodhisattva, whatever great being, has faith in the prospect of fully and perfectly awakening to unsurpassable, complete, and perfect awakening, and has patience, longing, aspiration, diligence, heedfulness, devotion, elevated intention, relinquishment, respectfulness, joy, exultation, clarity, contentment, and unwavering effort — and moreover is fully sustained by the Perfection of Wisdom and skillful means — know, Subhūti, that such a great bodhisattva will not collapse and be destroyed midway along the path; without deflection, without interruption, that being will come to rest in omniscience itself.

The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The Fourteenth Fascicle.

Subhūti, consider this analogy: suppose a man of confused and wandering intelligence were to lash together a boat that had not been properly made or repaired, and having loaded it with goods and weighed it down with cargo, were to send it out into the water — then, Subhūti, know that that boat is the kind of thing destined to break apart: it will not make it to the far shore, the cargo will be lost, and the boat itself will be destroyed. In just this way, a ship's captain who lacks skillful means and is of confused intelligence will be separated from great wealth and meet with ruin, falling utterly away from the great source of precious jewels.

In just the same way, Subhūti, there may be some bodhisattva who has faith in the prospect of fully and perfectly awakening to unsurpassable, complete, and perfect awakening, and who has patience, longing, aspiration, diligence, heedfulness, devotion, elevated intention, relinquishment, respectfulness, joy, contentment, clarity, exultation, and unwavering effort — and yet, if that bodhisattva has not been fully embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and has become separated from skillful means, then, Subhūti, that bodhisattva will be destroyed and ruined midway, failing to reach the great source of the precious jewel of omniscience. Such a one falls utterly away from the great aim for oneself and falls utterly away from the great mass of precious jewels that constitutes the great aim for others — falling, that is, utterly away from the great source of the precious jewel of omniscience. Know this to be so.

Subhūti, what then is this destruction and ruin midway along the bodhisattva's path?

It is this: falling to the ground of a śrāvaka or to the ground of a pratyekabuddha.

Subhūti, consider this analogy. A skilled and experienced ship's captain, wishing to sail out to sea, has a vessel properly bound together, its hull-seams well caulked, well fitted out and repaired in every way. He draws it into the water, loads it with cargo until full, stows everything properly, sets it with a favorable wind, and sails it steadily in the direction he wishes to go. Subhūti, know that such a ship will not be destroyed by the water; it will reach the destination toward which it is headed, and that captain too will come to possess this great gain — that is, the precious jewels of the world.

Subhūti, in just the same way, when a bodhisattva, a great being, who aspires to fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening possesses faith, possesses forbearance, possesses craving's antidote, possesses aspiration, possesses diligence, possesses heedfulness, possesses devotion, possesses superior intention, possesses relinquishment, possesses reverence, possesses joy, possesses delight, possesses clarity, possesses great rejoicing, and possesses the quality of never abandoning effort — when all this is fully embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and not separated from skillful means — then, Subhūti, that bodhisattva, that great being, will not be destroyed and ruined midway, will not be cut off, but will abide on the path to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Know this to be so.

Why is that? Subhūti, it is as follows. Those bodhisattvas, those great beings, who aspire to fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening and who possess faith, possess forbearance, possess aspiration's fervor, possess aspiration, possess diligence, possess heedfulness, possess devotion, possess superior intention, possess relinquishment, possess reverence, possess joy, possess delight, possess clarity, possess great rejoicing, and possess the quality of never abandoning effort — and whose qualities are fully embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and not separated from skillful means — such ones actualize the qualities through which omniscience is attained, and they enter perfectly onto the path to fully awakening to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Except for that, they do not accomplish the ground of a śrāvaka or the ground of a pratyekabuddha.

Subhūti, consider another analogy. Suppose a certain person has been born and has now reached one hundred and twenty years of age, old, aged, and decrepit. And suppose that person's body is afflicted by some illness arising from wind, or from bile, or from phlegm, or from a combination of all three. Subhūti, what do you think — could that person rise from a seat without support? Subhūti replied: Blessed One, he could not. The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, even if that person could rise from the seat, still, overwhelmed as he is by such old age and illness, he would have no strength to walk so much as one bowshot's distance or two bowshots' distance. Even if he could rise from the seat, he would have no strength to walk anywhere at all.

Subhūti, in just the same way, when a bodhisattva, a great being, who aspires to fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening possesses faith, possesses forbearance, possesses fervent longing, possesses aspiration, possesses diligence, possesses heedfulness, possesses devotion, possesses superior intention, possesses relinquishment, possesses reverence, possesses joy, possesses delight, possesses clarity, possesses great rejoicing, and possesses the quality of never abandoning effort — and yet —

— and yet that bodhisattva is not taken up and embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom, and is parted from skillful means — then, Subhūti, even though such a one has rightly entered upon the task of fully awakening to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, that bodhisattva will be consumed by the intermediate fall and brought to ruin. Know that this means coming to rest on the ground of the śrāvakas or the ground of the pratyekabuddhas. And why is that? It is because that bodhisattva has not been taken up and embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom, and has been parted from skillful means.

Subhūti, consider this analogy. A man who was born and has reached the age of one hundred and twenty years, old, aged, and decrepit, is struck in that very body by some illness arising from wind, or arising from bile, or arising from phlegm, or arising from their combination. Two strong men support him well from either side, steadying him and holding him securely, and they say to him: "Good sir, until you have reached the place you wish to go, you will not face the danger of falling midway on the road as long as we two are supporting you. Go wherever you wish to go." With that, he will be able to rise from the bed.

In just the same way, Subhūti, when a bodhisattva, a great being, who aspires to fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening possesses faith, possesses forbearance, possesses fervent longing, possesses aspiration, possesses diligence, possesses heedfulness, possesses devotion, possesses superior intention, possesses relinquishment, possesses reverence, possesses joy, possesses delight, possesses clarity, possesses great rejoicing, and possesses the quality of never abandoning effort — and when, moreover, that bodhisattva is taken up and embraced by the Perfection of Wisdom and is not parted from skillful means — then, Subhūti, that bodhisattva, that great being, will not be consumed by the intermediate fall and will not be brought to ruin. Know that from that very place, that bodhisattva, that great being, will be able to reach the ground of unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.

This concludes the fourteenth chapter, called the Chapter of Analogies, from the noble Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines.

Then the venerable Subhūti addressed the Blessed One with these words: "Blessed One, how should a bodhisattva, a great being, who is just beginning, abide in the Perfection of Wisdom? How should such a one train?"

When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One declared to the venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, here a bodhisattva, a great being, who is just beginning and wishes to train in the Perfection of Wisdom should draw near to, rely upon, and attend upon virtuous friends — those who will guide and teach this Perfection of Wisdom, and those who will recite the meaning of the Perfection of Wisdom by scriptural transmission. Know, Subhūti, that those individuals are the virtuous friends of that bodhisattva, that great being.

"To such a bodhisattva, the virtuous friend should say: 'Come here, son of good family. Train in the perfection of generosity. Likewise, train in the perfection of moral discipline, the perfection of patience, the perfection of diligence, the perfection of meditative concentration, and the perfection of wisdom. Son of good family, whatever acts of generosity you perform, dedicate them all entirely to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.'"



Subhūti, whatever sufferings there are for the world that moves about in saṃsāra, from all of these he acts to protect it, and he makes effort and generates diligence in order to eliminate those sufferings. Subhūti, it is in this way that the bodhisattva mahāsattva, having become fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment, becomes the protector of the world.

Subhūti, how does the bodhisattva mahāsattva, having become fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment, become the refuge of the world? Subhūti, whatever sentient beings are subject to the nature of birth, subject to the nature of aging, subject to the nature of sickness, subject to the nature of death, and subject to the nature of grief, lamentation, suffering, mental unhappiness, and turmoil — those sentient beings he causes to be utterly freed from birth, aging, sickness, death, grief, lamentation, suffering, mental unhappiness, and turmoil. Subhūti, it is in this way that the bodhisattva mahāsattva, having become fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment, becomes the refuge of the world.

Subhūti, how does the bodhisattva mahāsattva, having become fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment, become the abode of the world? Subhūti, that Tathāgata, Arhat, perfectly complete Buddha teaches the Dharma to sentient beings so as to bring about the absence of engagement. Subhūti, it is in this way that the bodhisattva mahāsattva, having become fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment, becomes the abode of the world.

Subhūti asked, "Blessed One, how is there an absence of engagement?"

The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, whatever is the absence of connection of form, that is the absence of engagement of form. Whatever is the absence of engagement of form, that is the absence of connection of form. Whatever is the absence of connection of form, that is the absence of arising and absence of cessation of form. Whatever is the absence of arising and absence of cessation of form, that is the absence of engagement of form. That which is the absence of engagement with respect to form is the absence of connection of form; that absence of connection of form is the absence of engagement of form. It is likewise for feeling, perception, and formations.

Subhūti, whatever is the absence of connection of consciousness, that is the absence of engagement of consciousness. Whatever is the absence of engagement of consciousness, that is the absence of connection of consciousness. Whatever is the absence of connection of consciousness, that is the absence of arising and absence of cessation of consciousness. Whatever is the absence of arising and absence of cessation of consciousness, that is the absence of engagement of consciousness. That which is the absence of eng

Subhūti, whatever is the far shore of form, that is not form; yet just as the far shore is, so too is form. It is likewise for feeling, perception, and formations. Subhūti, whatever is the far shore of consciousness, that is not consciousness; yet just as the far shore is, so too is consciousness. Subhūti, just as the far shore of form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness is, so too are all phenomena.

Subhūti said: Blessed One, if the far shore of form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness is like that for all phenomena, then Blessed One, have bodhisattva great beings not already become fully and perfectly awakened to all phenomena? Why is this? Blessed One, it is because there is no conceptualization whatsoever in this regard.

The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, it is so, it is so. In that which is the far shore there is no conceptualization whatsoever. Subhūti, precisely because there is no conceptualization, bodhisattva great beings do become fully and perfectly awakened to all phenomena. Subhūti, this too is the most difficult of tasks performed by bodhisattva great beings: that while thus definitely contemplating all phenomena, they neither actualize them nor lose heart, but think, "Thus shall we become fully and perfectly awakened to these phenomena; thus, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, shall we teach these phenomena and fully explain them."

Subhūti, in this way, bodhisattva great beings, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, become the ally of the world.

Subhūti, how do bodhisattva great beings, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, become an island for the world? Subhūti, it is like this: for example, in a river or an ocean, whatever place is completely surrounded by water is called an island. Subhūti, likewise, form is utterly cut off from a prior limit and a later limit. So too are feeling, perception, and formations; and, Subhūti, so too is consciousness utterly cut off from a prior limit and a later limit. Subhūti, by this utter cutting-off, all phenomena are utterly cut off from a prior limit and a later limit.

Subhūti, whatever is the utter cutting-off of all phenomena—this is peace, this is sublime, this is complete passing beyond sorrow, this is just as it is, this is unmistaken. Subhūti, it is in this way that bodhisattva great beings, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, become an island for the world.

Subhūti, how do bodhisattva great beings, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, become a light for the world? Subhūti, here, bodhisattva great beings, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, for a long time illuminate with wisdom those sentient beings who are utterly enveloped by the eggshell and cataract of ignorance and overwhelmed by darkness, and they dispel all the pitch-black darkness of unknowing.





Subhūti, thus the bodhisattva great being, having become fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, becomes a support for immeasurable, countless sentient beings. Subhūti said: Blessed One, in that the bodhisattva great being who has donned this armor causes immeasurable, countless sentient beings to pass utterly beyond suffering, this is indeed a difficult deed. When this was said, the Blessed One spoke thus to venerable Subhūti: Subhūti, it is so, it is so. The bodhisattva great being who has donned this armor, in causing immeasurable, countless sentient beings to pass utterly beyond suffering, performs a difficult deed.

Subhūti, furthermore, the armor that the bodhisattva great being dons, the great armor, is not connected with form, nor is it connected for the purpose of form; likewise it is not connected with feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, nor is it connected for the purpose of consciousness. It is not connected with the level of the śrāvaka, nor is it connected for the purpose of the level of the śrāvaka. It is not connected with the level of the pratyekabuddha, nor is it connected for the purpose of the level of the pratyekabuddha. It is not connected with the level of buddhahood, nor is it connected for the purpose of the level of buddhahood. Why is this? Subhūti, alas, this great armor that the bodhisattva great being dons, the great armor, is not connected with any phenomenon at all.

Subhūti said: Blessed One, in this way, the bodhisattva great being who has donned the great armor, who thus practices the profound Perfection of Wisdom, does not place hope in three abodes. What are the three? They are the level of the śrāvaka, the level of the pratyekabuddha, and the level of buddhahood. When this was said, the Blessed One spoke thus to venerable Subhūti: Subhūti, seeing what significance do you say that the bodhisattva great being who has donned the great armor, who thus practices the profound Perfection of Wisdom, does not place hope in three abodes—namely, the level of the śrāvaka, the level of the pratyekabuddha, and the level of buddhahood?

Subhūti replied: Subhūti, for the bodhisattva great being who has donned the great armor, who thus practices the profound Perfection of Wisdom, to place hope in the level of the śrāvaka or the level of the pratyekabuddha—this has no basis and no occasion. Rather, one who has donned this great armor for the sake of all sentient beings should place hope only in buddhahood itself.

Subhūti said: Blessed One, the Perfection of Wisdom is profound. It is not to be cultivated by anyone; it has not been cultivated by anyone; it is not being cultivated by anyone; it will not be cultivated by anyone. There is not even the slightest thing to be cultivated, nor is there anywhere it is to be cultivated. Why is this? Blessed One, it is because in the Perfection of Wisdom, no phenomenon whatsoever is thoroughly established. Blessed One, this cultivation of the Perfection of Wisdom is thus like the cultivation of space. Blessed One, this cultivation of the Perfection of Wisdom is thus the non-cultivation of all phenomena.







Whatever is the suchness of the Tathāgata and whatever is the suchness of all phenomena, that is the suchness of the elder Subhūti. The elder Subhūti has followed after that very suchness; therefore he was born following after the Tathāgata. Yet that suchness of the Tathāgata is not itself something other, being suchness—it is simply that which follows after that suchness. Thus the elder Subhūti was born following after the Tathāgata. It is by that abiding which is the abiding in the suchness of the Tathāgata that the elder Subhūti was born following after the Tathāgata.

Just as the suchness of the Tathāgata is without change, without alteration, without conceptualization, and without discursive thought, so too the suchness of the elder Subhūti is without change, without alteration, without conceptualization, and without discursive thought. Thus the elder Subhūti too was born following after the Tathāgata through that suchness which is without change, without alteration, without conceptualization, and without discursive thought.

Just as the suchness of the Tathāgata is without change, without alteration, without conceptualization, without discursive thought, and unobstructed with regard to anything whatsoever, so too the suchness of all phenomena is without change, without alteration, without conceptualization, without discursive thought, and unobstructed with regard to anything whatsoever. Why is this? Because this suchness—whatever is the suchness of the Tathāgata and whatever is the suchness of all phenomena—is one; it is nondual and cannot be made into two. The suchness that is nondual is not suchness anywhere, nor from anything, nor of anything; and precisely because it is not the suchness of anything, that suchness is nondual, and the nondual suchness cannot be made into two.

Thus the elder Subhūti was born following after the Tathāgata through an unfabricated suchness, for whatever unfabricated suchness there is, it can never become other than suchness. And because it can never become other than suchness, that suchness is nondual, and the nondual suchness cannot be made into two. Thus the elder Subhūti was born following after the Tathāgata.

Just as the suchness of the Tathāgata in every respect does not conceptualize or discursively think about any phenomenon whatsoever, so too the suchness of the elder Subhūti in every respect does not conceptualize or discursively think about any phenomenon whatsoever. Likewise, the Tathāgata and Subhūti are both manifestly emanated through suchness; neither of the two is fabricated, and they are not different, since nothing that would differentiate them can be found. Thus the elder Subhūti was born following after the Tathāgata.

Just as the suchness of the Tathāgata is not other than the suchness of all phenomena, so too the suchness of Subhūti is not other than the suchness of all phenomena. And whatever is not other than the suchness of all phenomena can never fail to be the suchness of anything—for that very suchness is the suchness of all phenomena.





When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One said this to venerable Śāriputra: "Śāriputra, it is so, it is just so. Śāriputra, take the case of a bodhisattva great being who, having generated the mind set upon unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, dwells for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River, giving gifts, guarding moral discipline, accomplishing patience, exerting diligence, and resting in meditative concentration. Even though he thereby enters vastly and genuinely into practice, and his mind becomes greatly set upon manifestly and completely awakening to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, if this Perfection of Wisdom is not fully grasped and he remains devoid of skillful means, then he falls, in just this way, to the level of a śrāvaka or the level of a pratyekabuddha.

"Śāriputra, furthermore, take the bodhisattva who, in regard to the moral discipline, the meditative concentration, the wisdom, the liberation, and the vision of the wisdom of liberation of the buddhas, the Blessed Ones — past, future, and present — thinks about and grasps these in the manner of signs. Such a one does not know and does not see the moral discipline of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas; it is not meditative concentration, not wisdom, not liberation; he does not know and does not see the vision of the wisdom of liberation of the Tathāgatas, the arhats, the perfectly complete buddhas. Because he does not know this and does not see this, he listens to the sound of the word 'emptiness,' and he treats that sound as a sign; having made that sound into a sign, he wishes to dedicate it fully to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. For this reason, one should understand that this one comes to abide on the level of a śrāvaka or on the level of a pratyekabuddha. Why is that? Śāriputra, whoever is not grasped by the Perfection of Wisdom and remains devoid of skillful means will indeed turn out this way."

Śāriputra said: "Blessed One, as I understand the meaning of what the Blessed One has explained, even a bodhisattva who possesses an extremely vast accumulation of merit, if he is not grasped by the Perfection of Wisdom and remains devoid of skillful means and lacking a spiritual friend, will become uncertain of attaining unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Blessed One, for this reason, the bodhisattva great being who wishes to manifestly and completely awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening should cultivate the Perfection of Wisdom and should also strive in skillful means."

When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One said this to venerable Śāriputra: "Śāriputra, it is so, it is just so. Śāriputra, take the bodhisattva great being who is not grasped by the Perfection of Wisdom and remains devoid of skillful means: even though he possesses a great accumulation of merit, if he is devoid of a spiritual friend, his attainment of unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening becomes uncertain. Śāriputra, for this reason, the bodhisattva great being who wishes to manifestly and completely awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening should cultivate the Perfection of Wisdom and should also be endowed with skillful means."

Then Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, together with the young gods of the Desire Realm, and Brahmā, lord of this world system of Sahā, together with the young gods of the Form Realm, said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, this Perfection of Wisdom is profound."



Why is this? Venerable Subhūti, it is because all phenomena are equal to space. Venerable Subhūti, if it were established that unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening will arise, then bodhisattvas as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River would become irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Venerable Subhūti, but since bodhisattvas as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River do turn back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, therefore, Venerable Subhūti, it should be understood in this way: that the arising of unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening is difficult, and that fully awakening into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening is supremely difficult to accomplish.

When this had been said, the venerable Subhūti spoke thus to the venerable Śāriputra:

Venerable Śāriputra, does whatever form there is turn back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, does that phenomenon by which one turns back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening exist somewhere other than form?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, do whatever feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness there are turn back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, they do not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, does that phenomenon by which one turns back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening exist somewhere other than feeling, perception, and formations, or somewhere other than consciousness?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, does the suchness of form turn back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, do the suchness of feeling, the suchness of perception, the suchness of formations, and the suchness of consciousness turn back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, they do not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, does that phenomenon by which one turns back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening exist somewhere other than the suchness of form?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, does that phenomenon by which one turns back from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening exist somewhere other than the suchness of feeling, the suchness of perception, and the suchness of formations, or somewhere other than the suchness of consciousness?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, does whatever form there is fully and completely awaken into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, it does not.

Subhūti said: Venerable Śāriputra, do whatever feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness there are fully and completely awaken into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?

Śāriputra said: Venerable Subhūti, they do not.





Śāriputra said, "That is not so, venerable Subhūti."

Subhūti said, "Venerable Śāriputra, do you correctly perceive even a single quality of a bodhisattva within suchness?"

Śāriputra said, "That is not so, venerable Subhūti."

Subhūti said, "Venerable Śāriputra, since it is thus—since even that quality of a bodhisattva is not observed as truth itself or as abiding itself—why do you think, 'This one belongs to the vehicle of the śrāvakas, this one belongs to the vehicle of the pratyekabuddhas, this one belongs to the Great Vehicle'? Śāriputra, when a bodhisattva great being hears that the analyses made regarding suchness are without distinction, without differentiation, and cannot be separated out, and yet his mind does not sink, does not become discouraged in any way, and does not turn away, that bodhisattva great being should be known as certain to emerge into awakening."

Then the Blessed One said this to venerable Subhūti: "Subhūti, it is so, it is just so. Subhūti, that you have the confidence to speak in this way is due to the power of the Tathāgata and the blessing of the Buddha. Excellent, Subhūti, excellent! When those bodhisattvas, having heard that the analyses made regarding suchness are without distinction, without differentiation, and without anything to be separated out, do not have their minds sink, do not become discouraged in any way, and do not turn away, Subhūti, that bodhisattva great being should be known as certain to emerge into awakening."

Then venerable Śāriputra said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, by what awakening does this bodhisattva great being come to emerge with certainty?"

The Blessed One declared: "Śāriputra, it is by unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening that this bodhisattva great being comes to emerge with certainty."

Then venerable Subhūti said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, since a bodhisattva great being wishes to emerge with certainty into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, how should he abide, how should he train?"

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, since a bodhisattva great being wishes to emerge with certainty into unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, he should abide equally toward all sentient beings; he should generate a mind of equality toward all sentient beings; he should observe others with a mind free of inequality; he should observe others with a mind of loving-kindness; he should observe others with a mind that benefits; he should observe others with a virtuous mind; he should observe others with a mind that has cast off pride; he should observe others with a mind free of hostility; he should observe others with a mind free of harmfulness; he should observe others with a mind free of malice. Toward all sentient beings he should establish the perception of a mother, the perception of a father, the perception of a son, and the perception of a daughter, and observe others accordingly.

"Subhūti, thus, since a bodhisattva great being wishes to fully and completely awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, he should abide toward all sentient beings in this way, and he should train in this way: 'May I become the protector of all sentient beings.' He himself should also abide turned away from all wrongdoing."





























Furthermore, Subhūti, in whatever way the bodhisattva mahāsattva examines all phenomena individually, in just that way does the bodhisattva mahāsattva become inseparable from the perfection of wisdom. Furthermore, Subhūti, in whatever way the bodhisattva mahāsattva becomes inseparable from the perfection of wisdom, in just that way does he generate immeasurable and countless merit.

Subhūti asked, "Blessed One, what distinction or difference is to be made between 'immeasurable' and 'countless'?"

The Blessed One declared, "Subhūti, 'immeasurable' means that to which valid cognition does not extend. Subhūti, 'countless' means that which cannot be exhausted by counting."

Subhūti said, "Blessed One, is there a synonym by which form too becomes immeasurable? Likewise for feeling, perception, and formations. And Blessed One, is there a synonym by which consciousness too becomes immeasurable?"

The Blessed One declared, "Subhūti, as for what you have said—'Blessed One, is there a synonym by which form too becomes immeasurable? Likewise for feeling, perception, and formations,

"Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly awakened Buddha, has taught even this extent of the nature of reality of all phenomena, and yet that very nature of reality of all phenomena is inexpressible—how marvelous! Blessed One, insofar as I have understood the meaning of what the Blessed One has taught, in just that way all phenomena are indeed inexpressible."

The Blessed One declared, "So it is, Subhūti, so it is. Subhūti, all phenomena are indeed inexpressible. Why is this? Subhūti, because the emptiness of all phenomena cannot be expressed."

Subhūti said, "Blessed One, is there increase or decrease with respect to that which is inexpressible in meaning?"

The Blessed One declared, "Subhūti, there is not."

Subhūti said, "Blessed One, if there is no increase and no decrease with respect to that which is inexpressible in meaning, then there would also be no increase and no decrease with respect to the perfection of generosity. Likewise, Blessed One, there would be no increase and no decrease with respect to the perfection of moral discipline, the perfection of patience, the perfection of diligence, the perfection of meditative concentration, and the perfection of wisdom. Blessed One, if there is no increase and no decrease with respect to these six perfections, then, Blessed One, how could it be that, by the power of these six perfections which do not increase, a bodhisattva great being would come to fully awaken to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening? Or how would one draw near to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening? Blessed One, without perfecting the perfections, a bodhisattva great being does not draw near to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

The Blessed One declared, "Subhūti, so it is, so it is. Subhūti, although there is indeed no increase or decrease with respect to the meaning of the perfections, nevertheless, Subhūti, a bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom and cultivates the perfection of wisdom skillfully in means does not think, 'This perfection of generosity is increasing' or 'This perfection of generosity is decreasing.' Rather, that one thinks, 'This term "perfection of generosity" is a mere name,' and having considered this, dedicates those acts of giving that are given, those attentions, those generations of mind, and those roots of virtue fully toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, dedicating them in accordance with the nature of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening itself. Subhūti, furthermore, a bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom and cultivates the perfection of wisdom skillfully in means does not think, 'This perfection of moral discipline is increasing.'

Nor does that one think, "This perfection of moral discipline is decreasing." Rather, that one thinks, "This term 'perfection of moral discipline' is a mere name," and having considered this, having properly undertaken that moral discipline and abiding in it, dedicates those attentions, those generations of mind, and those roots of virtue fully toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, dedicating them in accordance with the nature of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening itself.

Subhūti, furthermore, a bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom and cultivates the perfection of wisdom skillfully in means does not think, "This perfection of patience is increasing." Nor does that one think, "This perfection of patience is decreasing." Rather, that one thinks, "This term 'perfection of patience' is a mere name," and having considered this, accomplishing that patience, dedicates those attentions, those generations of mind, and those roots of virtue fully toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, dedicating them in accordance with the nature of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening itself.

Subhūti, furthermore, a bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom and cultivates the perfection of wisdom skillfully in means does not think, "This perfection of diligence is increasing." Nor does that one think, "This perfection of diligence is decreasing." Rather, that one thinks, "This term 'perfection of diligence' is a mere name," and having considered this, engaging in that diligence, dedicates those attentions, those generations of mind, and those roots of virtue fully toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, dedicating them in accordance with the nature of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening itself.

Subhūti, furthermore, a bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom and cultivates the perfection of wisdom skillfully in means does not think, "This perfection of meditative concentration is increasing." Nor does that one think, "This perfection of meditative concentration is decreasing." Rather, that one thinks, "This term 'perfection of meditative concentration' is a mere name," and having considered this, settling into equipoise even within those concentrations, dedicates those attentions, those generations of mind, and those roots of virtue fully toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, dedicating them in accordance with the nature of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening itself.

Subhūti, furthermore, a bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom and cultivates the perfection of wisdom skillfully in means does not think, "This perfection of wisdom is increasing." Nor does that one think, "This perfection of wisdom is decreasing." Rather, that one thinks, "This perfection of wisdom is a mere name," and having considered this, practicing and cultivating that perfection of wisdom, dedicates those attentions, those generations of mind, and those roots of virtue fully toward unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, dedicating them in accordance with the nature of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening itself.





The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—does mind exist in suchness?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, it does not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—is mind suchness?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, it is not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—does mind exist apart from suchness?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, it does not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, have you truly seen suchness?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, I have not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—does one who practices in that way practice in what is profound?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, one who practices in that way does not practice in anything at all. Why is that? Because in that regard the modes of engagement do not operate, and there is no engagement whatsoever."

The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, that bodhisattva great being who practices the perfection of wisdom—in what does he practice?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, he practices in the ultimate." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—does that bodhisattva great being who practices in the ultimate practice in signs?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, he does not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—does he thereby destroy those signs?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, he does not." The Blessed One declared: "Subhūti, what do you think—does the bodhisattva great being's practice of the perfection of wisdom result in the destruction of signs?" Subhūti replied: "Blessed One, when that bodhisattva great being practices the conduct of a bodhisattva, he does not apply himself thinking, 'May I attain the abandonment of signs right here.' For if he were to attain it, since all the qualities of a buddha would not be complete, he would become a śrāvaka instead. Blessed One, that bodhisattva great being knows what the defining characteristic is and what the sign characterized by it is, and yet also becomes thoroughly familiar with the absence of that sign—this is the skillful means of the bodhisattva great being."

Then the venerable Śāriputra said this to the venerable Subhūti: "Venerable Subhūti, if a bodhisattva great being cultivates in a dream the three doors of liberation—emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness—does his perfection of wisdom thereby increase?" Subhūti said: "Venerable Śāriputra, if cultivating during the day causes increase, then it also increases in a dream. Why is that? Venerable Śāriputra, the Blessed One has said that dream and waking day are not to be conceptually distinguished. Venerable Śāriputra, if a bodhisattva great being who has found the perfection of wisdom practices the perfection of wisdom continuously, day after day, then at that time, for that bodhisattva great being who has become habituated to the perfection of wisdom, the perfection of wisdom will expand even in his dreams."

Śāriputra said: "Venerable Subhūti, if any man or woman performs a virtuous or non-virtuous action in a dream, does that action accumulate or approach accumulation?" Subhūti said:







Śāriputra, furthermore, if a bodhisattva great being goes into a wilderness where there is drought, he should not be alarmed, should not be utterly terrified, should not fall into utter terror. Why is this? Because a bodhisattva great being is one whose nature it is not to be alarmed and not to be utterly terrified. He should generate this thought: "I shall train so as to cut off the craving of all sentient beings." Even if he thinks, "Should I come to die of thirst, I will be born into the world of hungry ghosts," the bodhisattva great being should still not be alarmed, should not be utterly terrified, should not fall into utter terror because of this. Rather he should think: "Alas, these beings have but little merit, since in this world such things as this drought appear to them," and toward all sentient beings he should generate a mind of great compassion, thinking: "Just as, when I have become a perfect and complete buddha in unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, in that buddha-field of mine such droughts will never arise anywhere, in any form, at any time, and will not even be known—so shall I bring it about. Just as all sentient beings will find water endowed with the eight qualities, so shall I hold to merit for that purpose. Just as at that time my own perfection of diligence will become completely fulfilled, so, for the sake of all sentient beings, I shall firmly undertake diligence."

Śāriputra, furthermore, if a bodhisattva great being goes into a wilderness of famine, he should not be alarmed, should not be utterly terrified, should not fall into utter terror. He should put on the armor of this thought: "Just as, when I have become a perfect and complete buddha in unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, in that buddha-field of mine such famines will never arise anywhere, in any form, at any time, and will not even be known, and all those beings will be joyful and happy and will meet with every happiness—so shall I firmly undertake diligence. Thus shall I thoroughly purify a buddha-field. Just as whatever aspiration those beings have, and whatever they think in their minds, will be accomplished exactly as thought—just as, for example, by the mere mental wish of the gods of the Thirty-Three, everything arises—so, by the mere mental wish of those beings, all of it shall be accomplished, and everything wished for in the mind shall arise. Just as at that time my own perfection of meditative concentration will become completely fulfilled, so, for the sake of all sentient beings, I shall strive to purify my own mind completely." Thinking thus, and thinking, "Just as the Dharma-aspiration of those beings will become completely fulfilled, and the requisites of livelihood for all sentient beings will never, at any time, in any way, be lacking"—so he firmly undertakes diligence.

Śāriputra, furthermore, if a bodhisattva great being goes into a wilderness that has become a place of disease, he should not be alarmed, should not be utterly terrified, should not fall into utter terror. He should investigate, reflect, and examine in this way: "Here there is no phenomenon that causes illness, nor is there any phenomenon called 'illness.'" Thus he should examine it as emptiness, and should not be alarmed, should not be utterly terrified, should not fall into utter terror.

Śāriputra, a bodhisattva great being is one who—



















Subhūti, when a bodhisattva great being who wishes to fully and manifestly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening is asked in this way by another bodhisattva great being, "What is it that one should become thoroughly familiar with regarding these teachings—namely, not actualizing emptiness, not actualizing signlessness, not actualizing wishlessness, not actualizing non-formation, not actualizing non-arising, not actualizing non-production, and also not actualizing non-existence—while also cultivating the perfection of wisdom? And when accomplishing which minds, in accordance with what nature of mind should one accomplish them?"—one should ask this.

Suppose, Subhūti, that when a bodhisattva great being is asked this by another bodhisattva great being, he explains in reply, "A bodhisattva great being should attend mentally to emptiness. He should attend mentally to signlessness, wishlessness, non-formation, non-occurrence, non-arising, and non-existence itself." If he does not directly teach the mind that does not abandon all sentient beings, or does not skillfully explain this in reply, then, Subhūti, one should know that this bodhisattva great being has not been prophesied by the tathāgatas, arhats, perfectly complete buddhas of the past to be irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.

Why is this? Because he does not teach, does not analyze, does not directly explain, and does not fully understand the unshared qualities that belong to an irreversible bodhisattva great being; and because, even when questioned, he does not explain this in reply and does not give an answer—thus he fails to attain the ground that belongs to an irreversible bodhisattva great being.

Subhūti asked: Blessed One, is there some further way, some further explanation, by which a bodhisattva great being becomes irreversible?

The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, there is indeed a way by which a bodhisattva great being becomes irreversible. Whether or not a bodhisattva great being has heard this perfection of wisdom, if he understands in this way and answers in this way, he should be known as an irreversible bodhisattva great being.

Subhūti asked: Blessed One, for this reason, though many practice toward awakening, few indeed are those who answer in this way.

The Blessed One declared: Subhūti, it is just as you say—few indeed are those bodhisattva great beings who are prophesied to the stage of irreversible wisdom; yet those who have been so prophesied answer in exactly this way. One should know that these various bodhisattva great beings have kindled and generated roots of virtue, and that these various bodhisattva great beings cannot be seized by the world together with its gods, humans, and asuras.

Furthermore, Subhūti, if a bodhisattva great being, even within a dream, correctly views all phenomena as like a dream and does not actualize them, then, Subhūti, this too should be recognized as a defining characteristic of the irreversibility of an irreversible bodhisattva great being.

Furthermore, Subhūti, if a bodhisattva great being, even within a dream, has no desire for the level of a śrāvaka or the level of a pratyekabuddha or for the three realms, and does not generate a mind that seeks their advantages, then, Subhūti, this too should be recognized as a defining characteristic of the irreversibility of an irreversible bodhisattva great being.

Furthermore, Subhūti, if a bodhisattva great being, even within a dream, finds himself in the midst of an assembly of many hundreds, many thousands, many hundreds of thousands, many hundreds of millions, many thousands of millions, many hundreds of thousands of millions, or many hundreds of thousands of tens of millions of beings, and sits within the courtyard of that assembly's maṇḍala, surrounded on all sides by an assembly of monks and encircled in front by an assembly of bodhisattvas, and sees himself, having become a Tathāgata, an Arhat, a perfectly complete Buddha, teaching the Dharma, then, Subhūti, this too should be recognized as a defining characteristic of the irreversibility of an irreversible bodhisattva great being.

Furthermore, Subhūti, if, even within the dream of a bodhisattva great being, he finds himself rising up into the sky and teaching the Dharma to beings, and knows fully that his light fills a fathom's span, and goes to other directions, and having performed the deeds of a buddha in those other world systems, there too emanates monks and teaches the Dharma — Subhūti, if in this way, even within a dream, he is fully recognized as an irreversible bodhisattva great being, then, Subhūti, this too should be recognized as a defining characteristic of the irreversibility of an irreversible bodhisattva great being.
