Scatter them also upon the bodhisattva great being Dharmodgata, who is teaching the Dharma while seated on the Dharma throne." Having said this, he gave her a thousand divine loads of divine māndārava flowers.

Then the bodhisattva great being Sadāprarudita (Ever-Weeping) took those flowers. With some of the flowers he scattered flowers upon that ground, and with some of the flowers he scattered them upon the bodhisattva great being Dharmodgata.

Then the bodhisattva great being Dharmodgata, after seven years had passed, rose from that meditative absorption and came to where the Dharma seat was, and sat down upon the seat that had been spread out. Surrounded by many hundreds of thousands in his retinue, who gazed upon him from the front, he taught the Perfection of Wisdom.

Then, the moment the bodhisattva great being Sadāprarudita saw the bodhisattva great being Dharmodgata, he attained a happiness comparable to that of a monk who has entered the first meditative concentration and applies his mind single-pointedly.

At that time, the bodhisattva great being Dharmodgata's teaching of the Perfection of Wisdom was as follows:

Since all phenomena are equal, the Perfection of Wisdom is equality itself.
Since all phenomena are isolated, the Perfection of Wisdom is isolation itself.
Since all phenomena are unmoving, the Perfection of Wisdom is unmoving itself.
Since all phenomena are without conceit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without conceit itself.
Since all phenomena are without pride, the Perfection of Wisdom is without pride itself.
Since all phenomena have a single taste, the Perfection of Wisdom has a single taste.
Since all phenomena are without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since all phenomena are unborn, the Perfection of Wisdom is unborn.
Since all phenomena are unceasing, the Perfection of Wisdom is unceasing.
Since space is without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since the ocean is without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since Mount Sumeru is manifold, the Perfection of Wisdom is manifold.
Since space is without conceptualization, the Perfection of Wisdom is without conceptualization.
Since form is without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Likewise, since feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since the earth element is without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Likewise, since the water element, the fire element, and the wind element are without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since the space element is without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since the consciousness element is without limit, the Perfection of Wisdom is without limit.
Since phenomena that are like vajras are equal, the Perfection of Wisdom is equal.
Since all phenomena are unmixed, the Perfection of Wisdom is unmixed.
Since all phenomena are unobservable, the Perfection of Wisdom is unobservable.
Since the destruction of all phenomena is equal, the Perfection of Wisdom is equal in destruction.
Since all phenomena are without agency, the Perfection of Wisdom is without agency.

Since all phenomena are inconceivable, the Perfection of Wisdom should be understood to be inconceivable. This is what is meant. Then, while the bodhisattva great being Sadāprarudita (Ever Weeping) remained in that state, at that time the king of absorptions called Equality of All Phenomena arose in him. Together with it there arose the absorption called Solitude of All Phenomena, the absorption called Immovability of All Phenomena, the absorption called Freedom from Conceit in All Phenomena, the absorption called Freedom from Inflation in All Phenomena, the absorption called One Taste of All Phenomena, the absorption called Boundlessness of All Phenomena, the absorption called Non-arising of All Phenomena, the absorption called Non-cessation of All Phenomena, the absorption called Boundlessness of Space, the absorption called Boundlessness of the Ocean, the absorption called Manifold Mount Meru, the absorption called Non-conceptuality of Space, the absorption called Boundlessness of Form, and likewise the absorptions called Boundlessness of Feeling, Perception, Formations, and Consciousness, the absorption called Boundlessness of the Earth Element, and likewise the absorptions called Boundlessness of the Water Element, the Fire Element, and the Wind Element, the absorption called Boundlessness of the Space Element, the absorption called Boundlessness of the Consciousness Element, the absorption called Vajra-like, the absorption called Unmixedness of All Phenomena, the absorption called Unobservability of All Phenomena, the absorption called Equality of the Destruction of All Phenomena, the absorption called Absence of Agency in All Phenomena, and the absorption called Inconceivability of All Phenomena. Thus the bodhisattva great being Sadāprarudita fully attained sixty thousand doors of absorption of this kind and more.

This concludes the thirty-first chapter, called "The Chapter on the Noble Dharma," from the noble Eight Thousand Line Perfection of Wisdom.

Subhūti, immediately upon attaining the sixty thousand doors of absorption, the bodhisattva great being Sadāprarudita saw, in the eastern direction, the southern direction, the western direction, the northern direction, the intermediate directions, the direction below, the direction above, and throughout the world systems of the ten directions—as many great trichiliocosms as there are grains of sand in the Ganges River—buddhas, blessed ones, as numerous as the grains of sand in the Ganges River, each surrounded by an assembly of monks and attended before by a host of bodhisattvas, teaching this very Perfection of Wisdom in this same manner, with this same name, and in these same words. It was just as if, right now, in this very great trichiliocosm world system where I dwell, the Teacher, surrounded by an assembly of monks and attended before by a host of bodhisattvas, were teaching this Perfection of Wisdom in this same manner, with this same name, and in these same words. Thus he became possessed of inconceivably vast learning and an ocean of what he had heard, and wherever he was born, he was never again separated from the buddhas. In every rebirth, he was born in whatever place the blessed buddhas became directly present to him, and even in his dreams he was never separated from the blessed buddhas.

By that, he abandoned all unfavorable states and accomplished every excellent form of leisure. Then the Blessed One said to venerable Ānanda, "Ānanda, by this account as well you should know that this Perfection of Wisdom accomplishes the wisdom of omniscience for bodhisattva great beings. Ānanda, therefore, bodhisattva great beings who wish to attain the wisdom of omniscience should practice this Perfection of Wisdom. This Perfection of Wisdom should be listened to, grasped, held in mind, read, mastered, recited aloud, taught, explained, transmitted, chanted, and written down. Through the blessing of the Tathāgata, having written the letters clearly and vividly upon a great volume, one should honor, esteem, revere, worship, and venerate it with flowers, incense, perfume, garlands, unguents, powders, music, garments, parasols, victory banners, bells, banners, and rows of lamps encircling all around, and with many kinds of offerings. Ānanda, this is my instruction to be followed after me. Why is this? Because from this Perfection of Wisdom the wisdom of omniscience becomes fully perfected. Ānanda, what do you think—is the Tathāgata your Teacher?" Ānanda replied, "Blessed One, he is my Teacher. Sugata (the 'Well-Gone One') is my Teacher." When he had spoken thus, the Blessed One said this to venerable Ānanda: "Ānanda, toward the Tathāgata who is your Teacher, you have rendered service through bodily acts of kindness that are pleasing, verbal acts of kindness that are pleasing, and mental acts of kindness that are pleasing. Therefore, Ānanda, just as you have shown joy, clarity, faith, and reverence toward this body of mine as I now dwell, live, and remain, so too, Ānanda, after I have passed away, act likewise toward this Perfection of Wisdom. Ānanda, so that this Perfection of Wisdom may never decline, and so that you may not become the last of persons, I entrust it to you, Ānanda, twice and thrice—I entrust it to you as a legacy. Ānanda, you should know that for as long as this Perfection of Wisdom is actively practiced in the world, the Tathāgata remains present. Ānanda, you should know that for that long, the Tathāgata is teaching the Dharma. Ānanda, those sentient beings who listen to this Perfection of Wisdom, grasp it, hold it, read it, master it, recite it aloud, teach it, explain it, transmit it, chant it, write it down, and who honor, esteem, revere, worship, and venerate it with flowers, incense, perfume, garlands, unguents, powders, garments, parasols, victory banners, bells, banners, and rows of lamps encircling all around, and with many kinds of offerings—those beings should be known to be inseparable from seeing the buddhas, from hearing the sacred Dharma, and from attending upon the Saṅgha. Ānanda, those sentient beings should be known to be dwelling in the presence of the Tathāgata." The Blessed One, rejoicing, spoke thus.

The bodhisattva great beings Maitreya and the others, along with the venerable Subhūti, the venerable Śāriputra, the venerable Ānanda, Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, and the world with its gods, humans, asuras, and gandharvas, all rejoiced and highly praised what the Blessed One had taught.

This concludes the thirty-second chapter, called "The Chapter on Complete Entrustment," from the Noble Eight Thousand Line Perfection of Wisdom.

The Blessed Mother, the Eight Thousand Line Perfection of Wisdom, is complete.

It was translated, edited, and finalized by the Indian preceptors Śākyasena and Jñānasiddhi together with the chief editor-translator, the monk Dharmaśīla, and others. Later, by command of the true sovereign, the divine ruler of Tibet, the auspicious divine prince and ruler Lhasde Tsen, the Indian preceptor Subhāṣita and the translator-editor, the monk Rinchen Zangpo, revised the translation to accord with the commentary. Subsequently the great Indian preceptor and paṇḍita Dīpaṃkaraśrījñāna, together with the chief editor-translator, the monk Rinchen Zangpo, checked it against the central Indian commentary, corrected and edited it, and established the final text. Later still, at Nyethang in lower Kyi, the great paṇḍita Dīpaṃkaraśrījñāna and the translator Dromtön Gyalwai Jungne, on the occasion of teaching the Eight Thousand Lines, established a somewhat abbreviated version. Afterward, at the temple of Radreng, the translator Dromtön Gyalwai Jungne checked it against three Indian sūtra texts and established it twice more. Later that same translator gave explanations and also established minor points in detail. In more recent times, the great translator, the Śākya monk Ngok Loden Sherap, gathered many Kashmiri and central Indian manuscripts and established the definitive text.

Here ends the Various [Perfection of] Wisdom [texts].

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

The phrase rendered "thoroughbreds, great elephants" translates two well-known arhat epithets: "thoroughbred" is a standard rendering of the Tibetan "thoroughbred horse," referring to those of sharp spiritual capacity, and "great elephant" denotes those of great spiritual strength and endurance. Both are stock epithets in canonical arhat lists.

The exception noted — "but one exception, namely the Venerable Ānanda" — is significant doctrinally: Ānanda, the Buddha's personal attendant and memorizer of discourses, had not yet attained arhatship at this time, and the text singles him out as the lone non-arhat in the assembly.

The final sentence, in which Subhūti says he does not see the phenomenon designated by "bodhisattva," opens the central Perfection of Wisdom theme of the unreality of all dharmas, including the very agent of the bodhisattva path. The Tibetan "yang dag par rjes su ma mthong" literally means "I do not rightly see following after" — idiomatically, "I do not find upon examination" or "I do not perceive as truly existent."  The phrase rendered "does not make even the awakening mind itself into an object of conceit" translates the Tibetan literally: the bodhisattva does not generate pride or self-congratulatory thought (rlomsems) even with respect to bodhicitta. This is a subtle but important point: even the highest motivation is not to be reified or clung to with conceit.

The celebrated exchange between Subhūti and Śāriputra on the nature of no-mind follows the classical Perfection of Wisdom dialectic: any predicate — existence or non-existence — applied to the mind's ultimate nature is itself undermined, since the very ground on which such a question rests is empty. The rendering "no-mind" for sems med pa follows the text's own usage and the tradition's identification of it with the luminous, unconstructed nature of mind, not a mere absence.  The phrase rendered "abiding well, in the manner of non-abiding" translates a deliberately paradoxical formulation central to Prajñāpāramitā teaching: the irreversible bodhisattva "abides" on their stage precisely by not grasping at any fixed abiding — a doctrinal point worth preserving literally rather than smoothing over.

Subhūti's declaration that he cannot "find" the bodhisattva or the Perfection of Wisdom is a classic Prajñāpāramitā move: the very terms used in the instruction are empty of findable referents, yet the instruction proceeds — the tension is intentional and doctrinally significant.  The name "Kuntu Gyupa Threngwacan" is a proper name retained in translation form: it means roughly "the Wandering Garland-Wearer" or "the Ever-Roaming One with a Garland." This appears to be the name of a specific bodhisattva or figure addressed in this sūtra context. The Tibetan ཀུན་ཏུ་རྒྱུ་ཕྲེང་བ་ཅན could correspond to a Sanskrit name such as Nityaparivrāja or Nityagranthin; the identification is uncertain from this passage alone and a reader should be aware this is a proper name, not a descriptive phrase being rendered into English. The term is left transliterated here as a name rather than translated into English description, to preserve consistency and avoid a misleading generic reading.  The phrase rendered "does not pass into nirvāṇa before they are fully perfected" translates a construction indicating that the bodhisattva does not take an early nirvāṇa — a Foundational Vehicle nirvāṇa — while the Tathāgata's qualities remain incomplete in him. This is the standard Mahāyāna doctrinal point distinguishing the bodhisattva's path from that of the śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha.

The series "does not grow despondent, does not lose heart, feels no embarrassment, does not become dismayed" renders a cluster of Tibetan terms describing psychological steadiness in the face of the radical emptiness teaching; the precise sequence of these terms varies slightly across recensions.  The passage breaks off mid-sentence at the end of the supplied Tibetan, ending with "does not practice with regard to the arising of consciousness." This is simply where the provided source text ends; the sentence continues in the next passage. The translation reflects this open structure faithfully.

The term rendered throughout as "sign" (Skt. nimitta) is central here: it denotes any object grasped as a fixed, inherently characterized thing. Practicing "with regard to a sign" means engaging with a reified, conceptually marked object — the opposite of the signless engagement the Perfection of Wisdom requires. This is standard Prajñāpāramitā doctrine and no gloss was inserted into the body to keep the prose clean for narration.  The phrase rendered as 'does not know it rightly' translates a doubled expression meaning roughly 'does not know, does not know fully or correctly' — a rhetorical intensification common in Perfection of Wisdom literature, indicating that even the knowing of the absorption falls away, because the absorption has no findable object. This is not mere ignorance but the transcendence of a knowing subject grasping an object.

The closing line is cut off mid-sentence in the supplied passage and has been translated only to the point the Tibetan text provided.  The phrase rendered "they exist truly in the way that they do not exist" reflects a deliberately paradoxical formulation in the Tibetan: phenomena are said to exist correctly precisely as what is absent or void of inherent existence — their mode of being is their very emptiness. This is a key Perfection of Wisdom dialectical move and the phrasing is intentionally preserved rather than smoothed over.

The term "childlike ordinary persons without learning" renders the compound for uninstructed worldlings who have not heard the Dharma, distinguished here sharply from noble ones who see reality directly.  The phrase rendered as "the five aggregates together with the six sense faculties" translates a somewhat compressed Tibetan formulation in which Subhūti appears to equate "form" and "consciousness" with both the six sense faculties and the five aggregates together. This is doctrinally unusual but follows the text as given; the six sense faculties are included perhaps because they are the basis of the appropriating consciousness and are subsumed under the form aggregate in Abhidharma analysis.

The final sentence ends mid-construction in the Tibetan, trailing with a connective particle, indicating the passage continues beyond what is given here.  The phrase rendered "the underlying meaning of bodhisattva is that there is no underlying meaning" reflects a deliberate doctrinal paradox in the text: the Tibetan gzhi'i don med pa, literally "the foundational meaning is non-existent/absence of foundation," points to the emptiness of inherent reference in the term itself, even as the passage goes on to give conventional explanatory glosses. This is an intentional tension in the source and not a textual error.

The phrase "the mind that is equal to the unequalled" renders mnyam pa med pa dang mnyam pa'i sems, a standard Mahāyāna epithet for the bodhicitta or the buddha-mind: it is without equal, yet in being so it is said to be "equal" to other things likewise beyond compare, such as nirvāṇa.  The phrase rendered "mind is without mind" translates sems med pa, which the tradition reads as pointing to the fact that mind, in its ultimate nature, is beyond the reification of "mind" as a substantial entity — it lacks inherent existence as "mind." This is a classical Prajñāpāramitā formulation: the very mind that is praised is empty of being a findable mind, and thus there is no real object of clinging. The argument is deliberately paradoxical in the manner characteristic of this literature.

The epithet rendered "son of Maitrā" for Maitreya (Tibetan Byams pa, Pali Metteyya) follows the Tibetan gang po byams ma'i bu, "son of Maitrā," understood as a matronymic; this is consistent with Sanskrit sources that give his mother's name as Maitrī or Maitrā.  The interlocutor's name rendered as Gaṅgaputra Maitrāyaṇīputra follows the Tibetan གང་པོ་བྱམས་མའི་བུ, which is a compound of Gaṅgaputra ("son of Gaṅgā") and Maitrāyaṇīputra ("son of Maitrāyaṇī"), a known figure in certain Perfection of Wisdom sūtra contexts. The phrase "donned armor that is without armor" renders གོ་ཆ་མ་མཆིས་པ་བགོས་པ, literally "donned the non-existent armor" — a deliberate paradox central to the Mahāyāna Perfection of Wisdom teaching that the bodhisattva's great resolve operates without any reified referent; the rendering preserves this paradox as doctrine intends.  The phrase rendered "an armor without armor" translates a construction meaning that the great armor of the bodhisattva is, paradoxically, devoid of any substantial armor — the armor consists precisely in the absence of a graspable armor. This is a standard Perfection of Wisdom dialectical reversal and is rendered literally here as the text intends it doctrinally.  The speaker identified as "Gañgadevīputra" renders the Tibetan "gang bo byams ma'i bu," literally "son of Gaṅgādevī." This name appears in the Aṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā tradition; the rendering follows standard scholarly identification.

The triple formula "have attained / have attained / will attain" reflects the Tibetan's deliberate listing of past, present completion, and future — a standard rhetorical device affirming omniscience across the three times, consistent with the immediately preceding statement that the Great Vehicle is equal throughout the three times.  The phrase rendered "reflects closely upon" translates a term that in this Perfection of Wisdom context carries the sense of attentive, analytical contemplation — neither mere conceptual thinking nor formal meditative absorption, but the sustained scrutiny through which the bodhisattva investigates the nature of phenomena. The passage breaks off mid-sentence in the source, so the translation ends where the Tibetan ends.  The fascicle division marker appearing mid-passage indicates this is the opening of the second fascicle of the Aṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā. The preceding material closes one section; what follows from Śāriputra's question onward is the beginning of new discourse. The repetition of content regarding form and consciousness at the opening of this passage partially recapitulates the preceding translated excerpt, as the Tibetan text overlaps at the join; this rendering follows the source faithfully.

The phrase rendered "even if my body were cut into a hundred pieces and I were small and weak" is somewhat compressed in the Tibetan; the intent is that even under extreme bodily harm and in conditions of weakness, the bodhisattva generates no ill will — this is consistent with the patience literature of the Mahāyāna.  The phrase rendered "utterly empty and without object of observation" translates "thams cad med cing mi dmigs pa," literally "without anything and not apprehended/observed." This is a standard Perfection of Wisdom formulation indicating that neither the subject nor any referent object can be pinpointed; the rendering captures both the emptiness and the non-objectifiability intended by the tradition.

The final exchange turns on a subtle dialectical point: Subhūti's counter-question "can what has the nature of non-arising be arisen?" implies the two categories cannot interact, since arising and non-arising are mutually exclusive — which is itself the answer to Śāriputra's question about how attainment is possible. This is left to unfold in the text without editorial insertion.  The term rendered throughout as "without fixed abiding" translates the Tibetan phrase for non-dwelling or non-establishment, a key Perfection of Wisdom concept indicating that noble disciples and bodhisattvas do not fix upon or settle into any dharma as an ultimate resting point. The apparent paradox Śāriputra raises at the close — that inseparability from mental engagement entails separation from the Perfection of Wisdom, and vice versa — is a deliberate dialectical provocation characteristic of this literature, and no emendation is warranted.  The phrase rendered "empty of inherent content" translates a Tibetan term meaning "solitary" or "secluded" and is used here in a technical philosophical sense indicating that sentient beings are empty or devoid of any intrinsic, self-contained nature, paralleling the other negations in the series. The rendering aims to preserve this technical nuance for a listening audience without disrupting the flow.

The phrase "entered into the certainty of the real" refers to those who have taken up the fixed path of the śrāvaka vehicle toward nirvāṇa without remainder, and who are therefore regarded in Mahāyāna texts as unable to generate the bodhicitta aimed at full buddhahood. This is a standard Mahāyāna doctrinal point.  The phrase rendered "don the great armor" reflects the standard Mahāyāna idiom of "putting on the armor of the bodhisattva vow" for the benefit of all beings, a recurring technical image in Prajñāpāramitā literature. No emendation of the Tibetan is required; the image is intentionally sudden and will be elaborated in what follows.

The passage describing Subhūti's statement about repaying the Buddha's kindness contains a nuanced theological point: the śrāvakas around the bodhisattva-to-be are said to have instructed him in the perfections, which is doctrinally unusual and may reflect a specific strand of Prajñāpāramitā reflection on the role of the early Saṅgha in the Buddha's path. The translation follows the Tibetan closely without smoothing over this.  The phrase rendered "distinguished by the unconditioned" translates a construction in which a soteriological attainment is said to be "distinguished" or "set apart" by the unconditioned (Skt. asaṃskṛta). This is a standard Perfection of Wisdom formulation warning against reifying even the unconditioned as a fixed category or ground of abiding. The point is subtle: one must not cling even to the correct insight that these fruits are unconditioned.

The phrase "fitting recipient of generosity" renders a standard technical expression for one who is a worthy field of merit for giving; the text is careful to list each of the four noble persons in this connection.  The phrase rendered "knowing through non-abiding" translates mi gnas pa'i tshul gyis shes par gnas pa, a somewhat compressed formulation meaning that the bodhisattva's abiding is itself a form of cognition characterized by non-fixation; this is a subtle Madhyamaka-inflected point and the rendering attempts to preserve the paradox rather than flatten it.  The term rendered "patience" throughout is bzod pa, which in this context refers not to ordinary forbearance but to a specific spiritual attainment — the acceptance or tolerance of the non-arising of phenomena, known in Sanskrit as anutpattikadharmakṣānti, the "patient acceptance of the non-arising of dharmas." This is the crucial soteriological prerequisite being described. The text's claim that all the noble attainments from stream-entry up to full buddhahood depend on this patience points to its centrality in the Perfection of Wisdom literature. The passage ends mid-sentence in the source text, so the translation likewise concludes at that natural break.  The list of great śrāvakas corresponds to the standard group in Perfection of Wisdom sūtras: Śāriputra, Maudgalyāyana (gang bo byams ma'i bu — literally "son of Maudgalyāyanī"), Mahākāśyapa (འོད་སྲུང་ཆེན་པོ), Mahākatyāyana, and Mahākauṣṭhila (lto rings chen po — "Great Long-Belly"). The name Ānanda (kun dga' bo) introduces himself here in the expected editorial role of one who knows what was taught. The dramatic climax of Subhūti's declaration — that no one will "properly hold" the Perfection of Wisdom because nothing has been taught, posited, or made to appear — is a standard Prajñāpāramitā rhetorical paradox and is rendered as the text intends it doctrinally.  The passage presents a subtle philosophical point when Subhūti declares that what is "unborn" cannot be a flower: the argument moves from the unborn nature of the flowers (affirmed by Śakra in a Madhyamaka-inflected register) to the conclusion that "unborn" transcends even the category of "flower" as a conventional designation. This is a standard Perfection of Wisdom dialectic on designation and reality, and the translation preserves it without smoothing it over.

The phrase rendered "he teaches even what is merely designated by name and word, yet does not contradict it" reflects the Tibetan tension between conventional designation and ultimate non-arising, which Śakra admires as the hallmark of Subhūti's depth.

The list of attainments — stream-entry, once-returning, non-returning, arhathood — reflects the four fruits of the śrāvaka path; the bodhisattva's training surpasses all of them without negating conventional practice, a standard Mahāyāna doctrinal point.  The phrase rendered "whose power" and "whose blessing" translates "su'i mthu" and "su'i byin gyi rlabs" — literally "the power of whom" and "the blessing of whom." The passage plays deliberately on the ambiguity of whether the teaching flows from Subhūti's own realization or from the Buddha's blessing; Subhūti's answer resolves this by attributing both to the Tathāgata, while also claiming them as his own exposition — a subtle non-duality that is doctrinally significant in Prajñāpāramitā literature.  The passage distinguishes three closely related epithets applied to the Perfection of Wisdom: "beyond measure" (ཚད་མ་མེད་པ), "without limit" (ཚད་མེད་པ), and "of infinite extent" (མཐའ་ཡས་པ). These are near-synonymous in Tibetan but are kept distinct throughout the passage with evident intentionality, suggesting they correspond to distinct Sanskrit terms, likely apramāṇa, aparimāṇa, and ananta respectively. They have been rendered with consistent distinct English phrases throughout.  The phrase rendered "a voice of boundless reach, deep and resonant" translates a compound describing the Tathāgata's speech as having a carrying quality that surpasses limits and a deep melodious tone. The two elements are treated as a hendiadys describing the quality of the buddha's voice.

The exchange about "word-designation" plays on the Madhyamaka and Abhidharma distinction between conventional labels and any supposed real referent. The term rendered "adventitiously arisen" translates a word meaning something like "suddenly or casually imputed," indicating the designation has no grounding in an intrinsic real entity.  The phrase rendered "marketplace called Dīpaṃkara within the royal palace's surrounding city" translates a compressed Tibetan passage about the Jātaka narrative of the young brahmin Megha (here unnamed) meeting Buddha Dīpaṃkara. The Tibetan reads literally "when around the royal palace, in the midst of the marketplace called Dīpaṃkara (mar me can)." The geography here is slightly ambiguous — "mar me can" means "possessing lamps" or "having lamps" and is the standard Tibetan rendering of the name Dīpaṃkara; it refers to the city or market district named after the Buddha, not a place lit by lamps in a merely descriptive sense. The rendering reflects the narrative convention.

The epithet cluster applied to the future Śākyamuni Buddha — "endowed with knowledge and conduct" (vidyācaraṇasaṃpanna), "knower of the world" (lokavid), "unsurpassed guide of persons to be tamed" (anuttarapuruṣadamyasārathi) — follows the standard tenfold epithet list of a buddha, here given in partial form as it appears in the Tibetan.  The epithet Kauśika is a patronymic name for Śakra (Indra), standard in Perfection of Wisdom literature; it has been left in Sanskrit as is conventional. The phrase "disciplines sentient beings by the three vehicles" reflects the bodhisattva's activity of guiding beings according to the vehicle suited to each, while the wonder expressed is that this is done without reifying beings as objects of perception, consistent with Madhyamaka non-conceptualism. The phrase rendered "without having left things undone" translates a Tibetan idiom for dying prematurely or before completing one's purpose, which is a standard protective benefit formula in this genre of sūtra.  The plant name "Mā-ghi" renders the Tibetan transliteration of a Sanskrit herb name that appears as "māgadhī" or a similar form in Sanskrit parallels. The exact identification of the plant is uncertain; it is consistently described in such contexts as a universal antidote. The name is preserved here as transliterated rather than guessed at.

The final sentence breaks off mid-construction, as the Tibetan passage itself ends mid-sentence; this is a continuation passage and the predicate follows in the next section.  The ninefold list of activities related to the text — upholding, bearing, reading, thoroughly mastering, reciting aloud, teaching, explaining in detail, chanting by rote, and proclaiming — is a standard Perfection of Wisdom formula recurring throughout the text; the renderings have been kept consistent with the preceding passage.  The series of epithets for the knowledge-mantra — "without measure," "beyond reckoning," "unsurpassed," "unequalled," and "equal to the unequalled" — correspond to a well-attested fivefold vidyā-formula in Perfection of Wisdom sūtras; the distinction between "without measure" (ཚད་མ་མེད་པ, apramāṇa) and "beyond reckoning" (ཚད་མེད་པ, amita) is subtle and the Tibetan redactors treated them as distinct, so both are rendered separately here.  The sequence of questions and answers here follows the standard Perfection of Wisdom sūtra rhetorical pattern in which the questioner is turned back to answer his own comparative-merit question, and then redirected to the deeper doctrinal point — namely that the Perfection of Wisdom is the very path through which all buddhas awaken, and therefore surpasses even the merit of offering to relics. This doctrinal logic governs the passage and should be understood as the implicit answer to Śakra's comparative-merit question, which is left formally unresolved but pointed toward in the Blessed One's counter-question.  The phrase rendered "physical body" throughout translates "bdag gi dngos po'i sku," literally "the body of my substantial entity" or "my material body." This refers to the rūpakāya, the form body of the Buddha, as distinct from the dharmakāya. The contrast drawn is between the form body and omniscience as the basis for recognizing a being as a Tathāgata — a doctrinally significant point distinguishing the physical manifestation from the gnosis that truly constitutes buddhahood.

The passage ends mid-sentence in the supplied Tibetan; the translation reflects this by ending at the same point.  The opening sentences are a continuation of the speech of the interlocutor from the preceding passage, presenting rhetorical questions about whether offering to the Perfection of Wisdom is of great meaning. The Tibetan interrogative particle "lags sam" marks these as reverential questions addressed to the Blessed One. The final sentence of the translation is deliberately left open as the Tibetan passage ends mid-sentence, flowing into what follows.  The phrase rendered "inquire into it thoroughly" translates yongs su dri bar bya, literally "to ask or question thoroughly," meaning to investigate the text's meaning by questioning qualified teachers — a standard practice in the textual-study sequence. The series of verbs in the listening-through-inquiring list is a standard enumeration in Perfection of Wisdom literature for the full cycle of textual engagement.  The phrase rendered "even in a small way when young, caused this to be entered into a volume" translates a somewhat compressed Tibetan construction. The sense is that even a modest, early act of having the Perfection of Wisdom written into a book — and then carrying or keeping that book — already constitutes the basis for the meritorious worship described. The passage then escalates this merit over that gained from filling all of Jambudvīpa with relic stūpas, a standard comparative device in Prajñāpāramitā literature.  The phrase rendered "a thousand world-systems" translates the Tibetan "stong spyi phud kyi 'jig rten gyi khams," which is a standard term for a system of one thousand world-realms, the first level of the cosmic hierarchy of Buddhist cosmology. The passage breaks off here as the text continues; the comparison is clearly escalating the scale of the merit-field in stages.  The passage uses a standard Buddhist cosmological schema of progressively larger world-systems: a "thousand world-system" (Skt. sāhasrī lokadhātu) is now followed by a "two-thousand middling world-system" (Skt. dvisāhasrī madhyamā lokadhātu), the second tier in the classical three-tier cosmological hierarchy. This progression is the structural device of the entire passage and continues in subsequent sections.

The phrase rendered "masters it fully" translates kun chub par byed pa, which can mean "to fully comprehend" or "to completely master"; the sense here is comprehensive internalization, not merely physical copying.  The phrase rendered "the way of the buddhas" translates sangs rgyas kyi tshul, literally "the conduct or manner of the buddhas," here functioning as a synonym for the continuity of the Buddhist dispensation. Some editions read this as referring to the buddha's lineage or succession; the translation captures both senses without forcing either.

The term rendered "intones the scriptural transmission" translates lung 'bogs pa, literally "to extract or draw forth a lung," referring to the practice of receiving or giving an oral scriptural transmission, a technical act distinct from ordinary reading or teaching.  The phrase rendered "surveyed and counted without exception" translates a construction meaning something like "having taken them all into consideration one by one without prior or posterior" — a somewhat compressed expression for enumerating the totality of beings. The rendering conveys the intended sense without loss of doctrinal content.  The sixfold formulaic series rendered throughout as "honoring, holding supreme, revering, venerating, making symbolic representations of, and showing devoted respect" represents a standard list of reverential acts found across Perfection of Wisdom literature; the term rendered "making symbolic representations" corresponds to the Tibetan ri mor byed, literally "making an image or painting of it," referring to the production of icon or facsimile representations as a form of veneration.  The long comparative passage enumerating fractions — hundredth, thousandth, hundred-thousandth, and so on up through "hundred thousand trillions" — follows a standard Perfection of Wisdom sūtra formula. The Tibetan term "bye ba khrag khrig brgya stong" (literally "ten million times one hundred thousand times one hundred thousand") is rendered here as "ten quadrillion" for natural spoken intelligibility; exact numerical correspondence to the Sanskrit koṭiniyutaśatasahasra is intended.  The term rendered "vidyā-mantra" translates རིག་སྔགས, a compound of rig (vidyā, a term of knowledge or incantation) and sngags (mantra). In the Perfection of Wisdom literature this compound functions as a technical epithet for the Perfection of Wisdom itself, not as an instruction to recite a separate mantra. The seven epithets applied here — great, without measure, without limit, without superior, unsurpassed, without equal, equal to the unequalled — are a stock series well attested in the Prajñāpāramitā corpus and closely parallel the epithets found in the Heart Sūtra's praise of the mantra, reflecting the same doctrinal tradition. The distinction between ཚད་མ་མེད་པ (without measure, aprameya) and ཚད་མེད་པ (without limit, asaṃkhyeya or ananta) is preserved in translation; the two terms are genuinely distinct in the Tibetan and likely reflect a Sanskrit distinction in the source text.  The passage contains a structural distinction between two lists: one of spiritual qualities "endowed with the branches of awakening" (attributed to the presence of buddhas in the world) and a parallel list "not endowed with the branches of awakening" (arising through bodhisattva activity in a buddha-less age). The shift from six higher knowledges to five in the second list is present in the Tibetan and is doctrinally significant: the sixth higher knowledge, the exhaustion of the outflows, is exclusive to fully awakened beings and so does not appear in the list available to bodhisattvas acting without a living buddha. The passage ends mid-sentence, continuing into the next fascicle.  The phrase rendered "make thoroughly complete" translates ཀུན་ཆུབ་པར་བྱེད་པ, which can also mean "fully master" or "perfect"; the reading adopted fits the standard list of ten activities related to a sūtra text found elsewhere in Prajñāpāramitā literature. The phrase ཡང་བ་མ་སྤངས་པས་འཆི་བ (dying without having abandoned what is light, i.e., dying of hunger and thirst) is an idiom; the rendering "hunger and thirst" follows standard commentarial explanation of this expression.  The king named "རྒྱལ་པོ་གཟུགས་ཅན་སྙིང་པོ" is rendered here as King Prasenarasāra, a provisional reconstruction; the Tibetan literally means "King Essence of Form," which may correspond to a Sanskrit royal name not immediately identifiable with certainty. Similarly "རྒྱལ་པོ་གསལ་རྒྱལ" is rendered as King Prasenajit, the well-known king of Kosala associated with the Buddha's time, as this is the most likely referent; the Tibetan literally means "King Victorious Brightness." Readers should be aware these royal identifications carry some uncertainty. The phrase rendered "disrupt this with my eyes" translates "མིག་གིས་མི་རྩོལ་བར་བྱ་བའི་ཕྱིར" somewhat idiomatically; the sense appears to be that Māra intends to distract or obstruct by appearing visibly.  The phrase rendered "from time without beginning" translates a Tibetan idiom meaning "for a long time" rather than strictly "from beginningless time," but the doctrinal import here concerns Māra's persistent and longstanding fixation on the Blessed One, and "from time without beginning" fits the Buddhist cosmological register naturally.

The compound "listening, retaining, upholding, reading, mastering, proclaiming, teaching, explaining, reciting transmission, and reciting" represents a standard tenfold enumeration of meritorious activities related to a sūtra text; all ten are rendered as precisely as Tibetan distinctions allow.  The phrase rendered "goes before, leads, and leads completely" translates a triad of Tibetan verbs: sngon du 'gro, 'dren pa, yongs su 'dren pa. The third intensifies the second ("leads" / "guides completely"), and the threefold formulation is characteristic of the sūtra's emphasis on the Perfection of Wisdom's primacy and all-encompassing function. No genuinely consequential textual uncertainty arises here.  The passage shifts between two registers of Tibetan honorific speech: ordinary verb forms when the Blessed One speaks and describes lay practitioners, and honorific verb forms when Śakra speaks of his own actions toward the Blessed One and the text. This distinction has been smoothed into natural English as it carries no semantic difference visible to a listener.

The term "Kauśika" is a patronymic epithet of Śakra (Indra), standard in Perfection of Wisdom literature, used here in place of his name in the Buddha's direct address to him.  The phrase rendered "kinsmen by birth" translates a compound that literally means "blood-relations" or those connected by the bond of birth — the exact social category is somewhat general and the rendering is approximate.

The fourfold analysis of non-findability — the fault-seeker, the act of seeking faults, the one in whom faults are sought, and the Perfection of Wisdom itself — is a recognizable Madhyamaka-style argument embedded in the sūtra, establishing that ultimate non-findability is precisely what renders the practitioner invulnerable to refutation.  The five heavens named here follow the standard cosmological sequence of the desire realm: the Heaven of the Four Great Kings (already introduced in the preceding passage), the Heaven of the Thirty-Three (Trāyastriṃśa, Śakra's realm), the Heaven Free from Strife (Yāma), the Joyous Heaven (Tuṣita), and the Heaven of Delight in Creation (Nirmāṇarati). The passage breaks off mid-sequence before the sixth desire-realm heaven and the form-realm heavens, which presumably continued in the original.  The Tibetan sequence of form-realm heavens listed here corresponds to the standard Abhidharma enumeration of the seventeen form-realm heavens. The list moves through the four dhyāna levels: Brahmaḥpurohita (Brahmaḥs-Retinue / Fore-chanters), Mahābrahmā (Great Brahmā), then the radiance-class heavens (Minor Radiance, Immeasurable Radiance, Resplendent Light), the virtue-class heavens (Minor Virtue, Immeasurable Virtue, Abundant Virtue), and the pure abode heavens (Cloudless, Increasing Merit, Great Fruit, Beings Without Perception, Non-Turbulent, Unafflicted, Superb Appearance, Clarity of Vision), culminating in Akaniṣṭha. The rendering follows this standard enumeration.

The "Heaven of Wielding Others' Creations" renders Skt. Paranirmitavaśavartin, the sixth and highest heaven of the desire realm — consistent with the preceding passage's enumeration ascending through the desire-realm heavens.  The phrase rendered "Great Splendor" translates the divine epithet or quality name "gzi brijid che ba" (Sanskrit: mahātejas), here functioning as both a descriptive quality and apparently a name or designation associated with a class of beings who converge on the place. The syntax is somewhat compressed: the text says these various beings come because they think "there is great splendor / Great Splendor (mahātejas) there," which may refer to the radiance of the Perfection of Wisdom itself drawing them. This reading follows the most natural construal of the passage.  The phrase rendered "pure and clean conduct" translates the pair of terms meaning something like "carefully guarded conduct" and "clean conduct," pointing to both the moral vigilance and the physical or ritual cleanliness associated with the place where the Perfection of Wisdom is being honored. The distinction between the two terms is real but the doctrinal difference is minor; the paired translation preserves both.  The passage ends mid-sentence in the source text, breaking off at the comparative clause beginning "even if that person has the text put into a volume while still young and merely bears or places it." This is a continuation sentence whose conclusion will appear in the next passage; the translation preserves the open construction accordingly.

The name of the buddha "ཆེ་གེ་མོ་ཞིག" rendered here as "The Very Great One" is a descriptive epithet rather than a standard Sanskrit equivalent preserved in the tradition. It functions as an example name within the passage's rhetorical enumeration.  The phrase rendered "depicting the Tathāgata's tradition in image" translates a clause meaning something like "for the sake of making an image or portrait of the Tathāgata's conduct or form." The Tibetan ri mor bgyi ba (to paint a picture, to depict) combined with tshul (tradition, way, form) is slightly elliptical; the sense is that a physical image of the Tathāgata points back to and is validated by the Perfection of Wisdom as the true body of meaning, which is why Śakra chooses the text. This is a doctrinally significant point about the relationship between physical relics or images and the dharmakāya as expressed through the Prajñāpāramitā.

The closing quotation attributed to the Blessed One — "Monks, do not cling to this as the supreme body; monks, look upon me as the dharmakāya" — is an allusion to a well-known formula found in related sūtras; the Tibetan here is terse and compressed, and the rendering reflects the standard doctrinal sense of the passage.  The speaker identified as "Dharmasubha the god" renders the Tibetan name Chos bzang lha, which appears to be a Tibetan translation of a Sanskrit theophoric name such as Dharmaśubha or Dharmasobha. This figure is a divine interlocutor in the sūtra. The passage involves a repeated rhetorical device of scaling up from Jambudvīpa through the two-thousand middling world-system to the full trichiliocosm, each step serving to magnify the relative merit of the Perfection of Wisdom over any quantity of Tathāgata images.  The text shifts mid-passage from a speech by Subhūti back into a colophon marker naming the fifth fascicle of the Aṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā, then opens a new teaching section featuring the jewel simile. The three humors named — bile-fever, bile, and phlegm, together with wind-disorder — correspond to the classical Āyurvedic and traditional Buddhist medical schema of the three bodily humors plus wind, rendered as faithfully as possible. The term rendered "film over the eye" is a classical eye-disease listed in Indian medical literature. The passage ends mid-sentence as Śakra begins his reply, consistent with the source being a continuous excerpt.  The phrase rendered "filled to the brim" translates a term literally meaning "crammed full, heaped up, densely packed," conveying the image of world-realms entirely saturated with relics. The comparison of the two portions — relics versus the Perfection of Wisdom text — is a well-known Prajñāpāramitā motif appearing across multiple sūtras of this literature; Śakra's speech here flows directly into what is clearly Śakra's continued address, though the passage shifts grammatically to first-person in a way that may reflect a compressed or ambiguous speaker attribution in the manuscript. The first-person speaker choosing the Perfection of Wisdom over the relics is most naturally read as Śakra continuing his speech to Ānanda, consistent with the surrounding dialogue.  The phrase rendered "leads the way before all of them" translates the repeated Tibetan construction meaning literally "goes in front of / precedes these." The doctrinal point is the standard Mahāyāna teaching that the Perfection of Wisdom is the guiding or governing perfection that gives the other five their character as genuine pāramitās; the repetition in the final sentence is an emphatic recapitulation of this point and has been retained as such.  The passage ends mid-sentence in the source text, with the list of devotional acts left syntactically open. The translation preserves this open structure as it continues into the following passage. The term rendered "transmits it by scriptural reading" translates the Tibetan for reciting a text aloud for transmission purposes, a distinct act in the list of ways of engaging with scripture. The phrase "makes it fully comprehensible" renders a term that can also mean "masters it thoroughly" or "brings it to full understanding"; both senses are present.  The term rendered as "reliquary" translates a phrase meaning a Tathāgata's bodily relic receptacle or stūpa, which in context encompasses both stūpa structures and the physical representation of the Tathāgata's body. The parallel structure in the passage contrasts two types of practitioners: one who venerates in solitude, and one who venerates and then spreads the Perfection of Wisdom to others — the latter being consistently praised as generating greater merit.  The phrase rendered "the complete exhaustion of the aggregates — in this supreme manner, which is the complete analysis of the true limit" translates a technically condensed Tibetan clause describing parinirvāṇa. The "true limit" is the ultimate, reality itself; "analysis" here renders "rab tu 'byed pa," meaning the discriminating penetration of that limit, while "taming" renders the leading of beings into that liberation. The doctrinal sense is that after attaining buddhahood one leads beings to nirvāṇa, i.e., to the complete cessation of the aggregates through realization of the ultimate.

The cosmological series — Jambudvīpa, the four great continents, the chiliocosm (thousand-world system), the dichiliocosm (two-thousand middling worlds), and the trichiliocosm (great three-thousand world-system) — follows the standard Buddhist cosmological hierarchy and is rendered accordingly.  The phrase rendered "illuminates it in word, thoroughly illuminates it, and illuminates it in all its aspects" translates three related Tibetan verbs of indicating or demonstrating, progressing from basic verbal expression through complete and sequential exposition. The triad is a standard formulation in Perfection of Wisdom literature for the full range of expository activity.

The phrase rendered "bringing their aggregates to complete exhaustion" refers to the entry into nirvāṇa without remainder, a standard Mahāyāna way of describing the liberation of sentient beings from the psychophysical continuum of conditioned existence. "The genuine limit" is a rendering of yang dag pa'i mtha', a common Perfection of Wisdom term for ultimate reality or the true endpoint of the path, equivalent to the absolute or dharmadhātu.  The phrase rendered "those whose aggregates have completely exhausted" describes sentient beings led to parinirvāṇa — the extinction of the aggregates — which is the standard Mahāyāna context for a buddha's activity of "disciplining" or "taming" beings unto their final liberation. The expression "opening of the true limit" translates yang dag pa'i mtha' rab tu 'byed pa, referring to the disclosure or penetration of the ultimate extreme, i.e., reality as it truly is, which functions here as a near-synonym for nirvāṇa or the dharmadhātu.

The cosmological progression — from the world-realm of the four great continents, to the thousand-fold universe, to the twice-thousand middling universe, to the great trichiliocosm, and then to world-realms as numerous as the Ganges sands — follows the standard Buddhist cosmological hierarchy of increasing scale used in merit-comparison passages throughout the Perfection of Wisdom literature.  The opening sentence is a repetition that closes the previous passage and transitions into the new section, as is standard in this type of Perfection of Wisdom literature. The repeated block later in the passage is likewise structural repetition serving a cumulative rhetorical escalation, moving from Jambudvīpa to a four-continent world-system and then toward a thousandfold world-system, which the passage has not yet completed at this point in the Tibetan. The phrase "སྟོང་སྤྱི་ཕུད་ཀྱི་འཇིག་རྟེན་གྱི་ཁམས" most naturally refers to a "chiliocosm" — a world-system of a thousand worlds — which is the standard cosmological unit in this literature; this is rendered accordingly.  The phrase rendered "the chief among thousands" translates stong spyi phud, literally "the summit or prime of a thousand," a technical cosmological term designating the second tier of world-systems in the classical Buddhist cosmological hierarchy (one thousand four-continent world-systems). This stands between the simple thousand-world system mentioned in the preceding passage and the two-thousand middle-world system, and the great trichiliocosm. The progression here is the standard triad: sāhasra, dvisāhasra, trisāhasra-mahāsāhasra.

The passage describing what happens after full awakening — taming sentient beings by "perfectly exhausting the aggregates" and "laying bare the true limit" — is doctrinally dense. The phrase yang dag pa'i mtha' rab tu 'byed pa, rendered "the utter laying bare of the true limit," refers to nirvāṇa as the absolute or ultimate limit of existence; the passage describes the Buddha's activity of leading beings to the final cessation wherein the aggregates are completely exhausted without remainder. This is standard Mahāyāna phrasing for guiding beings to parinirvāṇa while the Buddha himself acts from the dharmakāya.  The key doctrinal contrast in this passage is subtle and consequential: the "counterfeit" (གཟུགས་བརྙན, lit. "reflection/image") teaching identifies the impermanence of a skandha with its mere disintegration or perishing, while the authentic Perfection of Wisdom does not equate the two in that way. This reflects a Madhyamaka concern that reducing impermanence to momentary disintegration mistakes a conventional description for ultimate insight into emptiness and the nature of the aggregates. The term གཟུགས་བརྙན here means "counterfeit image" or "simulacrum" rather than a physical reflection, and is rendered accordingly throughout.  The cosmological sequence follows the classical Buddhist schema of world-systems: the single world-system of the four great continents, the small chiliocosm of one thousand worlds, the medium dichiliocosm of two thousand worlds, and the great trichiliocosm of three thousand times three thousand worlds. The Tibetan term སྟོང་སྤྱི་ཕུད is the small chiliocosm and སྟོང་གཉིས་པ་བར་མ is the medium dichiliocosm.

The passage describing the bodhisattva's "complete mastery in the unsurpassed thorough exhaustion of the aggregates" renders a dense phrase about guiding beings to nirvāṇa without remainder; the "true limit" is a standard term for nirvāṇa, rendered accordingly.  The repeated syntactic formula translating མངོན་པར་དད་པས་མངོན་པར་དད་པ་ལ and its parallels renders a deliberate instrumental-to-dative structure: giving "with X to one who has X." This is the text's way of emphasizing matched receptivity between giver and recipient, and the rendering preserves that parallel structure throughout. The phrase མངོན་པར་རྫོགས་པར་སངས་རྒྱས་ནས་ཀྱང་སེམས་ཅན་གྱི་ཁམས་གཞལ་དུ་མེད་པ་རྣམས་ཕུང་པོ་ཡང་དག་པར་ཟད་པ describes leading beings to the "complete exhaustion of the aggregates," a standard formula for parinirvāṇa without remainder; the rendering follows the commentarial understanding. The passage ends mid-sentence in the source, matching the provided Tibetan, and the translation reflects this accordingly.  The cosmological series — four continents, one thousand worlds, two thousand middling worlds, three thousand great thousands — reflects the standard Buddhist threefold world-system hierarchy. "One thousand worlds" is the small chiliocosm, "two thousand middling worlds" the medium chiliocosm, and "three thousand great thousands" the great trichiliocosm; the passage then multiplies the last by Gaṅgā-sand numbers, a standard rhetorical escalation in Perfection of Wisdom literature.

The fruit of once-returning (Skt. sakṛdāgāmiphala) is the second of the four fruits of the śrāvaka path, attained when one has reduced the strength of desire and aversion to the degree that only one more rebirth in the desire realm remains before liberation.  The phrase rendered "discerns the fruit of once-returning" translates a technical expression meaning that this text itself reveals or distinguishes the once-returner's fruit — indicating that engagement with it generates the conditions for attaining that stage. The parallel structure of the passage then escalates to the fruit of non-returning, continuing the text's pattern of graduated comparisons. The section heading "the sixth fascicle" is a scribal colophon marking a division of the root text and is retained as part of the transmitted text.  The cosmological progression here follows the standard Buddhist concentric scheme: Jambudvīpa, the world-system of the four great continents (cāturdvīpika), the smaller chiliocosm of one thousand worlds (sāhasrī), the intermediate dichiliocosm of two thousand worlds (dvisāhasrī), and the great trichiliocosm of three thousand times three thousand worlds (trisāhasramahāsāhasra). The passage then escalates further to a number of such great trichiliocosms equal to the grains of sand in the Ganges.

The phrase rendered "the perfect opening of the true and real limit" translates yang dag pa'i mtha' rab tu 'byed pa, referring to the penetration or discernment of the ultimate limit of reality, which in context means parinirvāṇa with its complete exhaustion of the aggregates. This is a genuinely compressed and somewhat difficult phrase; the rendering aims to preserve the doctrinal sense without over-interpreting.  The phrase rendered "those whose aggregates are utterly exhausted in the unsurpassed way — leading them to the complete discernment of the true end" translates a technically dense formulation describing nirvāṇa without remainder. The "true end" (yang dag pa'i mtha') is a standard epithet for nirvāṇa as the ultimate limit of saṃsāra; "complete discernment" renders rab tu 'byed pa in the sense of fully cutting through to that limit. The sequence here moves from non-return to arhatship to full buddhahood, marking a deliberate doctrinal escalation in the merit comparison.  The escalating cosmological sequence uses the standard Buddhist world-system terminology: the single world with four continents, the thousand-fold world, the two-thousand middle world-system, and the great three-thousand-fold world-system. These are the standard three tiers of the trichiliocosm, with an additional preliminary tier of Jambudvīpa alone and a culminating tier of Ganges-sand multiples of the trichiliocosm.

The final sentence is deliberately left open as the Tibetan passage itself is syntactically incomplete, ending mid-construction. The translation preserves this open structure as it continues in the next passage.  The passage contains a significant internal resumption: the full block of meritorious acts listed earlier in the preceding passage is recapitulated here nearly verbatim, and then climaxed with the additional act of exhorting another person to train in the path. The two iterations close with different conclusions — the first culminates in discernment of arhatship, the second in discernment of pratyekabuddhahood — reflecting what appears to be a deliberate ascending sequence of spiritual attainments, suggesting the full text continues with further iterations culminating in full buddhahood. The Tibetan སེམས་རྣམ་པར་སྦྱོང་བ in the first occurrence and སེམས་རྣམ་པར་སྤྱོད་པ in the second are textually distinct — "purifying the mind" versus "engaging or directing the mind" — and may reflect a variant reading or scribal variation in the Tibetan; both have been rendered accordingly.  The cosmological progression follows the standard Buddhist scheme of world-systems: the single world with four continents, the thousandfold small world-system, the two-thousandfold intermediate world-system, and the three-thousandfold great world-system, each level being a thousandfold expansion of the previous. The passage ends mid-sentence in the source text, describing the qualities of the meritorious person; the translation faithfully reflects this open construction.  The passage contains a rhetorical escalation ("let alone... let alone...") at the close, which in the Tibetan is a lta zhog construction marking an a-fortiori chain. The translation renders this as a sustained series opening with "let alone," anticipating that the chain continues beyond this excerpt. The phrase ཡང་དག་པའི་མཐར་རབ་ཏུ་འབྱེད་པ་ལ་མངོན་པར་འདུལ་བར་བྱེད་པར་འགྱུར་རོ is technically dense; "leading to perfect discipline in the utter end of reality" renders the sense of guiding beings to parinirvāṇa through the complete exhaustion of the aggregates, consistent with the standard Perfection of Wisdom commentarial reading.  The cosmological terms follow the standard Abhidharma schema: the "world-system of one thousand worlds" is the minor chiliocosm (sāhasrī lokadhātu), the "world-system of two thousand worlds" is the middling chiliocosm (dvisāhasrī), and the "great trichiliocosm" (trisāhasramahāsāhasrī lokadhātu) is the largest standard cosmological unit. These are rendered descriptively rather than with technical Sanskrit compound terms to preserve TTS clarity.  The passage employs the standard Buddhist cosmological sequence of expanding world systems: the world of the four great continents, then a system of one thousand worlds (a small chiliocosm), then two thousand middle worlds (a dichiliocosm), and finally the great trichiliocosm of three thousand times three thousand worlds. The rhetorical device throughout is "set aside" or "leave aside" — a way of saying the merit just described is surpassed by what follows. The phrase rendered "teaches it nearby with its meaning and its letters" reflects the Tibetan idiom of close, personal instruction covering both semantic content and the actual words of the text.  The phrase rendered "medicines for curing illness" translates a compound referring specifically to medicines taken when ill, as distinct from general medicinal requisites — a standard fourfold list of monastic requisites appears here with the fifth item (medicines for the sick) completing it. This is a conventional Vinaya-inflected formula and the translation reflects standard usage.  The long string of epithets describing the arhats and buddhas who have passed into nirvāṇa ("path cut off," "entering cut off," "conceptual elaboration and drawing into existence cut off," "tears dried," "thorns broken," etc.) follows a well-attested formulaic sequence found throughout the Aṣṭasāhasrikā Prajñāpāramitā. These are standard arhat-epithets. The phrase rendered "equal to the incomparable" translates mi mnyam pa dang mnyam pa, a classical superlative formula meaning "that which is the equal of the unequalled," i.e., matchless.

The passage ends mid-sentence, with the temporal clause left open; this is a structural feature of the source text as presented, not an error in translation.  The passage lists five aggregates of a buddha's qualities in a classical formula: moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis-vision of liberation. This pentad is a standard canonical enumeration.

The phrase rendered "the power of the Tathāgata's genuine, exact gnosis" corresponds to a technical term for the Tathāgata's knowledge of things as they are, distinct from knowledge of the extent of things. Both aspects of buddha-gnosis are implied in the surrounding context.

The expressions "equal to the unequal" and "without equal, equal to the unequal" render a compound that in Sanskrit would be asamasama, a stock epithet meaning "equal only to one who has no equal," i.e., uniquely matchless.  The list of eight non-human beings in the middle section follows the standard canonical grouping found in sūtra colophons and audience descriptions: gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, and mahoragas (great serpents). The Tibetan ནམ་མཁའ་ལྡིང is garuḍa and མི་འམ་ཅི is kinnara; both are rendered by their standard Sanskrit names without parenthetical gloss, as these beings appear routinely in this corpus.

The exchange between Maitreya and Subhūti here introduces the central Perfection of Wisdom tension: the bodhisattva must dedicate merit without reifying the act of dedication, its objects, or its aspects into fixed signs, yet the questioner (Subhūti) presses whether apprehending "no-thing" does not itself become a new form of inversion. This dialectic is characteristic of the large Prajñāpāramitā sūtras and should be understood in that doctrinal context.  The phrase rendered "exhausted and ceased, separated and transformed" translates a fourfold description of the past mind — zad cing 'gags par gyur la bral zhing rnam par gyur pa — indicating that the rejoicing mind, being momentary and past, is already gone, making the question of who or what dedicates philosophically acute. This is a central Perfection of Wisdom dialectic about the non-findability of the dedicating subject, and the phrasing is intentionally paradoxical. The translation preserves this tension without smoothing it over.  The passage contains a series of epithets applied to the past buddhas who have fully passed into nirvāṇa. The Tibetan phrase "lam chad pa 'jug pa chad pa" and the subsequent terms form a stock formulaic description of a fully liberated buddha: the path is cut off (no further path to traverse), entry has ceased, arising (of the afflictions or of rebirth) has ceased, and so on through the sequence ending with complete liberation of mind. This is a standard enumerative formula in Perfection of Wisdom literature and has been rendered accordingly, preserving each member of the sequence without conflating them.

The term rendered "great serpents" (lto 'phye chen po) refers to mahoragas, large serpentine beings in the traditional cosmological list of eight classes of non-human beings, here appearing in the standard ninefold enumeration.  The passage presents a philosophically intricate account of dedication of merit without reifying the dedicating mind. The key move is that correct dedication requires not grasping the mind as a fixed, identifiable thing; the nature of reality of the dedicating mind, the phenomena dedicated, and the goal to which dedication is made are all identical in being beyond conceptual fixation. The Tibetan "ཟད་པ་ལ་ཟད་པའོ" rendered as "what has ceased has ceased" reflects a recursive or emphatic formula for cessation, reinforcing the point that the mind of dedication is itself already exhausted and without inherent existence. The list at the end is a series of wholesome roots to be rejoiced in and dedicated, and the passage breaks off mid-enumeration, consistent with the continuity note.  The phrase rendered as "exhausted in exhaustion" translates the Tibetan "ཟད་པ་ལ་ཟད་པའོ" literally. This is a technical philosophical statement pointing to the idea that even "exhaustion" or "cessation" itself is exhausted — the mind, when investigated, resolves into a cessation that cannot be grasped as an entity, let alone dedicated. This is an expression of the Prajñāpāramitā teaching that the very instrument of dedication (the dedicating mind) has no findable nature, and therefore the dedication is genuine precisely because it grasps at nothing.

The epithets applied to the presently abiding buddhas — "whose stream of teaching has not been cut off, whose practice is uninterrupted, whose conceptual elaboration has been cut off, whose leading into existence has been cut off" — are standard formulaic descriptions in this literature distinguishing these buddhas as fully realized while still actively present and teaching. The passage ends mid-sentence and will continue.  The five "aggregates" of the buddhas listed here — moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and wisdom-vision of liberation — render the classical set of pañca-skandha in the special sense of the "five Dharma-bodies" or "five aggregates of the great person" (pañcadharmaskandha), a technical scholastic category distinct from the ordinary five aggregates of a sentient being. This distinction, though implicit in the Tibetan, is doctrinally significant for a learned audience.  The phrase rendered "what is exhausted is exhausted" translates "ཟད་པ་ལ་ཟད་པའོ" — a doubled construction that is genuinely cryptic. It refers to the mind's moment-by-moment cessation: each moment of mind has already perished and thus cannot be grasped as an object of dedication. The rendering follows the most natural doctrinal sense consistent with the Perfection of Wisdom context.

The long final section is a single extended syntactic period in the Tibetan, listing categories of wholesome roots to be dedicated; the translation preserves this structure, with the sentence deliberately left open as the passage is clearly mid-enumeration at the point of truncation.  The passage contains a dense series of three-tense formulations throughout, which is a standard Mahāyāna sūtra construction covering past, future, and present; these have been rendered in their natural English order. The term "mahoraga" for ལྟོ་འཕྱེ་ཆེན་པོ, literally "great serpent" but the name of a class of supernatural beings, is kept in Sanskrit following convention; similarly "garuḍa" for ནམ་མཁའ་ལྡིང and "kiṃnara" for མི་འམ་ཅི. The central doctrinal point of the dedication section turns on the paradox that a truly valid dedication to awakening is one that is free of any reifying clinging to the dedicating act, the dedicator, or the object dedicated — a characteristic Perfection of Wisdom teaching.  The passage contains a subtle dialectical structure regarding three failed modes of dedication: first, knowing signs and clinging; second, making something a sign and thinking of it; third, the opposite failure of mere mindlessness or lack of comprehension. Only the fourth mode — holding the sign to mind without reifying it as a sign — constitutes genuine dedication. The translation preserves this fourfold structure as the text presents it.

The phrase rendered "this too is the Perfection of Wisdom" in the opening sentence is genuinely paradoxical and appears to be intentional: even the incomplete or confused understanding of emptiness participates in the Perfection of Wisdom in some sense. This is a doctrinal point the text itself leaves provocative.  The fivefold list of aggregates attributed to the buddhas and their śrāvakas — moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the gnosis that sees liberation — renders the classical Pāli and Sanskrit formula dharmaskandha in its standard Abhidharma enumeration; the term translated as "aggregate" here is the same Tibetan word as the skandhas of a person, used by extension for these five qualities constituting the "body" of the Dharma.  The phrase rendered "does no harm to the Tathāgatas" translates the Tibetan "skur pa mi 'debs pa," literally "does not impute a denigration" or "does not slander." In this context the meaning is that by rejoicing and dedicating in the correct, signless mode one does not misrepresent or violate the spirit of the Tathāgatas' teaching, as opposed to the distortion caused by sign-based dedication described earlier.

The phrase "inexhaustible" for "chud ma zos pa" (literally "not consumed" or "not wasted") is a standard rendering in this doctrinal context, indicating that merit dedicated in the signless mode is never exhausted or lost.  The passage ends mid-sentence in the source, cutting off after the enumeration of the four immeasurables. The translation preserves this open ending faithfully.

The series of ten superlatives applied to the mass of merit — supreme, excellent, foremost, best, most excellent, magnificent, highest, unsurpassed, without anything above it, without equal, equal to the unequal — is a standard rhetorical climax in Perfection of Wisdom literature; all ten are rendered in sequence as the text demands.  The passage is a sustained anaphoric sequence escalating through the Śrāvaka path stages and beyond, using the rhetorical "set aside even that" device to build toward a climactic comparison involving bodhisattvas. The final sentence is syntactically incomplete in this excerpt, cutting off mid-list after "robes, alms-food" — this is simply where the supplied passage ends, not a textual lacuna.  The phrase rendered "it does not come within even a hundredth part" represents the standard Indian hyperbolic series of fractions used to express incommensurability: the Tibetan literally lists the denominators as one hundred, one thousand, one hundred thousand, ten million, one hundred million, one billion, one hundred billion, ten trillion, one hundred trillion. The translation preserves these as a graduated series rather than spelling out each numeral individually, for readability in TTS contexts. The series is doctrinally standard and presents no genuine uncertainty.  The list of heavenly realms in the long enumeration follows the standard Abhidharma cosmology, running through the desire-realm heavens (the six desire-realm god-realms from Yāma through Paranirmitavaśavartin) and then the Brahmā-world levels, and then the form-realm heavens through the Pure Abodes (Śuddhāvāsa). The Tibetan names are rendered by their standard English equivalents derived from their Sanskrit forms, since using the Tibetan phonetics alone would obscure the doctrinal referents for a general audience. The sentence ends mid-clause in the Tibetan as given; the translation faithfully reflects this open-ended structure.  The passage enumerates a dense series of superlatives in the description of the rejoicing — terms like "foremost, chief, excellent, supremely excellent, distinguished, highest, unsurpassed, unexcelled, higher even than the highest, incomparable, matchless, equal to the matchless, and supremely equal to the matchless." This is a stock formulaic sequence in Perfection of Wisdom literature; each term renders a distinct Tibetan superlative and the cumulative rhetorical effect is intentional. The five "heaps" — moral discipline, meditative absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the wisdom of the vision of liberation — are a standard Abhidharma enumeration of the excellences of a buddha or noble one, here rendered literally in accordance with the Tibetan. The section break "The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The Eighth Fascicle" renders a standard textual division marker in the Tibetan.  The phrase rendered as "equal and supremely equal to none" renders མཉམ་པ་མེད་པ་དང་མི་མཉམ་པ་དང་མཉམ་པ་དང་རབ་ཏུ་མཉམ་པ་མེད་པ, a cluster of four Sanskrit epithets (asamasama, anupama, samasama, asamasama in various configurations) that form a stock superlative series in prajñāpāramitā literature; the rendering here follows the standard sense of the series without forcing a strictly logical sequence, since the Tibetan is itself a compressed approximation of the Sanskrit chain.

The term རྣམ་པར་གྲོལ་བ ("liberation," Skt. vimukti) is used here in a technically distinctive way: each perfection, each act, each being is said to be "as liberation is" — pointing to their non-dual, emptiness-grounded character. This is a significant doctrinal usage that the translation preserves structurally.  The passage shifts from the rejoicing and dedication framework into a lengthy comparative merit argument, a standard Perfection of Wisdom sūtra device. The phrase rendered "with a perception that has an object of reference" renders the Tibetan for giving or practicing while still conceiving of a referential object, i.e., with dualistic, conceptually reifying perception, which is the inferior mode being contrasted with non-referential prajñāpāramitā practice. The passage ends mid-sentence, as the Tibetan text provided is itself incomplete at that point.  The phrase rendered "with pride" translates the Tibetan "nga rgyal dang bcas pa," literally "accompanied by pride or conceit." In this context it is a technical marker indicating that the patience, though genuine and extensive, is still practiced with a subtle sense of self — that is, with the reification of a subject who endures. This is precisely what distinguishes it, in what follows, from non-referential patience, and the apparent paradox of "patience with pride" is doctrinally intentional: the bodhisattva tolerates abuse but retains a subtle ego-grasping, which is contrasted with the superior patience of the non-referential approach.  The long string of superlatives applied to the rejoicing — "supreme, primary, excellent, supremely excellent, distinguished, unsurpassed, without superior, without anything above it, above even what is above, without equal, incomparable, equal to the equal, supremely equal, and inconceivable" — renders a stock Perfection of Wisdom formula listing ascending grades of excellence. The Tibetan terms here (dam pa, gtso bo, rab, mchog rab, gya nom pa, bla ma, bla na med pa, gong na med pa, gong ma'i yang gong ma, mnyam pa med pa, mi mnyam pa, mnyam pa, shin tu mnyam pa med pa, bsam gyis mi khyab pa) are a fixed enumerative series; the rendering follows the sequence as literally as readability permits.

The passage closes mid-sentence by design, continuing the same comparison begun above; the translation faithfully renders this as an incomplete sentence leading into the next passage.  The passage presents a sustained comparative argument structured as a series of nested hypotheticals, building on what preceded. The key doctrinal contrast is between merit generated by diligence practiced with a referential or object-directed perception versus merit generated through rejoicing at virtue and dedicating it within the framework of the Perfection of Wisdom with skillful means.
