The elaborate series of superlative synonyms for incomparability in the rejoicing passage reflects a standard Indic rhetorical device; each term in the chain is rendered distinctly to preserve the cumulative rhetorical force of the original. The passage breaks off mid-sentence, as the Tibetan itself is incomplete at this point, continuing from what was already rendered above.  The Sanskrit term behind "ten-thousandth" and its compounds renders the Tibetan བྱེ་བ, corresponding to Sanskrit koṭi, a unit of ten million in Indian numeration. The progression here is a standard Perfection of Wisdom rhetorical device amplifying incomparability by exhausting all numerical fractions. The rendering follows the internal logic of the series rather than strict Sanskrit numeral equivalents, for the sake of clarity in narration.

The phrase "bodhisattvas who have objects" renders དམིགས་པའི་འདུ་ཤེས་ཅན, meaning those whose meditative absorptions are still supported by or directed toward a referential object, in contrast to the object-free, non-referential samādhi of the Perfection of Wisdom. This is the key doctrinal point explaining why the merit of rejoicing combined with dedication surpasses the merit produced by the four dhyānas practiced with objectifying perception.

The five eyes mentioned are the bodily eye, divine eye, wisdom eye, Dharma eye, and buddha eye — a standard Perfection of Wisdom enumeration.  The phrase rendered "does not fabricate any phenomenon" and "does not give rise to any phenomenon" reflects an important Madhyamaka doctrinal point: the Perfection of Wisdom neither constructs nor suppresses phenomena, being beyond both arising and cessation, as the following clause makes explicit. The two statements are intentionally distinct: the first concerns fabrication in general; the second specifies non-arising as the means of abandoning obscurations.

The phrase "recited three times and set in motion in twelve aspects" refers to the classical teaching on the Four Noble Truths turned in three rounds with four aspects each, yielding twelve aspects in total. This is a standard Abhidharmic and Prajñāpāramitā formulation.  The passage contains a deliberate paradox in the final exchange: projecting in the way one does not project. This is a standard Prajñāpāramitā dialectical formulation pointing to the non-reifying nature of the wisdom that transcends all conceptual positing; the translation renders it literally, as the tradition intends it to be heard as a koan-like statement rather than resolved by paraphrase.

The verb rendered "project" throughout translates the Tibetan gтод pa, which in this context carries the sense of depositing, positing, or laying down a dharma as real — a technical use that the Blessed One progressively undermines. "Project" captures the sense of putting something forward as an object of apprehension.  The phrase rendered "makes this Perfection of Wisdom something distant" translates a term that in context means treating the Perfection of Wisdom as something to be pursued or cultivated from afar as an external object — a doctrinal point about the danger of reifying even the Perfection of Wisdom into an object of striving. The contrast between "accumulate," "fill oneself with," and genuine non-objectifying practice is central to the prajñāpāramitā dialectic here.

The expression "great object of reference" renders a technical Madhyamaka usage where taking any phenomenon as a substantial referent of thought is itself an obstacle; the "largeness" of the object of reference indicates the gravity of the conceptual error.  The phrase rendered "solitary and apart" translates "dben pa," which in Perfection of Wisdom literature carries the sense of being empty of inherent existence and free from conceptual elaboration — literally "remote" or "isolated." The parallel structure of the passage uses it to indicate that just as sentient beings have no truly established, self-enclosed existence, so too the Perfection of Wisdom is "isolated" from any such inherent grounding. The rendering "solitary and apart" preserves the literal flavor while the doctrinal resonance should be understood in this emptiness context.

The statement that "sentient beings do not fully awaken" and that therefore the Perfection of Wisdom "does not fully awaken" is a characteristically paradoxical formulation of the prajñāpāramitā literature: because no truly existent sentient being can be found to undergo awakening, awakening itself, as an event attributed to such beings, cannot be established as truly occurring. This is not a denial of awakening but a negation of its inherent, reified existence.  The term rendered as "remote" throughout translates a word that can also mean "solitary" or "secluded," but in this doctrinal context it carries the sense of being utterly beyond — free of grasping-supports — and functions as a near-synonym of emptiness and peace, so "remote" is the most accurate available rendering. The phrase "full cognition" renders a term meaning comprehensive knowing, distinguishable from ordinary perception; its precise referent in the original is a type of conceptual apprehension that, together with perception, designation, and conventional expression, is contrasted with the non-referential character of the Perfection of Wisdom.  The phrase rendered "derangement of wisdom" translates ཤེས་རབ་འཆལ་བ, literally "going astray or becoming deranged with respect to wisdom," meaning the corruption or loss of prajna — the opposite of the clear discrimination that the Perfection of Wisdom cultivates. The term is doctrinely significant as describing the karmic result of rejecting this teaching.

The escalating time-units at the close of the passage follow a standard canonical pattern of vast temporal enumeration; the rendering attempts to convey their cumulative force without inventing Tibetan-specific numerical vocabulary not present in the source.  The phrase rendered as "actions of immediate retribution" translates the five ānantarya-karmas, the five gravest acts that bring retribution in the very next life without interval. The Tibetan "མཚམས་མ་མཆིས་པ་ལྔ" is the standard Tibetan rendering of pañcānantarya.

The phrase "ruin their own stream and destroy the streams of others" and the parallel "poison their own stream" use the term "stream" (rgyud) in its sense of the mental continuum of a person. This is a technical usage: one's rgyud is one's ongoing psycho-physical continuum.  The sentence describing deluded persons who revile the Perfection of Wisdom and then "will go forth into renunciation within this well-proclaimed Dharma and Vinaya" is doctrinally paradoxical and syntactically compressed. The Tibetan reads as though such persons, despite their delusion and rejection of the Perfection of Wisdom, do in fact ordain within the Buddhist community — which is likely the intended irony or critique: they become monastics yet internally reject the deepest teaching. This reading has been preserved rather than smoothed over.  The phrase rendered "utterly estranged from the Three Jewels in every respect, from every side, and in every dimension" translates a fourfold Tibetan intensifier construction (thams cad nas thams cad rnam pa thams cad du thams cad phyi rol du gyur pa) — a standard Perfection of Wisdom rhetorical formula emphasizing total exclusion; the rendering preserves its cumulative force without reproducing the repetition awkwardly in English.

The term "fascicle" for bam po reflects the traditional Tibetan textual division marker indicating a manuscript bundle or recitation unit, here the ninth such unit of the text.  The phrase rendered "illuminates" for གསལ་བར་བགྱིད་པ literally means "that which makes clear" or "that which illuminates." This is a standard Perfection of Wisdom epithet pointing to wisdom's self-luminous quality and its dispelling of obscuration; no single English word captures all of this, but "illuminates" serves well in context.  The phrase rendered "without junction or connection" translates མཚམས་སྦྱོར་བ་མ་མཆིས་པ, literally "without joining of junctures." This is a somewhat unusual expression in the prajñāpāramitā context; it likely means the Perfection of Wisdom admits of no divisions, boundaries, or connective junctions, consistent with its non-dual purity. The rendering preserves both the literal sense and doctrinal import.

The term བདག, "self," used by Subhūti in the formula "by the purity of self" is striking. In this context it is almost certainly the self understood in its conventional or nominal sense — and its purity is non-dual with the purity of form, consciousness, and so forth — rather than affirming any substantialist self. This is a well-known rhetorical and doctrinal feature of certain prajñāpāramitā passages.  The term rendered "limitlessness" throughout the opening exchanges translates མུ་མ་མཆིས་པ, literally "having no limit or boundary," here used in a series parallel to the "purity" exchanges in the preceding passage. The shift from "purity" to "limitlessness" as the structural term is intentional and mirrors the Tibetan precisely.

The phrase "makes this Perfection of Wisdom into something distant" renders ཤེས་རབ་ཀྱི་ཕ་རོལ་ཏུ་ཕྱིན་པ་འདི་རིང་དུ་བགྱིད་པར་འགྱུར, literally "would make this Perfection of Wisdom far away" — the irony being that conceptualizing it as neither near nor far paradoxically distances it when taken as an object of perception.

The passage ends mid-sentence in the source; the translation reflects this without closure.  The phrase rendered "solitariness" throughout translates the Tibetan "dben pa," which literally means "emptiness, solitude, or aloneness" and in Prajñāpāramitā contexts carries the sense of phenomena being "alone" in their nature — that is, devoid of the dualistic overlays of characteristic marks and conceptual elaboration. This is a recognized technical use that shades into the sense of "transcendence" or "aloofness from extremes," though "solitariness" preserves the literal image the text is playing on.

The final dialectical exchange — that the Tathāgata both has and has not awakened to all phenomena — is a characteristic Prajñāpāramitā paradox resolved by the identity of the intrinsic nature of phenomena (their marklessness) with the Perfection of Wisdom itself. No fabricated dharma was grasped; hence awakening entails no epistemic object in the ordinary sense.  The phrase rendered "no one whatsoever perfectly and completely awakens" is a deliberately paradoxical statement. The Blessed One's answer to "Is the Perfection of Wisdom difficult to realize?" is not a direct answer but a doctrinal declaration: because there is no real agent or object, no one "awakens" in any hypostatized sense. This is consistent with the prajñāpāramitā literature's refusal to posit awakening as a graspable attainment.

The terms rendered "incomplete or complete" translate "ma rdzogs pa dang rdzogs pa," literally "not perfected and perfected." The sense here is that form is neither to be conceived as deficient nor as fulfilled or consummated — any such conceptual framing is itself abandoned. This reading is consistent with the non-elaboration theme running through the passage.  The phrase "individual perfect knowledges" renders the Tibetan term for the four analytical knowledges (pratisaṃvid), which are specific cognitive attainments of buddhas and advanced āryas. The text's recapitulation section abbreviates by substituting "ma yin" (it is not so either) between enumerated items, a characteristic Perfection of Wisdom sūtra telescoping style, which has been expanded naturally in the translation for clarity in narration.  The phrase rendered "it is exhausted at just that" translates the repeated expression "de snyed du zad do," which conveys that the nature of reality of phenomena is neither augmented nor diminished by being taught or not taught — it simply stands complete in itself. Some translators render this as "it remains as it is," but the Tibetan more precisely means it is wholly and exactly what it is, neither more nor less, which the "exhausted at just that" rendering captures in context.

The monk who pays homage to the Perfection of Wisdom is unnamed in the text; this is not an oversight but appears to be as the text stands.  The term rendered "echo" translates a Tibetan word that can mean "echo" or "reverberation." The text uses it first as "echo" in the analogy with the voice, and then extends it as a simile for all phenomena — a standard Prajñāpāramitā illustration of the illusory, ungraspable nature of things. The translation preserves this consistently.

The phrase "humans and non-humans who watch for an opportunity" refers to malevolent spirits and hostile beings who exploit the vulnerability of a practitioner who has lost the protection of wisdom — a standard motif in these sūtras.  The phrase rendered "has no echo" translates a term literally meaning "does not reverberate like an echo from a cliff face." The image is of space as that which neither echoes nor can be articulated or pointed to as an object, emphasizing its ineffability. This is a standard simile in the Perfection of Wisdom literature for non-referentiality.

The exclamation rendered "How wondrous!" translates a Tibetan interjection of admiration and astonishment that conventionally opens a statement of praise or wonder in sūtra dialogue.  The phrase rendered "does not abandon their composure" translates a construction meaning roughly "because they do not give up through being downcast or dejected," implying that steadfastness in practice prevents untimely death — a standard merit-protection formula in Perfection of Wisdom literature.

The final series of six negations (not leading in, not leading in decisively, not revealing truly, not revealing definitively, not causing to obtain, not causing to obtain definitively) reflects a careful scholastic enumeration of the ways a teaching ordinarily functions; their negation here underscores the non-objectifying, non-grasping character of the Perfection of Wisdom itself.  The sequence of epithets for the Perfection of Wisdom given by Subhūti at the close of this passage presents a series of compound formulations. The term rendered "Perfection of Sameness with what is unequal" translates mi mnyam pa dang mnyam pa nyid kyi pha rol tu phyin pa, which is a recognized technical expression in Prajñāpāramitā literature meaning that this wisdom is equalized with the unequalled, i.e., it is the perfection that is both equal and unequal, or "the perfection of equalness with the unequal." The rendering follows the Tibetan closely. The phrase "no one has been established as a recipient of generosity" renders sbyin pa'i gnas su byas pa yang med, literally "no one has been made a field of giving," i.e., no one has been made an object of meritorious giving — a technical idea that the teaching does not create a donor-recipient relationship.  The passage names a series of paradoxically titled perfections, several of which are doctrinally arresting. "The Perfection of No Discipline" renders the phrase whose literal sense is that past, present, and future moments are unapprehensible, and so no vinaya-style restraint applies at the ultimate level. "The Perfection of Dream, Echo, Optical Illusion, Mirage, and Magical Apparition" is a single compound perfection name drawn from the classic set of eight analogies for illusion-like phenomena. "The Perfection of Impermanence" and "the Perfection of Suffering" are genuinely paradoxical titles: the text appears to name a perfection after each of the three characteristics, concluding with emptiness, in order to indicate that thorough understanding of impermanence, suffering, and emptiness is itself the corresponding perfection. "The Perfection of No Purification" rests on the principle that since no support or basis is apprehensible, there is nothing that requires or undergoes purification at the ultimate level.  The phrase rendered as "Individual Perfect Knowledges" translates a term referring to the four pratisaṃvids, the four analytical knowledges, which are unshared attainments of buddhas and great bodhisattvas. This is a standard Abhidharma category.

The "Ten Grounds" in this context refers to the ten bodhisattva grounds, with generosity named as the first, corresponding to the standard Mahāyāna schema.

The term rendered "Self-Arising" for the perfection associated with all phenomena lacking inherent nature points to the quality of arising spontaneously without fabricated causes — a somewhat unusual formulation, though attested in this genre of Perfection of Wisdom literature.  The phrase rendered "craving for it" translates a term that normally means craving or thirst in a negative sense, but here it appears in a list of positive qualities whose absence is being noted, so it functions as something closer to "eager longing" or "thirst for it" in a spiritually positive register. The translation preserves the literal sense while allowing context to clarify.  The repeated phrase translated as "does not dwell in form as though thinking 'this is form,' and does not abide in it" renders a technically layered Tibetan construction distinguishing two modes of non-fixation: not taking something as its own object-label and not settling into it as a support. This is a standard Prajñāpāramitā teaching on non-apprehension and is rendered consistently throughout. The term "Kauśika" is the Blessed One's mode of addressing Śakra, being his clan name.  The passage plays on a reciprocal logical structure: not abiding in the designation "profound" is itself the form of training in yoga, and not training in yoga with that designation is itself not abiding in it. This is an intentional doctrinal chiasmus and has been rendered to preserve the symmetry rather than smooth it away.

The phrase rendered "will not cause them to withdraw their affection" translates the idiom of a Tathāgata's mind not being "drawn out" or "displeased" after being satisfied — meaning the bodhisattva's conduct will be irreproachable even in the presence of a buddha.  The phrase rendered "ultimate truth" at the end translates "yang dag pa'i mtha'," literally "the limit of the real" or "the real extreme," a term that in Perfection of Wisdom literature functions as a near-synonym for the ultimate, final reality — here kept close to the source rather than assimilated to the technical Yogācāra term pariniṣpanna; the parenthetical is offered as an approximate doctrinal anchor only.

The phrase "lta smos kyang ci 'tshal" (and its variants "lta smos kyang ci dgos") is a rhetorical expression meaning "what need is there even to speak of them" — implying the case under discussion is a fortiori obvious; this idiomatic force is preserved in the translation.  The phrase rendered "ultimate truth" in the preceding passage and here as "ultimate truth" translates yang dag pa'i mtha', literally "the authentic or perfect limit or extreme." In the context of Perfection of Wisdom literature this refers to the ultimate reality itself rather than the specifically Yogācāra technical term pariniṣpanna used in the preceding passage; the bracketed Sanskrit in the preceding passage was a translation choice that should be understood as approximate here.

The passage on the bodhisattva not being frightened by "the ground of the śrāvakas or the pratyekabuddhas" refers to the fear that a bodhisattva might fall back from the Mahāyāna path and settle for the liberation of the lesser vehicles.  The passage describing the pregnant woman's experience contains a phrase rendered as "the perception that runs counter to her former ways," translating a term meaning something like improper or contrary mental attention. The precise nuance is that she habitually dwells on perceptions that are contrary to her previous comfortable habits, likely referring to her changed experience of the body during late pregnancy. This is contextually clear but the exact technical implication of the Tibetan phrase is slightly ambiguous.  The passage contains a grammatically intricate series of negations regarding the aggregates. The phrases rendered as "what is not feeling, what is not perception, what is not formations" follow the Tibetan literally: the text uses the negative particle to distinguish these from their respective aggregate labels in the context of increase and decrease. This is a philosophically significant construction indicating that even the conceptual designations of the aggregates themselves are not to be reified. The parallel structure has been maintained throughout.  The phrase rendered "who has not broken his commitment" translates yi dam ma nyams pa, literally "whose samaya or pledge is unimpaired." This likely refers to a bodhisattva whose bodhicitta resolve or formal vow remains intact and unbroken, which is why māras cannot ultimately obstruct such a one. The doctrinal nuance is significant: it is the integrity of the practitioner's commitment, not merely their status, that provides protection.  The phrase rendered as "direct their attention toward and fully embrace" translates two technical terms used together throughout this passage. The first denotes the buddhas' compassionate mindfulness or intentional care directed toward beings or texts, and the second denotes their complete, sustaining protection or embrace. These together express the idea that the activity of practitioners is enabled and sustained by the active, watchful power of the buddhas dwelling throughout the cosmos. This is a key doctrinal point of the passage: the practitioner's engagement with the Perfection of Wisdom is not merely personal effort but is held within a field of buddha-power pervading all world-systems.  The phrase rendered "the core of the Dharma and the Vinaya is obtained" translates a compressed Tibetan phrase that may refer to authentic scriptural transmission reaching a new region, or to practitioners who have attained the essence of the teaching. The movement of the Perfection of Wisdom from south to middle to north is a well-known motif describing the historical spread of the Mahāyāna sūtras, generally understood by commentators as referring to India (south), a central region, and then northern lands. The rendering "diligent striving" for yongs su g.yo ba captures both its literal sense of thorough movement or exertion and its doctrinal sense of energetic application.  The repeated enumeration of eleven or so activities related to the text — upholding, bearing, reading, mastering, proclaiming, teaching, explaining, transmitting by scripture, chanting, and finally placing it in a volume — is a standard formulaic list in Perfection of Wisdom literature and is rendered consistently throughout. The phrase rendered "engage in yoga upon it in suchness" (de bzhin nyid du rnal 'byor du byed pa) means to practice or meditate on it in accordance with its true nature, suchness, and this technical sense is preserved in the translation.  The cascading series of large numbers — hundreds, thousands, hundreds of thousands, ten millions, billions, hundreds of billions, and the compound "tens of billions of times hundreds of thousands" — follows a standard Sanskrit cosmological enumeration. The Tibetan term bya ba (Skt. koṭi, ten million) combined with further multipliers produces these escalating figures; the rendering captures the rhetorical force of the passage without attempting false arithmetical precision.  The sevenfold action-series in the first paragraph — establishing, teaching, setting on the path, praising, generating joy, revealing, and making irreversible — parallels an almost identical passage in the preceding context. The rendering follows the same sequence for consistency.  The phrase rendered as "non-referential cognition" translates མི་དམིགས་པ, literally "non-apprehension" or "non-referencing," a key Perfection of Wisdom term denoting the absence of grasping at any object of reference; the Sanskrit equivalent is anupalambha. This is a significant technical term and warrants the gloss.

The sequence གླལ་བ་དང་རྒོད་པ་དང་སྟེག་ཅིང་ཡི་གེར་འདྲི་བ is somewhat compressed. The reading adopted — yawning, restlessness, fidgeting, and resorting to writing — follows the most natural Tibetan parsing, though "fidgeting" (སྟེག་ཅིང) could also be rendered "stretching" or "leaning back." The general sense of physical and mental unsettledness is clear and doctrinally unambiguous.  The dog simile is compressed and somewhat cryptic in the Tibetan. The phrase rendered "given up and set aside from a meal" translates a construction meaning the dog has been excluded or dismissed from what was prepared, and then turns to beg scraps from a worker. The point of the simile is that the dog, cut off from the main source of nourishment, goes looking in the wrong place — just as a bodhisattva who abandons the Perfection of Wisdom seeks the essence of awakening in lesser vehicles. This reading is consistent with the parallel Chinese recensions.  The final line is a sentence-initial clause that breaks off mid-passage, left open as the Tibetan itself ends here without completing the sentence. This is clearly a continuation into the next passage and has been rendered accordingly as an open clause awaiting continuation.  The phrase rendered "palace of the sun and moon" refers to the celestial palaces of the sun and moon deities — vastly magnificent structures used here as a simile for the vast scope of the Perfection of Wisdom, contrasted with the merely royal palace, which stands for the more limited aspiration of the śrāvaka path. The analogy is ironic: the carpenter seeking to build a royal palace should not be measuring a celestial palace that far exceeds it; similarly, one seeking the limited fruit of the śrāvaka path should not be drawing on the Perfection of Wisdom, which is suited to the immeasurably grander aim of full buddhahood. The passage ends mid-sentence in the source text, as the analogy continues beyond what was provided for translation.  The phrase rendered "a food of a hundred flavors — refined, beneficial, refined, pleasant" translates a compressed Tibetan list that is partially repetitive; "refined" appears twice, which may reflect a scribal or textual anomaly in this witness. The doctrinal point is clear regardless. The "sixty-day-old gruel" is an idiom for the coarsest, least nourishing food imaginable, contrasted with the supremely nourishing food of a hundred flavors as a figure for the Perfection of Wisdom versus the lesser-vehicle teachings.  The text lists two names, Viśikhā and Śibikā, that appear to be transliterations of Sanskrit proper names. In context they likely refer to types of people or characters well known in the Indian narrative world — Viśikhā is a famous female devotee, and Śibikā may refer to a palanquin-bearer or a figure from popular lore — but since the Tibetan simply transliterates, the names are retained as given without further gloss.

The phrase rendered "those of indeterminate sex" translates ma ning, the Tibetan for the Sanskrit paṇḍaka, a category that in the Vinaya and broader Indian literature denotes persons of ambiguous or non-normative sexual or gender constitution.  The term rendered "wheel-spinning jugglery with the hands" translates a phrase describing a kind of performance or trick involving spinning wheels or hoops manipulated by hand — the exact nature of the spectacle is uncertain, but the general sense of a distracting entertainment is clear.

The phrase "recite aloud" and "chant by heart" render two related but distinct terms for oral textual practice: one refers to recitation from transmission and the other to memorized chanting; the distinction is preserved as best the English allows.  The final sentence is somewhat compressed in the Tibetan. The sense is that when the Dharma-speaker is thwarted by unavailability of texts, he will tend to cast blame on the absent listeners, calling them hostile or incompatible — this is itself another manifestation of Māra's activity, even though the passage stops short of the explicit label here. The structure implies it is understood from context.  The passage contains a complex rhetorical move in the penultimate paragraph: a speaker arrives and delivers what appears to be orthodox Buddhist teaching — praising the gods, then immediately undercutting it with a summary of the Four Noble Truths and a list of the four śrāvaka fruits — but does so in a way designed to discourage bodhisattvas from pursuing the Perfection of Wisdom. The text identifies this as Māra's work not because the doctrine itself is false, but because its deployment is intended to turn bodhisattvas away from the Mahāyāna path toward seeking personal liberation. This is a significant doctrinal point worth noting for listeners.

The phrase rendered "right here and now, let suffering be utterly uprooted" in the first such speech is a Hīnayāna soteriological pitch being deliberately inserted to disrupt the Mahāyāna assembly — the Māra-work lies in the intent and effect, not the literal content.  The phrase rendered "wilderness of water" translates a term that likely refers to a region afflicted by floods or dangerous waterways, paralleling the other wilderness dangers listed. The exact nuance is slightly unusual but the structural parallelism with famine, bandits, and wild animals makes this the most coherent reading.  The phrase rendered as "leads them to rejection" translates a form involving doubt and abandonment; the wicked Māra's strategy is to first sow doubt and then bring about the abandonment of the authentic Perfection of Wisdom teaching. The passage is internally consistent with this reading.  The phrase rendered "realising the ultimate limit" translates mtha' mngon du byed, literally "making the limit manifest." In context this is Māra's fraudulent claim that the bodhisattva engaged in profound teachings is heading toward the śrāvaka's nirvāṇa — the "limit" being the extreme of personal cessation — rather than full awakening. This is a subtle doctrinal point worth flagging for the listener's benefit, though the translation captures the sense without interrupting the flow.  The list of physical afflictions — wind, bile, phlegm, combinations thereof, insects, flesh-worms, and so forth — reflects classical Indian medical and cosmological categories. "Combination" renders the Tibetan term for the humoral combination disorder in Āyurvedic and early Buddhist medical thought. The phrase rendered "flowing sickness" and "wasting disease" translates terms for epidemic or pervasive illness and debility respectively; their precise identities are uncertain, but they are clearly varieties of disease or affliction in the traditional list.  The passage employs a threefold temporal schema — past, future, and present buddhas — culminating in the first-person declaration by the Blessed One himself. The shift to first person in the final sentence is textually significant and has been rendered literally. The phrase rendered "she who gives birth to them" and "mother" reflects the classical Mahāyāna identification of the Perfection of Wisdom as the mother of all buddhas, a central doctrinal motif of the prajñāpāramitā literature.  The passage plays on two near-synonymous terms rendered "without essential nature" and "without substantial existence." The first, rendered elsewhere as "lacking essential nature" or "essenceless," grounds emptiness; the second, more literally "lacking a thing-ness" or "non-entity," reinforces the same point from a slightly different angle. Both point to the same emptiness teaching, and the doctrinal distinction is subtle enough that most commentators treat them as complementary rather than strictly different. The phrase "neither perishes nor utterly perishes" is a deliberately paradoxical formulation: emptiness, being beyond arising and ceasing, cannot be subject to destruction in either a mild or radical sense. This is a standard Prajñāpāramitā dialectical move.  The phrase rendered "collected minds" translates sems bsdus pa, literally "mind that is gathered or concentrated," denoting a mind that is focused or recollected, set in contrast to sems g.yengs pa, the "distracted mind." The passage plays these two states against each other in a dialectical structure common to Perfection of Wisdom literature, where even the collected state is shown to be exhausted and beyond exhaustion, and even the distracted state is shown to be without characteristics and thus undistracted at the level of the nature of reality.

The statement that the mind "has been blessed by the Tathāgata" renders byin gyis brlabs, adhiṣṭhāna, and here carries the sense that such a mind, recognized in its immeasurable and inexhaustible nature, participates in or is pervaded by the Tathāgata's blessing-power — a doctrinally significant point in Perfection of Wisdom exegesis.  The phrase rendered "there is nothing to which a label can be affixed" translates brdal btags pa med pa, literally "there is no application of a conventional designation." This is a significant doctrinal qualifier: even the thoroughly afflicted mind, when examined, cannot be pinned down by any label, because it lacks inherent existence as an afflicted thing.

The phrase "abide without foundation" renders gzhi med pa la gnas pa, meaning the mind has no fixed substratum or basis of support — a standard Prajñāpāramitā formulation pointing to the non-grounded, unsupported nature of mind.

The closing phrase "have no essential nature and are like what does not exist" renders ngo bo nyid med cing med pa dang 'dra ba, a strong śūnyatā statement: the minds lack an essential nature and are comparable to something utterly nonexistent, yet they appear and function — the paradox that underlies the entire passage's question-and-answer structure.  The phrase rendered as "spontaneously accomplished by way of inclining toward the absence of inherent existence" translates the Tibetan "dngos po med par gzhol ba lhun gyis grub pa." The term "lhun gyis grub pa" (spontaneously accomplished, self-arisen) is doctrinally significant and echoes the logic of the undefiled mind's purity: its very nature inclines naturally toward the absence of substantial inherent existence, and this is why it is "spontaneously" or "naturally" undefiled. This is consistent with the Perfection of Wisdom literature's understanding of the mind's luminous nature.  The phrase rendered "whatever is freedom in mind" translates a compressed Tibetan formulation that literally reads "whatever the separation of mind is." The sense, following the parallel logic of this sūtra passage, is that the natural freed or released state of mind transcends the category of aversion or delusion entirely, and thus the true nature of mind cannot be identified with either the afflicted or the temporarily purified state. This is a standard Prajñāpāramitā dialectical move affirming that ultimate mind-nature transcends all such predications.  The Tibetan text here distinguishes between two terms that are rendered distinctly: "vast mind" (སེམས་རྒྱ་ཆེན་པོ) and "mind that has become great" (སེམས་ཆེན་པོར་གྱུར་པ). These appear to be technical distinctions within the sūtra's enumeration of types of mind known by the Tathāgata, paralleling the classical list of sixteen mental qualities found in mind-contemplation passages. The distinction between "vast" and "great" in this context may reflect Yogācāra scholastic categorization, and the rendering attempts to preserve that differentiation in English.

The phrase rendered "without arousal" (ཀུན་ནས་སློང་བ་མེད་པ) refers to the absence of motivational agitation or prompting of the mind toward objects — a technical term in Abhidharma and Mahāyāna analysis of mental factors.  The phrase rendered "three eyes" refers to the traditional enumeration of the flesh eye, the divine eye, and the eye of wisdom, and "all eyes" extends this to include the Dharma eye and the buddha eye — the standard set of five eyes in Mahāyāna literature. The text here makes the point that mind without characteristic mark is not an object for any of these modes of vision.

The passage on minds with obstruction contains a subtle and important doctrinal point: the obstruction arises not from grasping at things that exist, but from imputing existence to what does not exist and then taking emptiness itself as an object of apprehension. This is the classical Perfection of Wisdom critique of reifying śūnyatā.

The explanation for minds without obstruction conspicuously lacks an explicit reason in this passage, unlike the parallel treatments above. This appears to be deliberate: no discursive explanation is given, mirroring the unobstructed, self-evident nature of such minds.  The phrase rendered "minds with something superior" and "minds with nothing superior" translates སེམས་བླ་ན་ཡོད་པ and སེམས་བླ་ན་མེད་པ respectively. The former literally means "mind having something above it" and the latter "mind having nothing above it," i.e., minds that are surpassable versus unsurpassable. These are standard Perfection of Wisdom catalogue terms for types of minds; the rendering chosen follows the literal structure of the Tibetan rather than a more idiomatic paraphrase, in keeping with the sūtra's enumerative style.

The explanation of minds not in meditative equipoise, "they enter into non-evenness by means of the even and the non-even," is a deliberately paradoxical formulation in the original; it is rendered literally. The corresponding explanation of minds in meditative equipoise, "they enter into evenness by means of the even and the even," likewise preserves the original's repetitive, formulaic character.  The phrase rendered "unfreed minds are by their very nature freed" reflects a genuine doctrinal paradox central to Perfection of Wisdom literature: minds appear unfree in the conventional sense yet are, in their ultimate nature (being without intrinsic existence), naturally and primordially liberated. This is not a contradiction but the text's deliberate teaching on the identity of bondage and liberation at the level of ultimate truth.

The terms "open and closed" translate "phye ba dang btsums pa," literally "opened and closed," a pair that in this context most likely refers to open or closed sense faculties or sense doors — eyes and the like being open (receptive) or shut — though some commentators read it as referring to open and clenched hands, i.e., generosity and its absence. The passage's concluding explanation, that all these births are known as "abiding in form and arising in form," suggests the sense-faculty reading is more contextually apt, but genuine uncertainty remains.  The folio marker "The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines, the thirteenth fascicle" appears mid-passage in the Tibetan text itself as a scribal or editorial insertion marking the fascicle division; it is retained in translation since it is part of the received text, though it interrupts the doctrinal flow. The four tetralemma-structured views on the thus-gone one after death and on the permanence and finitude of self and world are the classical fourteen unanswered questions attributed to the Buddha, here analyzed as "grounded in" each of the five aggregates in turn — a standard Abhidharma and Prajñāpāramitā doctrinal move showing that all such speculative views rest on clinging to the aggregates.  The terms "open" and "closed" render Tibetan phye ba and btsums pa, which in the context of this Perfection of Wisdom passage refer to beings whose minds are open or receptive versus those whose minds are closed or unreceptive — a distinction the Tathāgata perceives through his omniscient knowledge of beings' spiritual dispositions. The precise Sanskrit equivalents are vivṛta and saṃvṛta in this sense, distinct from the philosophical saṃvṛti meaning "conventional." This technical usage is consistent throughout the passage and the preceding context.  The phrase rendered "the open and the closed" follows the established rendering from the preceding passage and refers to what is apparently a paired classification in the source text, possibly corresponding to conditioned and unconditioned phenomena or a similar binary. The exact referent remains somewhat opaque without broader manuscript context, but consistency with the prior rendering has been maintained.

The description of Subhūti's question about who would trust in this teaching lists three categories: irreversible bodhisattvas, arhats with fully perfected discriminative understanding, and persons with excellent view. The middle category, literally "arhats whose thorough examination or investigation is fully perfected," may refer to arhats distinguished by their analytical insight; this reading is adopted as the most doctrinally coherent.  The phrase rendered "because they do not abide in those very dharmas in the way they truly are, they abide permanently" reflects a deliberate doctrinal paradox in the Tibetan: the Tathāgatas abide in the Perfection of Wisdom, and precisely because they do not fixate on or cling to those dharmas as having ultimate reality, they genuinely and permanently abide. The syntax of the Tibetan is compressed here and admits of more than one reading; the rendering given follows the standard Prajñāpāramitā doctrinal logic.  The phrase rendered throughout as "knows what has been done and feels what has been done" translates the Tibetan "byas pa shes shing byas pa tshor ba," which is a somewhat unusual technical formulation. It appears to describe the Tathāgata's direct and fully experiential cognition of all that has been done — the vehicles traveled, the deeds accomplished — suggesting both intellectual recognition and felt, embodied awareness. The doctrinal point seems to be that the Tathāgata's omniscience is not merely propositional but encompasses the full living reality of the path, sustained through the Perfection of Wisdom.

The phrase "mngon par 'dus ma byas pa" rendered as "not explicitly conditioned" is a nuanced choice. The term 'dus byas pa normally means "conditioned" or "compounded," and mngon par 'dus ma byas pa would be its negation. Some translators render this as "not manifestly compounded" or "not explicitly fabricated." The doctrinal context — paired with "uncreated" and "unchanging" — confirms these are three characterizations of the ultimate nature of phenomena from the Madhyamaka perspective.  The phrase rendered "self-arisen enlightenment" translates rang byung nyid, literally "self-origination itself," which in this context refers to the quality of being a pratyekabuddha or, more likely given the list's culminating structure, the self-arisen, uncaused nature of a buddha's awakening. The list Tathāgatahood, buddhahood, self-arisen-ness, omniscience forms a standard fourfold epithet cluster in this sūtra tradition, and "self-arisen enlightenment" preserves the doctrinal sense without overspecifying.

The epithet "unequalled-yet-equal" renders mi mnyam pa dang mnyam pa, literally "the unequal and the equal," a classical paradoxical title also appearing in other Perfection of Wisdom sūtras, typically as a name for the Buddha's activity or the Dharma itself — indicating it surpasses all comparisons yet encompasses all.  The phrase rendered "and so there is no conceiving of it as such" translates the somewhat unusual construction "snyam du med do," which literally means "there is no thinking of it as such" or "there is no notion of it as that." This is a standard Perfection of Wisdom idiom indicating that the nature of reality transcends any conceptual apprehension, and the rendering chosen preserves both the literal sense and the doctrinal force.

The passage ends mid-enumeration with "form too is immeasurable," breaking off before completing the parallel series for the remaining aggregates and all phenomena. This appears to be a deliberate textual cut-point, and the translation faithfully renders what is present without completing the pattern editorially.  The phrase rendered "without equal and yet equal" translates the Tibetan mi mnyam pa dang mnyam pa, literally "unequal-and-equal," a paradoxical compound. The doctrinal sense is that phenomena are peerless and incomparable (without equal to anything else) while simultaneously being on a par with all other phenomena in their shared nature of emptiness and equality with space. This double meaning is intentional and theologically significant.

The expression "supreme word of Dharma for those in whom consciousness has arisen" renders rnam par shes par gyur pa'i chos kyi tshig bla dgas, meaning this terminology is the most exalted doctrinal language available at the level of conscious, conceptual understanding — suggesting it points beyond ordinary conceptuality even while expressed within it.  The phrase rendered "the forbearance of non-arising" refers to anutpattikadharmakṣānti, the patience or tolerance that accepts the unborn nature of phenomena — a key attainment on the bodhisattva path, here confirmed by explicit doctrinal context.

The simile of the consecrated king is a standard Indian political analogy in which ministers carry out all practical business while the king's authority remains the source; the Perfection of Wisdom functions analogously as the ground and source of all three vehicles' qualities while those qualities perform the actual activity.  The phrase rendered "practise on the level of following through faith" translates a technical term indicating the śraddhānusārin stage, the lowest of the paths of engagement, in which a practitioner follows the Dharma primarily by means of faith rather than direct insight. This is a standard Abhidharma and Prajñāpāramitā category; the rendering tries to make it accessible without losing the technical reference.  The phrase rendered "rejoices in what is equal to seeing it" translates a construction meaning to take delight in that which is on a par with, or equivalent to, actual direct vision of the Perfection of Wisdom — a technical nuance suggesting that hearing and engaging with the text is counted as equivalent to beholding it directly. This is a subtlety the tradition emphasizes.

The passage ends mid-sentence in the source text; the translation faithfully breaks at the same point without completing the sentence.  The phrase rendered "fray apart like cotton passing through" translates a simile comparing the mind's dissolution to cotton being pulled through something — perhaps a carding or spinning process. The image conveys the mind becoming scattered and insubstantial; the precise mechanical referent is uncertain but the sense is clear.

The term "sред་པ" in the long list of qualities literally means "craving" or "thirst" but here functions in a positive register as "earnest longing" — a deliberate aspiration for awakening rather than afflictive craving. This contextual positive sense is well attested in Mahāyāna enumerations of qualities conducive to the path.  The section marker "The Perfection of Wisdom in Eight Thousand Lines. The Fourteenth Fascicle" renders a Tibetan colophon-style chapter division that appears mid-text; it has been rendered as a plain sentence in the flow since the brief for TTS forbids formatting markers, but it is structurally a chapter heading.

The list of qualities in the third paragraph adds མགུ་བ (contentment / satisfaction) alongside རབ་ཏུ་དགའ་བ (exultation) where the second occurrence of the list differs slightly from the first; both have been rendered faithfully according to their respective Tibetan forms.  The long serial list of qualities repeated across several passages presents several translation challenges. The term rendered "possesses craving's antidote" on first occurrence and "fervent longing" later is སྲེད་པ, literally "craving" or "thirst" (Skt. tṛṣṇā). In this context it is consistently positive and is likely used in the sense of intense aspiration or yearning for awakening rather than afflictive craving. Some commentaries distinguish a wholesome form of tṛṣṇā directed toward liberation. The rendering attempts to reflect this positive valence while acknowledging the unusual usage. The passage breaks off mid-sentence in the source, and the translation preserves this as supplied.  The phrase rendered "consumed by the intermediate fall and brought to ruin" translates bar ma dor chud zos shing yi gsod pa, a vivid idiomatic expression meaning to perish or be destroyed midway, losing one's goal before reaching it. The term bar ma dor (the "intermediate drop" or "midway fall") recurs as a structural motif through this analogy section. The phrase yi gsod pa carries the sense of spiritual exhaustion or destruction of one's purpose, not literal death.  The term rendered "far shore" (ཕ་རོལ, Skt. pāra) evokes the "far shore" implied in pāramitā ("gone to the far shore"); the passage plays on this to indicate the ultimate, non-conceptual nature shared equally by form and all phenomena. This is distinct from "perfection" (ཕ་རོལ་ཏུ་ཕྱིན་པ) and has been rendered literally as "far shore" to preserve the wordplay and doctrinal point about non-conceptuality.  The passage has an internal shift in speaker attribution: after Subhūti's first statement about "no basis, no occasion," the text reads as though Subhūti himself is answering the Buddha's question, though the honorific structure suggests it could also be read as the Buddha's continued explanation. I have followed the most natural reading of the Tibetan as given, with Subhūti responding to the Buddha's query.  The passage is highly repetitive and formulaic, characteristic of Prajñāpāramitā literature emphasizing the identity of suchness (tathatā) across the Tathāgata, Subhūti, and all phenomena. I have preserved the recursive, incantatory style rather than smoothing it into more varied prose, since this repetition is doctrinally purposeful (demonstrating nonduality through insistent restatement). The phrase "born following after the Tathāgata" (de bzhin gshegs pa'i rjes su skyes pa) is a technical expression meaning something like "born in conformity with" or "arisen in accord with" the Tathāgata's realization of suchness — I have rendered it literally to preserve its wordplay with "suchness" (de bzhin nyid, "thus-ness") and "Tathāgata" (de bzhin gshegs pa, "thus-gone one"), which share the root de bzhin ("thus/such") in Tibetan.  The final sentence appears to continue into further instructions not included in this excerpt; the passage ends mid-thought on the bodhisattva's training. The repeated formula "should observe others with a mind of X" (dmigs par bya) reflects the cultivation of the four immeasurables and related attitudes as part of bodhisattva conduct toward sentient beings.  The final sentence is truncated mid-construction in the source (continuing the parallel treatment of the remaining perfections); it has been rendered as it stands, ending where the source text ends.  The passage ends mid-sentence, on Subhūti's reply to Śāriputra's question about karma performed in dreams; the continuation is not included in the source text provided.  The final sentence is left incomplete, as the source text breaks off mid-clause ("byang chub sems dpa' sems dpa' chen po ni..."), continuing presumably into the next section not included here. "The eight qualities" (yan lag brgyad) of water is a standard reference to the eight qualities of pure/excellent water found in classical descriptions of pure lands (e.g., cool, sweet, light, soft, clear, unpolluted, gentle to the stomach, soothing to the throat).  The phrase "kindled and generated roots of virtue" (དགེ་བའི་རྩ་བ་འབར་བསྐྱེད་པར) is somewhat unusual phrasing in the source; the translation follows the most natural sense of "roots of virtue blazing forth/being generated," though the exact idiom is not fully standard.  The term rngan can byed pa (rendered here as "treat them scornfully") is part of a string of near-synonymous verbs for contempt and mockery (brnyas pa, sting ba, 'phya ba, smod pa); its precise nuance is uncertain, but the overall sense of the list—arrogant disdain toward other bodhisattvas—is clear and preserved. Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
